              "title": "The Distance Formula",
              "explanation": "In situations where two objects are moving towards or away from each other at different rates, the total distance between them is given by the product of their individual rates and times. Mathematically, this can be represented as rt = d, where r is rate, t is time, and d is distance.",

              "id": "SEC_14_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Understanding Distance Problems",
              "body": "Distance problems involve determining the total distance between objects moving towards or away from each other at different rates. These scenarios are common in real-world situations, such as travel, transportation, and sports. By using the formula rt = d and setting up a table to organize given information, we can solve these types of problems effectively.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 79"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_14_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "The Distance Formula: rt = d",
              "body": "The distance formula, rt = d, represents the relationship between rate (r), time (t), and distance (d). This formula is applicable when two objects are moving towards or away from each other at different rates. The variables r and t must have the same units, such as miles per hour (mph) for rate and hours for time.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 79"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_14_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Example: Two Joggers Running Towards Each Other",
              "body": "Suppose two joggers start from opposite ends of an 8-mile course running towards each other. One jogger is running at a rate of 4 mph, and the other is running at a rate of 6 mph. Using the distance formula, we can set up the following table to solve for time (t): Rate | Time | Distance Jogger 1 | 4 | 4t Jogger 2 | 6 | 6t Since they meet after t hours, the total distance is given by adding their individual distances: 4t + 6t = 8. By combining like terms and solving for t, we find that t = 4/10 hours, or approximately 24 minutes.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 79"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_14_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistake: Ignoring Units in the Distance Formula",
              "body": "When applying the distance formula rt = d, it's essential to ensure that the units for rate (r) and time (t) are consistent. A common mistake is ignoring these units or using incorrect values, leading to inaccurate solutions.",

              "text": "2.1 \nGraphing - Points and Lines\nObjective: Graph points and lines using xy coordinates. \nOften, to get an idea of the behavior of an equation we will mak e a picture that\nrepresents the solutions to the equations. A graph is simply a picture of the solu-\ntions to an equation. Before we spend much time on making a vis ual representa-\ntion of an equation, we ﬁrst have to understand the basis of gr aphing. Following\nis an example of what is called the coordinate plane. \n-4 -3 -2 -1 1 2 3\n-2\n-1 \n1 \n2\nThe plane is divided into four sections\nby a horizontal number line ( x-axis) \nand a vertical number line ( y-axis). \nWhere the two lines meet in the center\nis called the origin. This center origin\nis where x = 0 and y = 0 . As we move\nto the right the numbers count up\nfrom zero, representing x = 1 , 2 , 3\n/ . \nTo the left the numbers count down from zero, representing x = − 1 , − 2 , − 3 / . \nSimilarly, as we move up the number count up from zero, y = 1 , 2 , 3 / . , and as we\nmove down count down from zero, y = − 1 , − 2 , − 3. We can put dots on the\ngraph which we will call points. Each point has an “address” t hat deﬁnes its loca-\ntion. The ﬁrst number will be the value on the x − axis or horizontal number line. \nThis is the distance the point moves left/right from the orig in. The second\nnumber will represent the value on the y − axis or vertical number line. This is\nthe distance the point moves up/down from the origin. The poi nts are given as an\nordered pair ( x, y ) . \nWorld View Note: Locations on the globe are given in the same manner, each\nnumber is a distance from a central point, the origin which is where the prime\nmeridian and the equator. This “origin is just oﬀ the western coast of Africa. \nThe following example ﬁnds the address or coordinate pair fo r each of several\npoints on the coordinate plane. \nExample 119. \nGive the coordinates of each point. \nA\nB\nC\n89\nTracing from the origin, point A is\nright 1, up 4. This becomes A (1 , 4) . \nPoint B is left 5, up 3. Left is back-\nwards or negative so we have B ( − 5 , \n3) . C is straight down 2 units. There\nis no left or right. This means we go\nright zero so the point is C (0 , − 2) . \nA(1 , 4) , B ( − 5 , 3) , C (0 , − 2) Our Solution\nJust as we can give the coordinates for a set of points, we can t ake a set of points\nand plot them on the plane. \nExample 120. \nGraph the points A(3, 2) , B ( − 2 , 1) , C (3, − 4) , D ( − 2 , − 3) , E ( − 3, 0) , F (0 , 2) , \nG(0 , 0) \nRight 3\nUp 2\nA\nLeft 2\nUp 1 \nB\nThe ﬁrst point, A is at (3, 2) this\nmeans x = 3 (right 3) and y = 2 (up 2). \nFollowing these instructions, starting\nfrom the origin, we get our point. \nThe second point, B ( − 2 , 1) , is left 2\n(negative moves backwards), up 1. \nThis is also illustrated on the graph. \nA\nB\nD\nC\nRight 3Left 2\nDown 3 Down 4\nThe third point, C (3, − 4) is right 3, \ndown 4 (negative moves backwards). \nThe fourth point, D ( − 2 , − 3) is left\n2, down 3 (both negative, both move\nbackwards) \nThe last three points have zeros in them. We still treat these points just like the\nother points. If there is a zero there is just no movement. \nUp 2\nLeft 3\nD\nA\nC\nB F\nGE\nNext is E ( − 3, 0) . This is left 3 (nega-\ntive is backwards), and up zero, right\non the x − axis. \nThen is F (0 , 2) . This is right zero, \nand up two, right on the y − axis. \nFinally is G (0 , 0) . This point has no\nmovement. Thus the point is right on\nthe origin. \n90"
            }

          "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_15_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Introduction to Points and Lines",
              "body": "In mathematics, we use a two-dimensional grid called the coordinate plane to represent points and lines. The coordinate plane is divided into four sections by the x-axis (horizontal number line) and y-axis (vertical number line). Each point on the plane has an address called its coordinates, given as an ordered pair (x, y), where the first number represents the distance from the origin on the x-axis and the second number represents the distance from the origin on the y-axis. Understanding points and lines is crucial in mathematics because it allows us to visualize and solve equations graphically.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 89"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_15_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Definition of Ordered Pairs",
              "body": "An ordered pair (x, y) represents a point on the coordinate plane, where x is the horizontal position (distance from the origin on the x-axis) and y is the vertical position (distance from the origin on the y-axis). The first number in an ordered pair is called the x-coordinate, and the second number is called the y-coordinate. For example, the ordered pair (3, 2) means that the point is located 3 units to the right of the origin on the x-axis and 2 units above the origin on the y-axis.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 89"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_15_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Graphing a Point",
              "body": "Example: Graph the point (4, -2) on the coordinate plane. Solution: Start from the origin and move to the right by 4 units (since x = 4), then down by 2 units (since y = -2). The point is located 4 units to the right of the origin on the x-axis and 2 units below the origin on the y-axis.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 89"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_15_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistakes When Graphing Points",
              "body": "Students often make mistakes when graphing points by mixing up the x-coordinate and y-coordinate. For example, in the ordered pair (4, -2), they might move 2 units down instead of moving 4 units to the right. To avoid this mistake, always remember that the first number represents the horizontal position (distance from the origin on the x-axis) and the second number represents the vertical position (distance from the origin on the y-axis).",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 89"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_15_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Self-Explanation",
              "body": "Now that we understand how to represent points and lines using ordered pairs, take a moment to think about how you can apply this concept to solve problems in the future.               "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 89"
              ]
            }

        {
          "id": "SEC_16",
          "chapter_id": "CH_3",
          "number": "2.2",
          "title": "Slope",
          "order": 15,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 95,
              "page_end": 95,
              "text": "2.2\nGraphing - Slope\nObjective: Find the slope of a line given a graph or two points . \nAs we graph lines, we will want to be able to identify diﬀerent properties of the\nlines we graph. One of the most important properties of a line is its slope. Slope\nis a measure of steepness. A line with a large slope, such as 25 , is very steep. A\nline with a small slope, such as 1 \n10 is very ﬂat. We will also use slope to describe\nthe direction of the line. A line that goes up from left to righ t will have a positive\nslope and a line that goes down from left to right will have a ne gative slope. \nAs we measure steepness we are interested in how fast the line rises compared to\nhow far the line runs. For this reason we will describe slope a s the fraction rise\nrun\n. \nRise would be a vertical change, or a change in the y-values. Run would be a hor-\nizontal change, or a change in the x-values. So another way to describe slope\nwould be the fraction change iny\nchange inx . It turns out that if we have a graph we can draw\nvertical and horiztonal lines from one point to another to ma ke what is called a\nslope triangle. The sides of the slope triangle give us our sl ope. The following\nexamples show graphs that we ﬁnd the slope of using this idea.\nExample 123. \nRun 6\nRise − 4\nTo ﬁnd the slope of this line we will\nconsider the rise, or verticle change\nand the run or horizontal change. \nDrawing these lines in makes a slope\ntriangle that we can use to count from\none point to the next the graph goes\ndown 4, right 6. This is rise − 4, run\n6. As a fraction it would be, − 4\n6\n. \nReduce the fraction to get − 2 \n3\n. \n− 2 \n3\nOur Solution\nWorld View Note: When French mathematicians Rene Descartes and Pierre de\nFermat ﬁrst developed the coordinate plane and the idea of gr aphing lines (and\nother functions) the y-axis was not a verticle line! \nExample 124. \n95\nRise 6\nRun 3 To ﬁnd the slope of this line, the rise\nis up 6, the run is right 3. Our slope is\nthen written as a fraction, rise\nrun\nor 6\n3\n. \nThis fraction reduces to 2. This will\nbe our slope. \n2 Our Solution\nThere are two special lines that have unique slopes that we ne ed to be aware of. \nThey are illustrated in the following example. \nExample 125. \nIn this graph there is no rise, but the\nrun is 3 units. This slope becomes\n0\n3\n= 0 . This line , \nand all horizontal lines havea zero slope. \nThis line has a rise of 5, but no run. \nThe slope becomes 5\n0\n= \nundeﬁned. This line , \nand all vertical lines, have no slope. \nAs you can see there is a big diﬀerence between having a zero sl ope and having no\nslope or undeﬁned slope. Remember, slope is a measure of stee pness. The ﬁrst\nslope is not steep at all, in fact it is ﬂat. Therefore it has a z ero slope. The\nsecond slope can’t get any steeper. It is so steep that there i s no number large\nenough to express how steep it is. This is an undeﬁned slope. \nWe can ﬁnd the slope of a line through two points without seein g the points on a\ngraph. We can do this using a slope formula. If the rise is the c hange in y values, \nwe can calculate this by subtracting the y values of a point. Similarly, if run is a\nchange in the x values, we can calculate this by subtracting the x values of a\npoint. In this way we get the following equation for slope. \nThe slope of a line through ( x 1 , y 1 ) and ( x 2 , y 2 ) is y2 − y1 \nx 2 − x 1 \n96"
            }

          "learning_objectives": [

          "concepts": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_16_C1",
              "title": "What is Slope?",
              "explanation": "Slope is a measure of steepness. It represents how fast a line rises compared to how far it runs. The steeper the line, the larger its slope will be. Slope can be positive (a line that goes up from left to right), negative (a line that goes down from left to right), or zero (a horizontal line).",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 95"
              ],

              "mastery": {

              "id": "SEC_16_C2",
              "title": "Slope Formula",
              "explanation": "The slope of a line through two points (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) can be found using the formula: slope = (y2 - y1) / (x2 - x1). This formula calculates the change in y-values divided by the change in x-values.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 95"

          "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_16_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "What is Slope?",
              "body": "Imagine you're walking up a hill. The steepness of the hill depends on how fast it rises compared to how far it runs. That's exactly what slope measures! It tells us whether a line is flat, gently sloping, or extremely steep. In this lesson, we'll explore how to find the slope of a line given two points or a graph.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 95"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_16_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Defining Slope",
              "body": "Slope is defined as the ratio of the vertical change (rise) to the horizontal change (run). It's usually represented as a fraction: rise / run. When we have a graph, we can draw vertical and horizontal lines from one point to another to make what's called a slope triangle. The sides of this triangle give us our slope.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 95"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_16_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Finding Slope with Two Points",
              "body": "Let's find the slope of the line that passes through points (2, 3) and (4, 5). Using the formula, we get: slope = (5 - 3) / (4 - 2) = 2 / 2 = 1. So the slope of this line is 1.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 95"
              ]
            },

              "id": "SEC_16_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Mistaking Zero Slope for No Slope",
              "body": "Be careful not to confuse a zero slope with no slope! A horizontal line has a zero slope because it's flat, but a vertical line has no slope because it's infinitely steep. Remember, slope is a measure of steepness, so a zero slope means the line is level.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 95"
              ]
            },

              "id": "SEC_16_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Putting it Together",
              "body": "Now that we've learned about slope, try to think about how you can apply this concept to real-life situations. For example, if you're planning a hike and want to know how steep the trail will be, you can use the slope formula to estimate the difficulty of the climb.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_17",
          "chapter_id": "CH_3",
          "number": "2.3",
          "title": "Slope-Intercept Form",
          "order": 16,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 102,
              "page_end": 102,
              "text": "2.3\nGraphing - Slope-Intercept Form\nObjective: Give the equation of a line with a known slope and y -inter-\ncept. \nWhen graphing a line we found one method we could use is to make a table of\nvalues. However, if we can identify some properties of the li ne, we may be able to\nmake a graph much quicker and easier. One such method is ﬁndin g the slope and\nthe y-intercept of the equation. The slope can be represente d by m and the y-\nintercept, where it crosses the axis and x = 0 , can be represented by (0 , b ) where b\nis the value where the graph crosses the vertical y-axis. Any other point on the\nline can be represented by ( x, y ) . Using this information we will look at the slope\nformula and solve the formula for y. \nExample 132. \nm, (0 , b ) , ( x, y ) Using the slope formula gives: \ny − b\nx − 0 \n= m Simplify\ny − b\nx = m Multiply both sides by x\ny − b = mx Add b to both sides\n+ b + b\ny = mx + b Our Solution\nThis equation, y = m x + b can be thought of as the equation of any line that as a\nslope of m and a y-intercept of b. This formula is known as the slope-intercept\nequation. \nSlope − Intercept Equation: y = m x + b\nIf we know the slope and the y-intercept we can easily ﬁnd the e quation that rep-\nresents the line. \nExample 133. \nSlope = 3\n4\n, y − intercept = − 3 Use the slope − intercept equation\ny = mx + b m is the slope, b is the y − intercept\ny = 3\n4\nx − 3 Our Solution\nWe can also ﬁnd the equation by looking at a graph and ﬁnding th e slope and y-\nintercept. \nExample 134. \n102\nIdentify the point where the graph\ncrosses the y-axis (0,3). This means\nthe y-intercept is 3. \nIdenﬁty one other point and draw a\nslope triangle to ﬁnd the slope. The\nslope is − 2 \n3\ny = mx + b Slope-intercept equation\ny = − 2 \n3\nx + 3 Our Solution\nWe can also move the opposite direction, using the equation i dentify the slope\nand y-intercept and graph the equation from this informatio n. However, it will be\nimportant for the equation to ﬁrst be in slope intercept form . If it is not, we will\nhave to solve it for y so we can identify the slope and the y-intercept. \nExample 135. \nWrite in slope− intercept form: 2 x − 4y = 6 Solve for y\n− 2 x − 2 x Subtract 2 x from both sides\n− 4y = − 2 x + 6 Put x term ﬁrst\n− 4 − 4 − 4 Divide each term by− 4\ny = 1 \n2 \nx − 3\n2 \nOur Solution\nOnce we have an equation in slope-intercept form we can graph it by ﬁrst plotting\nthe y-intercept, then using the slope, ﬁnd a second point and connecting the dots. \nExample 136. \nGraph y = 1 \n2 \nx − 4 Recall the slope − intercept formula\ny = mx + b Idenﬁty the slope, m, and the y − intercept, b\nm = 1 \n2 \n, b = − 4 Make the graph\nStarting with a point at the y-inter-\ncept of − 4, \nThen use the slope rise\nrun\n, so we will rise\n1 unit and run 2 units to ﬁnd the next\npoint. \nOnce we have both points, connect the\ndots to get our graph. \nWorld View Note: Before our current system of graphing, French Mathemati-\ncian Nicole Oresme, in 1323 sugggested graphing lines that w ould look more like a\n103"
            }

              "id": "SEC_17_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "What's Slope-Intercept Form?",
              "body": "When we have a straight line, its equation can be expressed in slope-intercept form: y = mx + b. The 'm' stands for the slope, which tells us how steep the line is. The 'b' represents where the line crosses the y-axis (its y-intercept). This form is super useful because it makes it easy to graph lines and solve problems about them.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 102"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_17_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Slope-Intercept Form Formula",
              "body": "The slope-intercept form of a linear equation is given by y = mx + b, where m is the slope (coefficient of x) and b is the y-intercept. This formula applies to all straight lines.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 102"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_17_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Example: Solve for Slope-Intercept Form",
              "body": "Let's say we have an equation in standard form: 2x - 4y = 6. To convert it to slope-intercept form, we need to solve for y. We can do this by isolating y on one side of the equation. First, subtract 2x from both sides to get -4y = -2x + 6. Then divide each term by -4: y = (1/2)x - 3/2. Now we have our slope-intercept form!",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 102"
              ]
            },

              "title": "Common Mistake: Misidentifying Slope and Y-Intercept",
              "body": "Be careful not to mix up the slope and y-intercept when working with slope-intercept form! It's easy to get confused, but remember that m represents the slope (coefficient of x) and b represents the y-intercept. If you swap these values, your answers will be wrong.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 102"
              ]
            },

        {
          "id": "SEC_18",
          "chapter_id": "CH_3",
          "number": "2.4",
          "title": "Point-Slope Form",
          "order": 17,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 107,
              "page_end": 107,
              "text": "2.4\nGraphing - Point-Slope Form\nObjective: Give the equation of a line with a known slope and p oint. \nThe slope-intercept form has the advantage of being simple t o remember and use, \nhowever, it has one major disadvantage: we must know the y-in tercept in order to\nuse it! Generally we do not know the y-intercept, we only know one or more\npoints (that are not the y-intercept). In these cases we can’ t use the slope inter-\ncept equation, so we will use a diﬀerent more ﬂexible formula . If we let the slope\nof an equation be m, and a speciﬁc point on the line be ( x1 , y 1 ) , and any other\npoint on the line be ( x, y ) . We can use the slope formula to make a second equa-\ntion. \nExample 139. \nm, ( x1 , y 1 ) , ( x, y ) Recall slope formula\ny2 − y1 \nx2 − x1 \n= m Plug in values\ny − y1 \nx − x1 \n= m Multiply both sides by( x − x1 ) \ny − y1 = m( x − x1 ) Our Solution\nIf we know the slope, m of an equation and any point on the line ( x1 , y 1 ) we can\neasily plug these values into the equation above which will b e called the point-\nslope formula. \nPoint − Slope Formula: y − y1 = m( x − x 1 ) \nExample 140. \nWrite the equation of the line through the point (3, − 4) with a slope of 3\n5\n. \ny − y1 = m( x − x1 ) Plug values into point− slope formula\ny − ( − 4) = 3\n5 \n( x − 3) Simplify signs\ny + 4 = 3\n5 \n( x − 3) Our Solution\nOften, we will prefer ﬁnal answers be written in slope interc ept form. If the direc-\n107\ntions ask for the answer in slope-intercept form we will simp ly distribute the\nslope, then solve for y. \nExample 141. \nWrite the equation of the line through the point ( − 6, 2) with a slope of − 2 \n3\nin\nslope-intercept form. \ny − y1 = m( x − x1 ) Plug values into point− slope formula\ny − 2 = − 2 \n3\n( x − ( − 6)) Simplify signs\ny − 2 = − 2 \n3\n( x + 6) Distribute slope\ny − 2 = − 2 \n3\nx − 4 Solve for y\n+ 2 + 2 \ny = − 2 \n3\nx − 2 Our Solution\nAn important thing to observe about the point slope formula i s that the operation\nbetween the x’s and y’s is subtraction. This means when you simplify the signs\nyou will have the opposite of the numbers in the point. We need to be very\ncareful with signs as we use the point-slope formula. \nIn order to ﬁnd the equation of a line we will always need to kno w the slope. If\nwe don’t know the slope to begin with we will have to do some wor k to ﬁnd it\nﬁrst before we can get an equation. \nExample 142. \nFind the equation of the line through the points ( − 2 , 5) and (4, − 3) . \nm = y2 − y1 \nx2 − x1 \nFirst we must ﬁnd the slope\nm = − 3 − 5 \n4 − ( − 2) = − 8\n6 = − 4\n3\nPlug values in slope formula and evaluate\ny − y1 = m( x − x1 ) With slope and either point, use point − slope formula\ny − 5 = − 4\n3\n( x − ( − 2)) Simplify signs\ny − 5 = − 4\n3\n( x + 2) Our Solution\nExample 143. \n108"
            }
          ],

          "id": "SEC_19",
          "chapter_id": "CH_3",
          "number": "2.5",
          "title": "Parallel & Perpendicular Lines",
          "order": 18,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 112,
              "page_end": 112,
              "text": "2.5\nGraphing - Parallel and Perpendicular Lines\nObjective: Identify the equation of a line given a parallel o r perpendic-\nular line. \nThere is an interesting connection between the slope of line s that are parallel and\nthe slope of lines that are perpendicular (meet at a right ang le). This is shown in\nthe following example. \nExample 145. \nThe above graph has two parallel\nlines. The slope of the top line is\ndown 2, run 3, or − 2 \n3\n. The slope of\nthe bottom line is down 2, run 3 as\nwell, or − 2 \n3\n. \nThe above graph has two perpendic-\nular lines. The slope of the ﬂatter line\nis up 2, run 3 or\n2 \n3\n. The slope of the steeper line is down 3, \nrun 2 or − 3\n2 \n. \nWorld View Note: Greek Mathematician Euclid lived around 300 BC and pub-\nlished a book titled, The Elements . In it is the famous parallel postulate which\nmathematicians have tried for years to drop from the list of p ostulates. The\nattempts have failed, yet all the work done has developed new types of geome-\ntries! \nAs the above graphs illustrate, parallel lines have the same slope and perpendic-\nular lines have opposite (one positive, one negative) recip rocal (ﬂipped fraction) \nslopes. We can use these properties to make conclusions abou t parallel and per-\npendicular lines. \nExample 146. \nFind the slope of a line parallel to 5 y − 2 x = 7. \n5 y − 2 x = 7 To ﬁnd the slope we will put equation in slope− intercept form\n+ 2 x + 2 x\nAdd 2 x to both sides\n5 y = 2 x + 7 Put x term ﬁrst\n5 5 5 Divide each term by5 \ny = 2 \n5 \nx + 7\n5 \nThe slope is the coeﬃcient of x\n112\nm = 2 \n5 \nSlope of ﬁrst line. Parallel lines have the same slope\nm = 2 \n5 \nOur Solution\nExample 147. \nFind the slope of a line perpendicular to 3x − 4y = 2 \n3x − 4y = 2 To ﬁnd slope we will put equation in slope− intercept form\n− 3x − 3x\nSubtract 3x from both sides\n− 4y = − 3x + 2 Put x term ﬁrst\n− 4 − 4 − 4 Divide each term by− 4\ny = 3\n4\nx − 1 \n2 \nThe slope is the coeﬃcient of x\nm = 3\n4\nSlope of ﬁrst lines. Perpendicular lines have opposite reciprocal slopes\nm = − 4\n3\nOur Solution\nOnce we have a slope, it is possible to ﬁnd the complete equati on of the second\nline if we know one point on the second line. \nExample 148. \nFind the equation of a line through (4, − 5) and parallel to 2 x − 3y = 6. \n2 x − 3y = 6 We ﬁrst need slope of parallel line\n− 2 x − 2 x\nSubtract 2 x from each side\n− 3y = − 2 x + 6 Put x term ﬁrst\n− 3 − 3 − 3 Divide each term by− 3\ny = 2 \n3\nx − 2 Identify the slope, the coeﬃcient of x\nm = 2 \n3\nParallel lines have the same slope\nm = 2 \n3\nWe will use this slope and our point(4, − 5) \ny − y1 = m( x − x1 ) Plug this information into point slope formula\ny − ( − 5) = 2 \n3\n( x − 4) Simplify signs\ny + 5 = 2 \n3\n( x − 4) Our Solution\n113"
            }

              "steps": [
                "Step 1: Put the equation in slope-intercept form (y = mx + b).",
                "Step 2: Simplify the equation to get y = (3/4)x - 1/2.",
                "Step 3: Identify the slope, which is the coefficient of x. In this case, the slope is 3/4.",
                "Step 4: Remember that perpendicular lines have opposite reciprocal slopes. So, if one line has a slope of m, then the other line has a slope of -1/m."
              ],
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 112"
              ]
            }

      "id": "CH_4",

      "title": "Inequalities",
      "order": 3,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_20",
          "chapter_id": "CH_4",
          "number": "3.1",
          "title": "Solve and Graph Inequalities",
          "order": 19,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 118,
              "page_end": 118,
              "text": "3.1 \nInequalities - Solve and Graph Inequalities\nObjective: Solve, graph, and give interval notation for the solution to\nlinear inequalities. \nWhen we have an equation such as x = 4 we have a speciﬁc value for our variable. \nWith inequalities we will give a range of values for our varia ble. To do this we\nwill not use equals, but one of the following symbols: \n> Greater than\n⩾ Greater than or equal to\n< Less than\n⩽ Less than or equal to\nWorld View Note: English mathematician Thomas Harriot ﬁrst used the above\nsymbols in 1631. However, they were not immediately accepte d as symbols such\nas ⊏ and ⊐ were already coined by another English mathematician, Will iam\nOughtred. \nIf we have an expression such as x < 4, this means our variable can be any number\nsmaller than 4 such as − 2 , 0 , 3, 3.9 or even 3.999999999 as long as it is smaller\n118\nthan 4. If we have an expression such as x ⩾ − 2 , this means our variable can be\nany number greater than or equal to − 2 , such as 5 , 0 , − 1 , − 1.9999, or even − 2 . \nBecause we don’t have one set value for our variable, it is oft en useful to draw a\npicture of the solutions to the inequality on a number line. W e will start from the\nvalue in the problem and bold the lower part of the number line if the variable is\nsmaller than the number, and bold the upper part of the number line if the vari-\nable is larger. The value itself we will mark with brackets, e ither ) or ( for less\nthan or greater than respectively, and ] or [ for less than or e qual to or greater\nthan or equal to respectively. \nOnce the graph is drawn we can quickly convert the graph into w hat is called\ninterval notation. Interval notation gives two numbers, th e ﬁrst is the smallest\nvalue, the second is the largest value. If there is no largest value, we can use ∞ \n(inﬁnity). If there is no smallest value, we can use − ∞ negative inﬁnity. If we\nuse either positive or negative inﬁnity we will always use a c urved bracket for that\nvalue. \nExample 151. \nGraph the inequality and give the interval notation\nx < 2 Start at 2 and shade below\nUse ) for less than\nOur Graph\n( − ∞ , 2) Interval Notation\nExample 152. \nGraph the inequality and give the interval notation\ny ⩾ − 1 Start at − 1 and shade above\nUse [ for greater than or equal\nOur Graph\n[ − 1 , ∞ ) Interval Notation\nWe can also take a graph and ﬁnd the inequality for it. \n119"
            }
          ],

              "title": "Linear Inequalities",
              "explanation": "A linear inequality is an expression that compares two quantities, often x and some constant, using one of the four symbols: >, ≥, <, or ≤. For example, 2x + 3 > 5 is a linear inequality, as it compares 2x + 3 to the constant 5.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 118"

              "title": "Interval Notation",
              "explanation": "Interval notation is a way of writing down the solution set of an inequality using two numbers: the smallest and largest values in the solution set.           "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_20_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "What Are Linear Inequalities?",
              "body": "Linear inequalities are essential in mathematics and everyday life. They help us express a range of values for our variables, which is often more realistic than having a single value. Think about speed limits on highways: you're allowed to drive at speeds from 60 km/h to 100 km/h, but not exactly 101 km/h. Inequalities like these are crucial in physics, engineering, and economics.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 118"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_20_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Linear Inequality Definition",
              "body": "A linear inequality is an expression of the form ax + b > c or ax + b < c, where a ≠ 0, b, and c are constants. We use four symbols: > (greater than), ≥ (greater than or equal to), < (less than), and ≤ (less than or equal to). For example, x - 2 > 3 is a linear inequality.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 118"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_20_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Solve the Inequality x + 2 ≥ 5",
              "body": "To solve the inequality x + 2 ≥ 5, we subtract 2 from both sides. This gives us x ≥ 3. We can also graph this on a number line by starting at 3 and shading everything above it.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 118"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_20_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistake: Forgetting to Use Brackets",
              "body": "When graphing an inequality, remember to use brackets to mark the value itself. In the example x < 2, we would shade below 2 and mark [2) as our interval notation. Don't forget the brackets! This can be a common mistake when converting graphs into interval notation.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 118"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_20_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "What Did I Learn?",
              "body": "I learned how to solve linear inequalities using correct notation and symbols, graph them on a number line with proper shading and brackets, and convert the graphs into interval notation correctly. I also understood that intervals can have no largest or smallest value, in which case we use ∞ for infinity.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 118"
              ]
            }
          ],

                "Step 2: Graph this on a number line by starting at 3 and shading everything above it."

        {
          "id": "SEC_21",
          "chapter_id": "CH_4",

          "title": "Compound Inequalitites",
          "order": 20,
          "source_spans": [

              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 124,
              "page_end": 124,
              "text": "3.2\nInequalities - Compound Inequalities\nObjective: Solve, graph and give interval notation to the so lution of\ncompound inequalities. \nSeveral inequalities can be combined together to form what a re called compound\ninequalities. There are three types of compound inequaliti es which we will investi-\ngate in this lesson. \nThe ﬁrst type of a compound inequality is an OR inequality. Fo r this type of\ninequality we want a true statment from either one inequalit y OR the other\ninequality OR both. When we are graphing these type of inequa lities we will\ngraph each individual inequality above the number line, the n move them both\ndown together onto the actual number line for our graph that c ombines them\ntogether. \nWhen we give interval notation for our solution, if there are two diﬀerent parts to\nthe graph we will put a ∪ (union) symbol between two sets of interval notation, \none for each part. \nExample 157. \nSolve each inequality, graph the solution, and give interva l notation of solution\n2 x − 5 > 3 or 4 − x ⩾ 6 Solve each inequality\n+ 5 + 5 \n− 4 − 4 Add or subtract ﬁrst\n2 x > 8 or − x ⩾ 2 Divide\n2 2 − 1 − 1 Dividing by negative ﬂips sign\nx > 4 or x ⩽ − 2 Graph the inequalities separatly above number line\n( − ∞ , − 2] ∪ (4, ∞ ) Interval Notation\nWorld View Note: The symbol for inﬁnity was ﬁrst used by the Romans, \nalthough at the time the number was used for 1000. The greeks a lso used the\nsymbol for 10,000. \nThere are several diﬀerent results that could result from an OR statement. The\ngraphs could be pointing diﬀerent directions, as in the grap h above, or pointing in\nthe same direction as in the graph below on the left, or pointi ng opposite direc-\ntions, but overlapping as in the graph below on the right. Not ice how interval\nnotation works for each of these cases. \n124\nAs the graphs overlap, we take the\nlargest graph for our solution. \nInterval Notation: ( − ∞ , 1) \nWhen the graphs are combined they\ncover the entire number line. \nInterval Notation: ( − ∞ , ∞ ) or R\nThe second type of compound inequality is an AND inequality. AND inequalities\nrequire both statements to be true. If one is false, they both are false. When we\ngraph these inequalities we can follow a similar process, ﬁr st graph both inequali-\nties above the number line, but this time only where they over lap will be drawn\nonto the number line for our ﬁnal graph. When our solution is g iven in interval\nnotation it will be expressed in a manner very similar to sing le inequalities (there\nis a symbol that can be used for AND, the intersection - ∩ , but we will not use it\nhere). \nExample 158. \nSolve each inequality, graph the solution, and express it in terval notation. \n2 x + 8 ⩾ 5 x − 7 and 5 x − 3 > 3x + 1 Move variables to one side\n− 2 x − 2 x\n− 3x − 3x\n8 ⩾ 3x − 7 and 2 x − 3 > 1 Add 7 or 3 to both sides\n+ 7 + 7 + 3 + 3\n15 ⩾ 3x and 2 x > 4 Divide\n3 3 2 2 \n5 ⩾ x and x > 2 Graph, x is smaller ( or equal ) than 5 , \ngreater than 2 \n(2 , 5] Interval Notation\nAgain, as we graph AND inequalities, only the overlapping pa rts of the individual\ngraphs makes it to the ﬁnal number line. As we graph AND inequa lities there are\nalso three diﬀerent types of results we could get. The ﬁrst is shown in the above\n125"
            }

              "id": "SEC_21_C1",
              "title": "What are Compound Inequalities?",
              "explanation": "Compound inequalities are a combination of two or more inequalities that can be combined using logical operators like 'or' and 'and'. They allow us to solve problems where we need to find the intersection or union of multiple solution sets. This is essential in real-world applications, such as finance, engineering, and science.",
              "source_refs": [

              "title": "Types of Compound Inequalities",
              "explanation": "There are two main types of compound inequalities: OR inequalities and AND inequalities. OR inequalities require at least one statement to be true, while AND inequalities require both statements to be true. Understanding the difference between these two types is crucial in solving complex problems.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 124"

          "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_21_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Introduction to Compound Inequalities",
              "body": "In everyday life, we often need to solve problems that involve multiple conditions or constraints. For instance, when buying a house, we might want to find a property that is both within our budget and has a certain number of bedrooms. This is where compound inequalities come in – they allow us to express complex relationships between variables using logical operators. By combining two or more inequalities, we can find the solution set that satisfies all the conditions.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 124"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_21_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Definition of Compound Inequalities",
              "body": "A compound inequality is a combination of two or more inequalities connected by logical operators like 'or' and 'and'. Formally, it can be written as: A ∪ B (OR inequality) or A ∩ B (AND inequality), where A and B are the individual inequalities. The union (∪) symbol represents the OR operator, while the intersection (∩) symbol represents the AND operator.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 124"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_21_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Solving an OR Inequality",
              "body": "Let's solve the compound inequality: 2x − 5 > 3 or 4 − x ≥ 6. To start, we'll solve each individual inequality separately. For the first inequality, 2x − 5 > 3, we add 5 to both sides to get 2x > 8. Then, we divide by 2 to get x > 4. For the second inequality, 4 − x ≥ 6, we subtract 4 from both sides to get −x ≥ 2. Since we're dealing with an OR inequality, we need to find the union of the solution sets. Therefore, our final answer is (−∞, −2] ∪ (4, ∞).",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 124"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_21_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistakes in Solving Compound Inequalities",
              "body": "One common mistake students make when solving compound inequalities is to forget to consider the logical operators. For instance, in an OR inequality, they might focus only on one of the individual inequalities and ignore the other. Remember that both statements must be true for an AND inequality, while at least one statement must be true for an OR inequality.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 124"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_21_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Self-Explanation",
              "body": "In this lesson, we learned about compound inequalities and how to solve them using logical operators. Remember that there are two main types of compound inequalities: OR and AND. To solve an OR inequality, find the union of the solution sets, while for an AND inequality, find the intersection. With practice, you'll become proficient in solving complex problems involving multiple conditions or constraints.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 124"
              ]
            }

          "id": "SEC_22",
          "chapter_id": "CH_4",

          "title": "Absolute Value Inequalities",
          "order": 21,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 128,
              "page_end": 128,
              "text": "3.3\nInequalities - Absolute Value Inequalities\nObjective: Solve, graph and give interval notation for the s olution to\ninequalities with absolute values. \nWhen an inequality has an absolute value we will have to remov e the absolute\nvalue in order to graph the solution or give interval notatio n. The way we remove\nthe absolute value depends on the direction of the inequalit y symbol. \nConsider | x| < 2 . \nAbsolute value is deﬁned as distance from zero. Another way t o read this\ninequality would be the distance from zero is less than 2. So o n a number line we\nwill shade all points that are less than 2 units away from zero . \nThis graph looks just like the graphs of the three part compou nd inequalities! \nWhen the absolute value is less than a number we will remove the absolute value\nby changing the problem to a three part inequality, with the n egative value on the\nleft and the positive value on the right. So | x| < 2 becomes − 2 < x < 2 , as the\ngraph above illustrates. \nConsider | x| > 2 . \nAbsolute value is deﬁned as distance from zero. Another way t o read this\ninequality would be the distance from zero is greater than 2. So on the number\nline we shade all points that are more than 2 units away from ze ro. \nThis graph looks just like the graphs of the OR compound inequ alities! When the\nabsolute value is greater than a number we will remove the absolute value by\nchanging the problem to an OR inequality, the ﬁrst inequalit y looking just like\nthe problem with no absolute value, the second ﬂipping the in equality symbol and\nchanging the value to a negative. So | x| > 2 becomes x > 2 or x < − 2 , as the graph\nabove illustrates. \nWorld View Note: The phrase “absolute value” comes from German mathemati-\ncian Karl Weierstrass in 1876, though he used the absolute va lue symbol for com-\nplex numbers. The ﬁrst known use of the symbol for integers co mes from a 1939\n128\nedition of a college algebra text! \nFor all absolute value inequalities we can also express our a nswers in interval\nnotation which is done the same way it is done for standard com pound inequali-\nties. \nWe can solve absolute value inequalities much like we solved absolute value equa-\ntions. Our ﬁrst step will be to isolate the absolute value. Ne xt we will remove the\nabsolute value by making a three part inequality if the absol ute value is less than\na number, or making an OR inequality if the absolute value is g reater than a\nnumber. Then we will solve these inequalites. Remember, if w e multiply or divide\nby a negative the inequality symbol will switch directions!\nExample 160. \nSolve, graph, and give interval notation for the solution\n| 4x − 5 | ⩾ 6 Absolute value is greater, use OR\n4x − 5 ⩾ 6 OR 4x − 5 ⩽ − 6 Solve\n+ 5 + 5 \n+ 5 + 5 Add 5 to both sides\n4x ⩾ 11 OR 4x ⩽ − 1 Divide both sides by4\n4 4 4 4\nx ⩾ 11 \n4 OR x ⩽ − 1 \n4\nGraph\n(\n− ∞ , − 1 \n4\n]\n∪ \n[ 11 \n4 , ∞ \n)\nInterval notation\nExample 161. \nSolve, graph, and give interval notation for the solution\n− 4 − 3| x| ⩽ − 16 Add 4 to both sides\n+ 4 + 4\n− 3| x| ⩽ − 12 Divide both sides by − 3\n− 3 − 3 Dividing by a negative switches the symbol\n| x| ⩾ 4 Absolute value is greater, use OR\n129"
            }

              "id": "SEC_22_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Understanding Absolute Value Inequalities",
              "body": "Absolute value inequalities are essential in mathematics because they help us solve problems involving distances or differences between values. Imagine you're on a number line, and you want to find all the points that are within 2 units of zero. This is exactly what an absolute value inequality represents! When solving these types of inequalities, we need to consider two cases: when the absolute value is less than or equal to a number, and when it's greater than or equal to a number.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 128"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_22_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Absolute Value Inequality Definition",
              "body": "An absolute value inequality can be written as |x| < k or |x| > k, where k is a positive real number. To solve these inequalities, we need to remove the absolute value function and break it down into simpler inequalities.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 128"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_22_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Solving an Absolute Value Inequality",
              "body": "Let's consider the inequality |4x - 5| ≥ 6. We can start by removing the absolute value, which gives us two separate inequalities: 4x - 5 ≥ 6 and 4x - 5 ≤ -6. Solving these inequalities separately, we get x ≥ (11/4) or x ≤ (-1/4).",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 128"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_22_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Pitfall: Switching Inequality Direction",
              "body": "A common mistake when solving absolute value inequalities is to switch the direction of the inequality sign. For example, consider the inequality |x| < 2 becoming -2 < x < 2. However, if we multiply both sides by a negative number, the direction of the inequality will change. Remember to always check for any negative numbers and adjust the inequality signs accordingly.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 128"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_22_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Checking Understanding of Absolute Value Inequalities",
              "body": "Now that you've learned about absolute value inequalities, try explaining it in your own words. What does the absolute value function represent?               ]
            }

              "prompt": "|4x - 5| ≥ 6",
              "steps": [

                "Dividing by 4, we get x ≤ (-1)/4."
              ],

          "id": "SEC_23",

          "title": "Graphing",
          "order": 22,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 134,
              "page_end": 134,
              "text": "4.1 \nSystems of Equations - Graphing\nObjective: Solve systems of equations by graphing and ident ifying the\npoint of intersection. \nWe have solved problems like 3x − 4 = 11 by adding 4 to both sides and then\ndividing by 3 (solution is x = 5) . We also have methods to solve equations with\nmore than one variable in them. It turns out that to solve for m ore than one vari-\nable we will need the same number of equations as variables. F or example, to\nsolve for two variables such as x and y we will need two equations. When we have\nseveral equations we are using to solve, we call the equation s a system of equa-\ntions. When solving a system of equations we are looking for a solut ion that\nworks in both equations. This solution is usually given as an ordered pair ( x, y ) . \nThe following example illustrates a solution working in bot h equations\nExample 165. \nShow (2,1) is the solution to the system 3x − y = 5 \nx + y = 3\n(2 , 1) Identify x and y from the orderd pair\nx = 2 , y = 1 Plug these values into each equation\n3(2) − (1) = 5 First equation\n6 − 1 = 5 Evaluate\n5 = 5 True\n(2) + (1) = 3 Second equation, evaluate\n3 = 3 True\nAs we found a true statement for both equations we know (2,1) i s the solution to\nthe system. It is in fact the only combination of numbers that works in both\nequations. In this lesson we will be working to ﬁnd this point given the equations. \nIt seems to follow that if we use points to describe the soluti on, we can use graphs\nto ﬁnd the solutions. \nIf the graph of a line is a picture of all the solutions, we can g raph two lines on\nthe same coordinate plane to see the solutions of both equati ons. We are inter-\n134\nested in the point that is a solution for both lines, this woul d be where the lines\nintersect! If we can ﬁnd the intersection of the lines we have found the solution\nthat works in both equations. \nExample 166. \ny = − 1 \n2 \nx + 3\ny = 3\n4\nx − 2 \nTo graph we identify slopes andy − intercepts\nFirst: m = − 1 \n2 \n, b = 3\nSecond: m = 3\n4\n, b = − 2 \nNow we can graph both lines on the same plane. \n(4,1) \nTo graph each equation, we start at\nthe y-intercept and use the slope rise\nrun\nto get the next point and connect the\ndots. \nRemember a negative slope is down-\nhill! \nFind the intersection point, (4,1) \n(4,1) Our Solution\nOften our equations won’t be in slope-intercept form and we w ill have to solve\nboth equations for y ﬁrst so we can idenﬁty the slope and y-intercept. \nExample 167. \n6x − 3y = − 9\n2 x + 2 y = − 6 Solve each equation fory\n6x − 3y = − 9 2 x + 2 y = − 6\n− 6x − 6x\n− 2 x − 2 x Subtract x terms\n− 3y = − 6x − 9 2 y = − 2 x − 6 Put x terms ﬁrst\n− 3 − 3 − 3 2 2 2 Divide by coeﬃcient of y\ny = 2 x + 3 y = − x − 3 Identify slope and y − intercepts\n135"
            }
          ],

              "source_refs": [

          "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_23_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Introduction to Graphing Systems of Equations",
              "body": "Graphing is a powerful tool for solving systems of equations. By representing each equation as a line on a coordinate plane, we can visualize the relationships between variables and find the point of intersection, which represents the solution to the system. This method is particularly useful when working with complex systems or when one equation is not easily solvable.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 134"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_23_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Definition of Graphing Systems of Equations",
              "body": "To graph a system of equations, we represent each equation as a line on a coordinate plane using the slope-intercept form (y = mx + b), where m is the slope and b is the y-intercept. The point of intersection between two lines represents the solution to the system, which satisfies both equations simultaneously.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 134"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_23_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Example 1: Graphing a System of Equations",
              "body": "Suppose we have the following system of equations: y = - rac{1}{2}x + 3 y = rac{3}{4}x - 2 To graph this system, we first identify the slopes and y-intercepts for each equation. We can then plot two lines on the same coordinate plane using the points (0, b) for each line. The point of intersection between these two lines represents the solution to the system. For example, if we plot the lines y = - rac{1}{2}x + 3 and y = rac{3}{4}x - 2 on the same coordinate plane, we find that they intersect at the point (4, 1). Therefore, the solution to this system of equations is x = 4 and y = 1.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 134"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_23_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistakes When Graphing Systems of Equations",
              "body": "One common mistake when graphing systems of equations is not accurately identifying the slopes and y-intercepts for each equation. This can lead to incorrect solutions or failure to find the intersection point between two lines. To avoid this, make sure to carefully identify the slope and y-intercept for each equation before plotting the lines on the coordinate plane.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 134"
              ]

        {
          "id": "SEC_24",
          "chapter_id": "CH_5",
          "number": "4.2",
          "title": "Substitution",
          "order": 23,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 139,
              "page_end": 139,
              "text": "4.2\nSystems of Equations - Substitution\nObjective: Solve systems of equations using substitution.\nWhen solving a system by graphing has several limitations. F irst, it requires the\ngraph to be perfectly drawn, if the lines are not straight we m ay arrive at the\nwrong answer. Second, graphing is not a great method to use if the answer is\nreally large, over 100 for example, or if the answer is a decim al the that graph will\nnot help us ﬁnd, 3.2134 for example. For these reasons we will rarely use graphing\nto solve our systems. Instead, an algebraic approach will be used. \nThe ﬁrst algebraic approach is called substitution. We will build the concepts of\nsubstitution through several example, then end with a ﬁve-s tep process to solve\nproblems using this method. \nExample 170. \nx = 5 \ny = 2 x − 3 We already knowx = 5 , substitute this into the other equation\ny = 2 (5) − 3 Evaluate, multiply ﬁrst\ny = 10 − 3 Subtract\ny = 7 We now also havey\n(5 , 7) Our Solution\nWhen we know what one variable equals we can plug that value (o r expression) in\nfor the variable in the other equation. It is very important t hat when we substi-\ntute, the substituted value goes in parenthesis. The reason for this is shown in the\nnext example. \nExample 171. \n2 x − 3y = 7\ny = 3x − 7 We knowy = 3x − 7, substitute this into the other equation\n2 x − 3(3x − 7) = 7 Solve this equation, distributing − 3 ﬁrst\n139\n2 x − 9x + 21 = 7 Combine like terms2 x − 9x\n− 7x + 21 = 7 Subtract 21 \n− 21 − 21 \n− 7x = − 14 Divide by − 7\n− 7 − 7\nx = 2 We now have ourx, plug into the y = equation to ﬁnd y\ny = 3(2) − 7 Evaluate, multiply ﬁrst\ny = 6 − 7 Subtract\ny = − 1 We now also havey\n(2 , − 1) Our Solution\nBy using the entire expression 3x − 7 to replace y in the other equation we were\nable to reduce the system to a single linear equation which we can easily solve for\nour ﬁrst variable. However, the lone variable (a variable wi thout a coeﬃcient) is\nnot always alone on one side of the equation. If this happens w e can isolate it by\nsolving for the lone variable. \nExample 172. \n3x + 2 y = 1 \nx − 5 y = 6 Lone variable isx, isolate by adding5 y to both sides. \n+ 5 y + 5 y\nx = 6 + 5 y Substitute this into the untouched equation\n3(6 + 5 y) + 2 y = 1 Solve this equation, distributing 3 ﬁrst\n18 + 15y + 2 y = 1 Combine like terms 15y + 2 y\n18 + 17y = 1 Subtract 18 from both sides\n− 18 − 18\n17y = − 17 Divide both sides by 17\n17 17\ny = − 1 We have oury, plug this into thex = equation to ﬁnd x\nx = 6 + 5 ( − 1) Evaluate, multiply ﬁrst\nx = 6 − 5 Subtract\nx = 1 We now also havex\n(1 , − 1) Our Solution\nThe process in the previous example is how we will solve probl ems using substitu-\n140"
            }

              "id": "SEC_24_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Why Substitution Matters",
              "body": "When solving systems of equations, graphing can be time-consuming and inaccurate, especially when dealing with large or decimal values. The substitution method provides an efficient and reliable alternative, allowing us to solve systems by replacing one variable with its equivalent expression from another equation.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 139"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_24_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Substitution Definition",
              "body": "Given two equations: y = 2x - 3 and x = 5, we can use substitution to find the value of y. We substitute the value of x (5) into the equation for y (y = 2(5) - 3), evaluating the expression step-by-step until we obtain a solution.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 139"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_24_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Substitution Example: Equations with Variables",
              "body": "Let's solve the following system of equations using substitution: x + y = 7 2x - 3y = 7 We know that x = 5, so we can substitute this value into the second equation: 2(5) - 3y = 7 10 - 3y = 7 Subtracting 10 from both sides gives us: -3y = -3 Dividing by -3 yields the solution for y: y = 1 Now that we have the value of y, we can plug it back into one of the original equations to find the value of x. Using the first equation, we get: x + 1 = 7 Subtracting 1 from both sides gives us the solution for x: x = 6",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 139"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_24_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Error: Forgetting Parentheses in Substitution",
              "body": "When substituting an expression into another equation, it is essential to enclose the substituted value within parentheses. If we forget to do so, we may end up with incorrect results or even simplify expressions incorrectly. For example, consider the following system of equations: x + y = 7 2x - 3y = 7 If we substitute x = 5 into the second equation without using parentheses, we get: 2(5) - 3y = 7 10 - 3y = 7 Subtracting 10 from both sides gives us: -3y = -3 Dividing by -3 yields an incorrect solution for y: y = -1 To avoid this mistake, always use parentheses when substituting expressions.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 139"
              ]
            },

          "id": "SEC_25",
          "chapter_id": "CH_5",

          "title": "Addition/Elimination",
          "order": 24,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 146,
              "page_end": 146,
              "text": "4.3\nSystems of Equations - Addition/Elimination\nObjective: Solve systems of equations using the addition/e limination\nmethod. \nWhen solving systems we have found that graphing is very limi ted when solving\nequations. We then considered a second method known as subst ituion. This is\nprobably the most used idea in solving systems in various are as of algebra. How-\never, substitution can get ugly if we don’t have a lone variab le. This leads us to\nour second method for solving systems of equations. This met hod is known as\neither Elimination or Addition. We will set up the process in the following exam-\nples, then deﬁne the ﬁve step process we can use to solve by eli mination. \nExample 177. \n3x − 4y = 8\n5 x + 4y = − 24\nNotice opposites in front of y ′s. Add columns. \n8x = − 16 Solve for x, divide by 8\n8 8\nx = − 2 We have ourx! \n5( − 2) + 4y = − 24 Plug into either original equation , simplify\n− 10 + 4y = − 24 Add 10 to both sides\n+ 10 + 10\n4y = − 14 Divide by 4\n4 4\ny = − 7\n2 Now we have oury! \n(\n− 2 , − 7\n2 \n)\nOur Solution\nIn the previous example one variable had opposites in front o f it, − 4y and 4y. \nAdding these together eliminated the y completely. This all owed us to solve for\nthe x. This is the idea behind the addition method. However, gener ally we won’t\nhave opposites in front of one of the variables. In this case w e will manipulate the\nequations to get the opposites we want by multiplying one or b oth equations (on\nboth sides!). This is shown in the next example. \nExample 178. \n− 6x + 5 y = 22\n2 x + 3y = 2 \nWe can get opposites in front ofx, by multiplying the\nsecond equation by3, to get − 6x and + 6x\n3(2 x + 3y) = (2)3 Distribute to get new second equation. \n146\n6x + 9y = 6 New second equation\n− 6x + 5 y = 22 First equation still the same, add\n14y = 28 Divide both sides by 14\n14 14\ny = 2 We have oury! \n2 x + 3(2) = 2 Plug into one of the original equations, simplify\n2 x + 6 = 2 Subtract 6 from both sides\n− 6 − 6\n2 x = − 4 Divide both sides by2 \n2 2 \nx = − 2 We also have ourx! \n( − 2 , 2) Our Solution\nWhen we looked at the x terms, − 6x and 2 x we decided to multiply the 2 x by 3\nto get the opposites we were looking for. What we are looking f or with our oppo-\nsites is the least common multiple (LCM) of the coeﬃcients. W e also could have\nsolved the above problem by looking at the terms with y, 5 y and 3y. The LCM of\n3 and 5 is 15. So we would want to multiply both equations, the 5 y by 3, and the\n3y by − 5 to get opposites, 15 y and − 15y. This illustrates an important point, \nsome problems we will have to multiply both equations by a con stant (on both\nsides) to get the opposites we want. \nExample 179. \n3x + 6y = − 9\n2 x + 9y = − 26\nWe can get opposites in front ofx, ﬁnd LCM of 6 and 9, \nThe LCM is 18. We will multiply to get 18y and − 18y\n3(3x + 6y) = ( − 9)3 Multiply the ﬁrst equation by3, both sides! \n9x + 18y = − 27\n− 2(2 x + 9y) = ( − 26)( − 2) Multiply the second equation by− 2 , both sides! \n− 4x − 18y = 52\n9x + 18y = − 27 Add two new equations together\n− 4x − 18y = 52\n5 x = 25 Divide both sides by 5 \n5 5 \nx = 5 We have our solution forx\n3(5) + 6y = − 9 Plug into either original equation, simplify\n15 + 6y = − 9 Subtract 15 from both sides\n− 15 − 15\n147"
            }

              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Why Addition/Elimination Matters",
              "body": "When solving systems of equations, graphing can be very limiting. Substitution is often the most used method, but it can become cumbersome if we don't have a single variable. The Addition/Elimination method provides an alternative approach that allows us to eliminate variables and solve for one or both unknowns.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 146"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_25_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Addition/Elimination Rule",
              "body": "To apply the Addition/Elimination method, we need to identify two equations in a system and decide which variable to eliminate. We can add or subtract multiples of one equation from another, or multiply both sides of an equation by a constant to introduce opposites in front of one or more variables. The goal is to create a new equation with only one variable, allowing us to solve for that variable.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 146"
              ]
            },

              "id": "SEC_25_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Solving a System using Addition/Elimination",
              "body": "Let's consider the system of equations: 3x - 4y = 8 and 5x + 4y = -24. We can eliminate y by adding these two equations together, as they have opposites in front of y. Adding (3x - 4y) and (5x + 4y), we get 8x = -16. Solving for x, we divide both sides by 8 to get x = -2. Next, we can plug this value into one of the original equations to solve for y.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 146"
              ]
            },

              "id": "SEC_25_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Not Identifying Opposites",
              "body": "A common mistake when using Addition/Elimination is not identifying opposites in front of a variable. For example, if we have the equation 2x + 3y = 5 and want to eliminate x, we should look for a multiple of the coefficient of x (which is 2) that will introduce an opposite sign when multiplied by another equation. If we simply add or subtract multiples of one equation from another without considering opposites, we may not be able to eliminate the variable as intended.",
              "source_refs": [

            },

              "text": "4.4\nSystems of Equations - Three Variables\nObjective: Solve systems of equations with three variables using addi-\ntion/elimination. \nSolving systems of equations with 3 variables is very simila r to how we solve sys-\ntems with two variables. When we had two variables we reduced the system down\nto one with only one variable (by substitution or addition). With three variables\nwe will reduce the system down to one with two variables (usua lly by addition), \nwhich we can then solve by either addition or substitution. \nTo reduce from three variables down to two it is very importan t to keep the work\norganized. We will use addition with two equations to elimin ate one variable. \nThis new equation we will call (A). Then we will use a diﬀerent pair of equations\nand use addition to eliminate the same variable. This second new equation we\nwill call (B). Once we have done this we will have two equation s (A) and (B) \nwith the same two variables that we can solve using either met hod. This is shown\nin the following examples. \nExample 182. \n3x + 2 y − z = − 1 \n− 2 x − 2 y + 3z = 5 We will eliminatey using two diﬀerent pairs of equations\n5 x + 2 y − z = 3\n151\n3x + 2 y − z = − 1 Using the ﬁrst two equations, \n− 2 x − 2 y + 3z = 5 Add the ﬁrst two equations\n( A) x + 2 z = 4 This is equation ( A) , our ﬁrst equation\n− 2 x − 2 y + 3z = 5 Using the second two equations\n5 x + 2 y − z = 3 Add the second two equations\n( B) 3 x + 2 z = 8 This is equation ( B) , our second equation\n( A) x + 2 z = 4 Using ( A) and ( B) we will solve this system. \n( B) 3 x + 2 z = 8 We will solve by addition\n− 1( x + 2 z) = (4)( − 1) Multiply ( A) by − 1 \n− x − 2 z = − 4\n− x − 2 z = − 4 Add to the second equation, unchanged\n3x + 2 z = 8\n2 x = 4 Solve, divide by 2 \n2 2 \nx = 2 We now havex! Plug this into either( A) or ( B) \n(2) + 2 z = 4 We plug it into( A) , solve this equation, subtract 2 \n− 2 − 2 \n2 z = 2 Divide by 2 \n2 2 \nz = 1 We now havez! Plug this and x into any original equation\n3(2) + 2 y − (1) = − 1 We use the ﬁrst, multiply 3(2) = 6 and combine with− 1 \n2 y + 5 = − 1 Solve, subtract 5 \n− 5 − 5 \n2 y = − 6 Divide by 2 \n2 2 \ny = − 3 We now havey! \n(2 , − 3, 1) Our Solution\nAs we are solving for x, y, and z we will have an ordered triplet ( x, y, z ) instead of\n152"
            }

              "title": "Introduction to Three Variables",
              "body": "When we have two variables in a system of equations, we can solve it by reducing the system down to one with only one variable. However, when we have three variables, we need to reduce the system down to one with two variables before solving it. This is where Three Variables come into play. By using addition/elimination method, we can eliminate one variable from two pairs of equations and solve for the remaining two variables.",

              "title": "Definition of Three Variables",
              "body": "A system of linear equations with three variables is represented as a set of three equations in three unknowns: x, y, and z. The solution to this system is an ordered triplet (x, y, z). To solve such a system, we need to reduce it down to one with two variables using addition/elimination method.",
              "source_refs": [

              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Worked Example: Solving a System of Equations with Three Variables",
              "body": "Let's consider the following system of equations: 3x + 2y - z = -1 -2x - 2y + 3z = 5 5x + 2y - z = 3 To solve this system, we need to eliminate one variable from two pairs of equations. We can use addition/elimination method to do so. Step 1: Eliminate y using the first two equations and the second two equations. ( A) x + 2z = 4 (obtained by adding the first two equations) ( B) 3x + 2z = 8 (obtained by adding the second two equations) Step 2: Solve for x using equation (B). x + 2z = 4 -1(x + 2z) = (-1)(4) x + 2z = -4 3x + 2z = 8 Add the two equations to eliminate x. 5z = 4 Step 3: Solve for z by dividing both sides of the equation by 5. z = 4/5 Now that we have z, we can substitute it back into one of the original equations to solve for x and y.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 151"
              ]

          "id": "SEC_27",
          "chapter_id": "CH_5",
          "number": "4.5",
          "title": "Application: Value Problems",
          "order": 26,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 158,
              "page_end": 158,
              "text": "4.5\nSystems of Equations - Value Problems\nObjective: Solve value problems by setting up a system of equ ations. \nOne application of system of equations are known as value pro blems. Value prob-\nlems are ones where each variable has a value attached to it. F or example, if our\nvariable is the number of nickles in a person’s pocket, those nickles would have a\nvalue of ﬁve cents each. We will use a table to help us set up and solve value\nproblems. The basic structure of the table is shown below. \nNumber Value Total\nItem 1 \nItem 2 \nTotal\nThe ﬁrst column in the table is used for the number of things we have. Quite\noften, this will be our variables. The second column is used f or the that value\neach item has. The third column is used for the total value whi ch we calculate by\nmultiplying the number by the value. For example, if we have 7 dimes, each with\na value of 10 cents, the total value is 7 · 10 = 70 cents. The last row of the table is\nfor totals. We only will use the third row (also marked total) for the totals that\n158\nare given to use. This means sometimes this row may have some b lanks in it. \nOnce the table is ﬁlled in we can easily make equations by addi ng each column, \nsetting it equal to the total at the bottom of the column. This is shown in the fol-\nlowing example. \nExample 186. \nIn a child’s bank are 11 coins that have a value of S 1.85. The coins are either\nquarters or dimes. How many coins each does child have? \nNumber Value Total\nQuarter q 25\nDime d 10\nTotal\nUsing value table, use q for quarters, d for dimes\nEach quarter′s value is 25 cents, dime′s is 10 cents\nNumber Value Total\nQuarter q 25 25q\nDime d 10 10d\nTotal\nMultiply number by value to get totals\nNumber Value Total\nQuarter q 25 25q\nDime d 10 10d\nTotal 11 185\nWe have 11 coins total. This is the number total. \nWe have 1.85 for the ﬁnal total, \nWrite ﬁnal total in cents( 185) \nBecause 25 and 10 are cents\nq + d = 11 First and last columns are our equations by adding\n25q + 10d = 185 Solve by either addition or substitution . \n− 10( q + d) = ( 11 )( − 10) Using addition, multiply ﬁrst equation by− 10\n− 10q − 10d = − 110\n− 10q − 10d = − 110 Add together equations\n25q + 10d = 185\n15q = 75 Divide both sides by 15\n15 15\nq = 5 We have ourq, number of quarters is5 \n(5) + d = 11 Plug into one of original equations\n− 5 − 5 Subtract 5 from both sides\nd = 6 We have ourd, number of dimes is6\n159"
            }
          ],
