          "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_27_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "What are Value Problems?",
              "body": "Value problems are a specific type of system of equations that involve variables with attached values. They are used to solve problems where we need to find not just the quantity of items, but their actual value. For example, if we have 7 dimes worth $1.85, we want to know how many dimes and quarters we have. This is a classic application of systems of equations.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 158"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_27_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Definition of Value Problems",
              "body": "A value problem can be defined as a system of equations where each variable has a specific value attached to it. We use a table to help us set up and solve these problems, with the Number column representing the quantity, the Value column representing the value of each item, and the Total column representing the total value.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 158"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_27_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Example: Coins in a Bank",
              "body": "Let's consider an example. In a child's bank are 11 coins worth $1.85. The coins are either quarters or dimes. We want to find out how many of each type there are. First, we set up the table: | Number | Value | Total | | --- | --- | --- | | q | 25 cents | 25q | | d | 10 cents | 10d | | Total | 11 coins | $1.85 | We can then create two equations: q + d = 11 and 25q + 10d = 185. We solve this system using either addition or substitution method. Let's use addition: 25q + 10d = 185 -10(q + d) = -10(11) -10q - 10d = -110 -10q - 10d = -110 25q + 10d = 185 15q = 75 q = 5 We find that q = 5, which means there are 5 quarters. We can then plug this value into one of the original equations to find d: d = 11 - q = 11 - 5 = 6 So we have 5 quarters and 6 dimes.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 158"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_27_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistake: Ignoring Total Value",
              "body": "One common mistake students make when solving value problems is ignoring the total value column in the table. This can lead to incorrect equations and solutions. Remember, the total value is crucial in setting up and solving value problems.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 158"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_27_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Self-Explanation",
              "body": "Think about how you would set up a table for a value problem involving different types of coins, such as pennies, nickels, and dimes. What equations would you create from the table? How would you solve the system?",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 158"
              ]
            }

          "id": "SEC_28",
          "chapter_id": "CH_5",
          "number": "4.6",
          "title": "Application: Mixture Problems",
          "order": 27,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 167,
              "page_end": 167,
              "text": "4.6\nSystems of Equations - Mixture Problems\nObjective: Solve mixture problems by setting up a system of e quations. \nOne application of systems of equations are mixture problem s. Mixture problems\nare ones where two diﬀerent solutions are mixed together res ulting in a new ﬁnal\nsolution. We will use the following table to help us solve mix ture problems: \nAmount Part Total\nItem 1 \nItem 2 \nFinal\nThe ﬁrst column is for the amount of each item we have. The seco nd column is\nlabeled “part”. If we mix percentages we will put the rate (wr itten as a decimal) \nin this column. If we mix prices we will put prices in this colu mn. Then we can\nmultiply the amount by the part to ﬁnd the total. Then we can ge t an equation\nby adding the amount and/or total columns that will help us so lve the problem\nand answer the questions. \nThese problems can have either one or two variables. We will s tart with one vari-\nable problems. \nExample 191. \nA chemist has 70 mL of a 50% methane solution. How much of a 80% s olution\nmust she add so the ﬁnal solution is 60% methane? \nAmount Part Total\nStart 70 0.5\nAdd x 0.8\nFinal\nSet up the mixture table. We start with 70, but\ndon′t know how much we add, that is x. The part\nis the percentages, 0.5 for start, 0.8 for add. \n167\nAmount Part Total\nStart 70 0.5\nAdd x 0.8\nFinal 70 + x 0.6\nAdd amount column to get ﬁnal amount. The\npart for this amount is 0.6 because we want the\nﬁnal solution to be 60% methane. \nAmount Part Total\nStart 70 0.5 35\nAdd x 0.8 0.8x\nFinal 70 + x 0.6 42 + 0.6x\nMultiply amount by part to get total. \nbe sure to distribute on the last row: ( 70 + x) 0.6\n35 + 0.8x = 42 + 0.6x The last column is our equation by adding\n− 0.6x − 0.6x Move variables to one side, subtract 0.6x\n35 + 0.2x = 42 Subtract 35 from both sides\n− 35 − 35\n0.2x = 7 Divide both sides by 0.2\n0.2 0.2\nx = 35 We have our x! \n35 mL must be added Our Solution\nThe same process can be used if the starting and ﬁnal amount ha ve a price\nattached to them, rather than a percentage. \nExample 192. \nA coﬀee mix is to be made that sells for S 2.50 by mixing two types of coﬀee. The\ncafe has 40 mL of coﬀee that costs S 3.00. How much of another coﬀee that costs\nS 1.50 should the cafe mix with the ﬁrst? \nAmount Part Total\nStart 40 3\nAdd x 1.5\nFinal\nSet up mixture table. We know the starting\namount and its cost, S 3. The added amount\nwe do not know but we do know its cost isS 1.50. \nAmount Part Total\nStart 40 3\nAdd x 1.5\nFinal 40 + x 2.5\nAdd the amounts to get the ﬁnal amount. \nWe want this ﬁnal amount to sell forS 2.50. \n168"
            }
          ],

          "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_28_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "What is a Mixture Problem?",
              "body": "A mixture problem is an application of systems of equations where we combine two or more substances to create a new solution. This can involve mixing liquids, solids, or gases with different concentrations or prices. We use the concept of systems of equations to solve these problems by setting up and solving a system of linear equations. Mixture problems are used in many real-world scenarios, such as calculating the amount of medication needed for patients, determining the concentration of chemical solutions, and pricing mixed products.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 167"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_28_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Mixture Problem Definition",
              "body": "A mixture problem is defined by a system of two linear equations: one equation represents the total amount after mixing, and the other equation represents the relationship between the parts. The variables in these equations are the amounts of each substance being mixed, which can be expressed as x (the unknown) or as given values.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 167"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_28_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Example 1: Solving a Mixture Problem with Percentages",
              "body": "Let's solve Example 191 from the textbook. A chemist has 70 mL of a 50% methane solution and wants to add an unknown amount x of an 80% solution so that the final solution is 60% methane. We set up the mixture table as follows: Amount Part Total Start 70 0.5 35 Add x 0.8 0.8x Final 70 + x 0.6 42 + 0.6x Multiplying amount by part gives us two equations, which we can then use to solve for the unknown variable x. We have: (1) 35 + 0.8x = 42 + 0.6x Subtracting 0.6x from both sides gives us: 35 + 0.2x = 42 Subtracting 35 from both sides gives us: 0.2x = 7 Dividing both sides by 0.2 gives us: x = 35 Our solution is x = 35, which means that the chemist needs to add 35 mL of an 80% methane solution.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 167"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_28_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Error in Mixture Problems",
              "body": "A common error when solving mixture problems is not distributing correctly on the last row of the mixture table. This can lead to incorrect calculations and solutions. When multiplying amount by part, be sure to distribute the variables correctly to avoid this mistake.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 167"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_28_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Self-Explanation: Understanding Mixture Problems",
              "body": "To ensure you understand mixture problems, try explaining each step of the solution process in your own words. Be able to identify the unknown variables, given information, and what you are asked to find. Use the mixture table to set up two linear equations that represent the total amount after mixing and the relationship between the parts.",

                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 167"
              ]
            }

              "text": "5.1 \nPolynomials - Exponent Properties\nObjective: Simplify expressions using the properties of ex ponents. \nProblems with expoenents can often be simpliﬁed using a few b asic exponent\nproperties. Exponents represent repeated multiplication . We will use this fact to\ndiscover the important properties. \nWorld View Note: The word exponent comes from the Latin “expo” meaning\nout of and “ponere” meaning place. While there is some debate , it seems that the\nBabylonians living in Iraq were the ﬁrst to do work with expon ents (dating back\nto the 23rd century BC or earlier!) \nExample 196. \na3a2 Expand exponents to multiplication problem\n( aaa )( aa) Now we have5 a′s being multiplied together\na5 Our Solution\nA quicker method to arrive at our answer would have been to jus t add the expo-\nnents: a3a2 = a3+2 = a5 This is known as the product rule of exponents\nProduct Rule of Exponents: aman = am+ n\nThe product rule of exponents can be used to simplify many pro blems. We will\nadd the exponent on like variables. This is shown in the follo wing examples\nExample 197. \n32 · 36 · 3 Same base, add the exponents 2 + 6 + 1 \n39 Our Solution\nExample 198. \n2 x3y5z · 5 xy 2 z3 Multiply 2 · 5 , add exponents on x, y and z\n10x4y7z4 Our Solution\nRather than multiplying, we will now try to divide with expon ents\nExample 199. \na5\na2 \nExpand exponents\naaaaa\naa Divide out two of thea′s \naaa Convert to exponents\na3 Our Solution\n177\nA quicker method to arrive at the solution would have been to j ust subtract the\nexponents, a5 \na2 = a5− 2 = a3. This is known as the quotient rule of exponents. \nQuotient Rule of Exponents: am\nan = am− n\nThe quotient rule of exponents can similarly be used to simpl ify exponent prob-\nlems by subtracting exponents on like variables. This is sho wn in the following\nexamples. \nExample 200. \n713\n75\nSame base, subtract the exponents\n78 Our Solution\nExample 201. \n5 a3b5c2 \n2 ab3c Subtract exponents on a, b and c\n5 \n2 \na2 b2 c Our Solution\nA third property we will look at will have an exponent problem raised to a second\nexponent. This is investigated in the following example. \nExample 202. \n( a2 ) 3\nThis means we havea2 three times\na2 · a2 · a2 Add exponents\na6 Our solution\nA quicker method to arrive at the solution would have been to j ust multiply the\nexponents, ( a2 ) 3 = a2 · 3 = a6. This is known as the power of a power rule of expo-\nnents. \nPower of a Power Rule of Exponents: ( am) n = amn\nThis property is often combined with two other properties wh ich we will investi-\ngate now. \nExample 203. \n( ab) 3 This means we have( ab) three times\n( ab)( ab)( ab) Three a′s and three b′s can be written with exponents\na3b3 Our Solution\n178"
            }

              "title": "What are Exponent Properties?",

              "id": "SEC_29_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Introducing Exponent Properties",
              "body": "Exponents represent repeated multiplication, and Exponent Properties help us simplify expressions with multiple variables. When we see an expression like a^3 * a^2, we can use the Product Rule to combine the exponents: a^(3+2) = a^5. This is just one example of how Exponent Properties make complex calculations easier.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 177"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_29_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Product Rule, Quotient Rule, and Power of a Power Rule",
              "body": "The Product Rule states that am * an = a^(m+n). The Quotient Rule states that am / an = a^(m-n). The Power of a Power Rule states that (am)^n = a^(mn). These rules help us simplify expressions by adding or subtracting exponents, and multiplying exponents when raising to a power.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 177"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_29_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Simplifying with Exponent Properties",
              "body": "Let's use the Product Rule to simplify the expression: 2^3 * 2^4. First, we combine the exponents using addition: 2^(3+4) = 2^7. Now, we can write this as a single exponent: 128.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 177"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_29_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistakes with Exponent Properties",
              "body": "A common mistake when applying Exponent Properties is forgetting to check for like variables before adding or subtracting exponents. For example, if we have the expression: a^3 * b^2, we might be tempted to add the exponents and get: a^(3+2) = a^5. However, this is incorrect because 'a' and 'b' are not like variables.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 177"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_29_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Reflecting on Exponent Properties",
              "body": "Now that we've learned the Product Rule, Quotient Rule, and Power of a Power Rule, let's think about how these properties can be applied to real-world problems. Consider a situation where you need to simplify an expression with multiple variables. Which rule would you use first? Why?",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 177"
              ]
            }

          "id": "SEC_30",

          "title": "Negative Exponents",
          "order": 29,

              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",

              "page_end": 183,
              "text": "5.2\nPolynomials - Negative Exponents\nObjective: Simplify expressions with negative exponents u sing the\nproperties of exponents. \nThere are a few special exponent properties that deal with ex ponents that are not\npositive. The ﬁrst is considered in the following example, w hich is worded out 2\ndiﬀerent ways: \nExample 214. \na3\na3\nUse the quotient rule to subtract exponents\na0 Our Solution, but now we consider the problema the second way: \na3\na3 Rewrite exponents as repeated multiplication\naaa\naaa\nReduce out all thea′s \n1 \n1 \n= 1 Our Solution, when we combine the two solutions we get: \na0 = 1 Our ﬁnal result. \nThis ﬁnal result is an imporant property known as the zero pow er rule of expo-\nnents\nZero Power Rule of Exponents: a0 = 1 \nAny number or expression raised to the zero power will always be 1. This is illus-\ntrated in the following example. \nExample 215. \n(3x2 ) 0 Zero power rule\n1 Our Solution\nAnother property we will consider here deals with negative e xponents. Again we\nwill solve the following example two ways. \n183\nExample 216. \na3\na5 Using the quotient rule, subtract exponents\na− 2 Our Solution, but we will also solve this problem another way. \na3\na5 Rewrite exponents as repeated multiplication\naaa\naaaaa Reduce three a′s out of top and bottom\n1 \naa Simplify to exponents\n1 \na2 Our Solution, putting these solutions together gives: \na− 2 = 1 \na2 Our Final Solution\nThis example illustrates an important property of exponent s. Negative exponents\nyield the reciprocal of the base. Once we take the reciprical the exponent is now\npositive. Also, it is important to note a negative exponent d oes not mean the\nexpression is negative, only that we need the reciprocal of t he base. Following are\nthe rules of negative exponents\nRules of Negative Exponets: \na− m = 1 \nm\n1 \na− m = am\n( a\nb\n) − m\n= bm\nam\nNegative exponents can be combined in several diﬀerent ways . As a general rule if\nwe think of our expression as a fraction, negative exponents in the numerator\nmust be moved to the denominator, likewise, negative expone nts in the denomi-\nnator need to be moved to the numerator. When the base with exp onent moves, \nthe exponent is now positive. This is illustrated in the foll owing example. \nExample 217. \na3b− 2 c\n2 d− 1 e− 4f 2 Negative exponents on b, d, and e need to ﬂip\na 3cde 4\n2 b2 f 2 Our Solution\n184"
            }
          ],

              "title": "Rules of Negative Exponents",
              "explanation": "There are several rules for working with negative exponents, including: (1) $a^{-m} = rac{1}{a^m}$, which states that a negative exponent is equivalent to taking the reciprocal of the base raised to a positive power; and (2) $(ab)^{-m} = b^{-m}a^{-m}$, which allows us to distribute the negative exponent across multiple factors.",

              "id": "SEC_30_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "What are Negative Exponents?",
              "body": "Negative exponents may seem intimidating at first, but they're actually quite straightforward. In essence, a negative exponent tells us that we need to take the reciprocal of the base. For example, if we have an expression like $a^{-2}$, this means that we need to take the reciprocal of $a^2$. This is a fundamental property of exponents, and it's essential to understand how they work in order to simplify complex expressions.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 183"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_30_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Definition of Negative Exponents",
              "body": "A negative exponent is defined as: $a^{-m} = rac{1}{a^m}$, where $a$ is the base and $m$ is the exponent. This means that any number or expression raised to a negative power will always be the reciprocal of the same number or expression raised to a positive power.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 183"
              ]

              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Simplifying an Expression with Negative Exponents",
              "body": "Suppose we want to simplify the expression $a^{-3}b^{2}c^{-1}$. Using the definition of negative exponents, we can rewrite this as: $ rac{1}{a^3} imes b^2 imes rac{1}{c}$.",
              "source_refs": [

              ]
            },

              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistakes with Negative Exponents",

        {
          "id": "SEC_31",
          "chapter_id": "CH_6",
          "number": "5.3",
          "title": "Scientiﬁc Notation",
          "order": 30,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 188,
              "page_end": 188,
              "text": "5.3\nPolynomials - Scientiﬁc Notation\nObjective: Multiply and divide expressions using scientiﬁ c notation and\nexponent properties. \nOne application of exponent properties comes from scientiﬁ c notation. Scientiﬁc\nnotation is used to represent really large or really small nu mbers. An example of\nreally large numbers would be the distance that light travel s in a year in miles. \nAn example of really small numbers would be the mass of a singl e hydrogen atom\nin grams. Doing basic operations such as multiplication and division with these\nnumbers would normally be very combersome. However, our exp onent properties\nmake this process much simpler. \nFirst we will take a look at what scientiﬁc notation is. Scien tiﬁc notation has two\nparts, a number between one and ten (it can be equal to one, but not ten), and\nthat number multiplied by ten to some exponent. \nScientiﬁc Notation: a × 10b where 1 ⩽ a < 10\nThe exponent, b, is very important to how we convert between scientiﬁc notat ion\nand normal numbers, or standard notation. The exponent tell s us how many\ntimes we will multiply by 10. Multiplying by 10 in aﬀect moves the decimal point\none place. So the exponent will tell us how many times the expo nent moves\nbetween scientiﬁc notation and standard notation. To decid e which direction to\nmove the decimal (left or right) we simply need to remember th at positive expo-\nnents mean in standard notation we have a big number (bigger t han ten) and neg-\native exponents mean in standard notation we have a small num ber (less than\none). \nKeeping this in mind, we can easily make conversions between standard notation\nand scientiﬁc notation. \nExample 221. \nConvert 14, 200 to scientiﬁc notation Put decimal after ﬁrst nonzero num ber\n1.42 Exponent is how many times decimal moved , 4\n× 104 Positive exponent, standard notation is big\n1.42 × 104 Our Solution\nExample 222. \nConvert 0.0042 to scientiﬁc notation Put decimal after ﬁrst nonzero number\n4.2 Exponent is how many times decimal moved , 3\n× 10− 3 Negative exponent, standard notation is small\n4.2 × 10− 3 Our Solution\n188\nExample 223. \nConvert 3.21 × 105 to standard notation Positive exponent means standard nota tion\nbig number. Move decimal right5 places\n321 , 000 Our Solution\nExample 224. \nConver 7.4× 10− 3 to standard notation Negative exponent means standard nota tion\nis a small number. Move decimal left3 places\n0.0074 Our Solution\nConverting between standard notation and scientiﬁc notati on is important to\nunderstand how scientiﬁc notation works and what it does. He re our main\ninterest is to be able to multiply and divide numbers in scien tiﬁc notation using\nexponent properties. The way we do this is ﬁrst do the operati on with the front\nnumber (multiply or divide) then use exponent properties to simplify the 10’s. \nScientiﬁc notation is the only time where it will be allowed t o have negative expo-\nnents in our ﬁnal solution. The negative exponent simply inf orms us that we are\ndealing with small numbers. Consider the following example s. \nExample 225. \n( 2.1 × 10− 7)( 3.7 × 105) Deal with numbers and 10′s separately\n( 2.1 )( 3.7) = 7.77 Multiply numbers\n10− 7105 = 10− 2 Use product rule on 10′s and add exponents\n7.77 × 10− 2 Our Solution\nExample 226. \n4.96 × 104\n3.1 × 10− 3\nDeal with numbers and 10′s separately\n4.96\n3.1 = 1.6 Divide Numbers\n104\n10− 3 = 107 Use quotient rule to subtract exponents, be careful with negatives! \nBe careful with negatives, 4 − ( − 3) = 4 + 3 = 7\n1.6 × 107 Our Solution\n189"
            }
          ],

            "COMP_31"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Scientiﬁc Notation well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Explain the concept of scientific notation and its significance in representing large or small numbers.",
            "Convert numbers between standard notation and scientific notation using exponent properties.",
            "Multiply and divide expressions in scientific notation using exponent properties."

This allows us to represent extremely large or small quantities using a compact and intuitive notation.",

              "id": "SEC_31_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "What is Scientific Notation?",
              "body": "Scientific notation is an essential tool in mathematics, especially when dealing with very large or very small numbers. Imagine trying to express the distance light travels in a year in miles - it would be an enormous number! Or consider the mass of a single hydrogen atom in grams - it's incredibly tiny. Scientific notation helps us simplify these calculations by representing such numbers in a more manageable form.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 188"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_31_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Scientific Notation Definition",
              "body": "The scientific notation of a number is given by a × 10^b, where 1 ≤ a < 10 and b is an integer. The exponent b tells us how many times we need to multiply or divide the number by 10 to obtain its standard form.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 188"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_31_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Converting Between Notations",
              "body": "Let's convert 14,200 to scientific notation. We start by placing the decimal point after the first nonzero digit: 1.42. The exponent is determined by how many places we moved the decimal point - in this case, 4. Since the exponent is positive, standard notation represents a large number. Therefore, our solution is 1.42 × 10^4.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 188"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_31_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistake: Negative Exponents",
              "body": "Be careful when dealing with negative exponents! A common mistake is to think that a negative exponent means we're multiplying by 1/10. However, in scientific notation, a negative exponent simply indicates that we're dealing with small numbers. Remember that moving the decimal point to the left (when using a negative exponent) corresponds to dividing by 10.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 188"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_31_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Self-Explanation",
              "body": "Recall that scientific notation has two main components: the number between 1 and 10, and the exponent. When converting between notations, pay attention to how many places you move the decimal point - this determines the value of the exponent. Also, remember that negative exponents represent small numbers.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 188"
              ]
            }
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_32",
          "chapter_id": "CH_6",
          "number": "5.4",
          "title": "Introduction to Polynomials",
          "order": 31,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 192,
              "page_end": 192,
              "text": "5.4\nPolynomials - Introduction to Polynomials\nObjective: Evaluate, add, and subtract polynomials. \nMany applications in mathematics have to do with what are cal led polynomials. \nPolynomials are made up of terms. Terms are a product of numbers and/or vari-\nables. For example, 5 x, 2 y2 , − 5 , ab 3c, and x are all terms. Terms are connected\nto each other by addition or subtraction. Expressions are of ten named based on\nthe number of terms in them. A monomial has one term, such as 3x2 . A bino-\nmial has two terms, such as a2 − b2 . A Trinomial has three terms, such as ax 2 + \nbx + c. The term polynomial means many terms. Monomials, binomials, trino-\nmials, and expressions with more terms all fall under the umb rella of “polyno-\nmials”. \nIf we know what the variable in a polynomial represents we can replace the vari-\nable with the number and evaluate the polynomial as shown in t he following\nexample. \nExample 230. \n2 x2 − 4x + 6 when x = − 4 Replace variablex with − 4\n2( − 4) 2 − 4( − 4) + 6 Exponents ﬁrst\n2( 16) − 4( − 4) + 6 Multiplication ( we can do all terms at once) \n32 + 16 + 6 Add\n54 Our Solution\nIt is important to be careful with negative variables and exp onents. Remember\nthe exponent only eﬀects the number it is physically attache d to. This means − \n32 = − 9 because the exponent is only attached to the 3. Also, ( − 3) 2 = 9 because\nthe exponent is attached to the parenthesis and eﬀects every thing inside. When\nwe replace a variable with parenthesis like in the previous e xample, the substi-\ntuted value is in parenthesis. So the ( − 4) 2 = 16 in the example. However, con-\nsider the next example. \nExample 231. \n− x2 + 2 x + 6 when x = 3 Replace variablex with 3\n− (3) 2 + 2(3) + 6 Exponent only on the3, not negative\n− 9 + 2(3) + 6 Multiply\n− 9 + 6 + 6 Add\n3 Our Solution\n192\nWorld View Note: Ada Lovelace in 1842 described a Diﬀerence Engine that\nwould be used to caluclate values of polynomials. Her work be came the founda-\ntion for what would become the modern computer (the programm ing language\nAda was named in her honor), more than 100 years after her deat h from cancer. \nGenerally when working with polynomials we do not know the va lue of the vari-\nable, so we will try and simplify instead. The simplest opera tion with polynomials\nis addition. When adding polynomials we are mearly combinin g like terms. Con-\nsider the following example\nExample 232. \n(4x3 − 2 x + 8) + (3x3 − 9x2 − 11 ) Combine like terms4x3 + 3x3 and 8 − 11 \n7x3 − 9x2 − 2 x − 3 Our Solution\nGenerally ﬁnal answers for polynomials are written so the ex ponent on the vari-\nable counts down. Example 3 demonstrates this with the expon ent counting down\n3, 2, 1, 0 (recall x0 = 1 ). Subtracting polynomials is almost as fast. One extra step\ncomes from the minus in front of the parenthesis. When we have a negative in\nfront of parenthesis we distribute it through, changing the signs of everything\ninside. The same is done for the subtraction sign. \nExample 233. \n(5 x2 − 2 x + 7) − (3x2 + 6x − 4) Distribute negative through second part\n5 x2 − 2 x + 7 − 3x2 − 6x + 4 Combine like terms5 x2 − 3x3, − 2 x − 6x, and 7 + 4\n2 x2 − 8x + 11 Our Solution\nAddition and subtraction can also be combined into the same p roblem as shown\nin this ﬁnal example. \nExample 234. \n(2 x2 − 4x + 3) + (5 x2 − 6x + 1) − ( x2 − 9x + 8) Distribute negative through\n2 x2 − 4x + 3 + 5 x2 − 6x + 1 − x2 + 9x − 8 Combine like terms\n6x2 − x − 4 Our Solution\n193"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_32"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Polynomials well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [],
          "concepts": [],
          "lesson_blocks": [],
          "worked_examples": [],
          "checks": [],
          "mastery_quiz": [],
          "is_assessment_section": false,
          "assessment_reason": "",

          "chat_history": []
        },
        {
          "id": "SEC_33",
          "chapter_id": "CH_6",
          "number": "5.5",
          "title": "Multiply Polynomials",
          "order": 32,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 196,
              "page_end": 196,
              "text": "5.5\nPolynomials - Multiplying Polynomials\nObjective: Multiply polynomials. \nMultiplying polynomials can take several diﬀerent forms ba sed on what we are\nmultiplying. We will ﬁrst look at multiplying monomials, th en monomials by\npolynomials and ﬁnish with polynomials by polynomials. \nMultiplying monomials is done by multiplying the numbers or coeﬃcients and\nthen adding the exponents on like factors. This is shown in th e next example. \nExample 235. \n(4x3y4z)(2 x2 y6z3) Multiply numbers and add exponents forx, y, and z\n8x5y10z4 Our Solution\nIn the previous example it is important to remember that the z has an exponent\nof 1 when no exponent is written. Thus for our answer the z has an exponent of\n1 + 3 = 4. Be very careful with exponents in polynomials. If we are add ing or sub-\ntracting the exponnets will stay the same, but when we multip ly (or divide) the\nexponents will be changing. \nNext we consider multiplying a monomial by a polynomial. We h ave seen this\noperation before with distributing through parenthesis. H ere we will see the exact\nsame process. \nExample 236. \n4x3(5 x2 − 2 x + 5) Distribute the 4x3, multiplying numbers, adding exponents\n20x5 − 8x4 + 20x3 Our Solution\nFollowing is another example with more variables. When dist ributing the expo-\nnents on a are added and the exponents on b are added. \nExample 237. \n2 a3b(3ab2 − 4a) Distribute, multiplying numbers and adding exponents\n6a4b3 − 8a4b Our Solution\nThere are several diﬀerent methods for multiplying polynom ials. All of which\nwork, often students prefer the method they are ﬁrst taught. Here three methods\nwill be discussed. All three methods will be used to solve the same two multipli-\ncation problems. \nMultiply by Distributing\n196\nJust as we distribute a monomial through parenthesis we can d istribute an entire\npolynomial. As we do this we take each term of the second polyn omial and put it\nin front of the ﬁrst polynomial. \nExample 238. \n(4x + 7y)(3x − 2 y) Distribute (4x + 7y) through parenthesis\n3x(4x + 7y) − 2 y(4x + 7y) Distribute the 3x and − 2 y\n12x2 + 21 xy − 8xy − 14y2 Combine like terms 21 xy − 8xy\n12x2 + 13xy − 14y2 Our Solution\nThis example illustrates an important point, the negative/ subtraction sign stays\nwith the 2 y. Which means on the second step the negative is also distribu ted\nthrough the last set of parenthesis. \nMultiplying by distributing can easily be extended to probl ems with more terms. \nFirst distribute the front parenthesis onto each term, then distribute again! \nExample 239. \n(2 x − 5)(4x2 − 7x + 3) Distribute (2 x − 5) through parenthesis\n4x2 (2 x − 5) − 7x(2 x − 5) + 3(2 x − 5) Distribute again through each parenthesis\n8x3 − 20x2 − 14x2 + 35x + 6x − 15 Combine like terms\n8x3 − 34x2 + 41 x − 15 Our Solution\nThis process of multiplying by distributing can easily be re versed to do an impor-\ntant procedure known as factoring. Factoring will be addres sed in a future lesson. \nMultiply by FOIL\nAnother form of multiplying is known as FOIL. Using the FOIL m ethod we mul-\ntiply each term in the ﬁrst binomial by each term in the second binomial. The\nletters of FOIL help us remember every combination. F stands for First, we mul-\ntiply the ﬁrst term of each binomial. O stand for Outside, we m ultiply the outside\ntwo terms. I stands for Inside, we multiply the inside two ter ms. L stands for\nLast, we multiply the last term of each binomial. This is show n in the next\nexample: \nExample 240. \n(4x + 7y)(3x − 2 y) Use FOIL to multiply\n(4x)(3x) = 12x2 F − First terms (4x)(3x) \n(4x)( − 2 y) = − 8xy O − Outside terms (4x)( − 2 y) \n(7y)(3x) = 21 xy I − Inside terms (7y)(3x) \n(7y)( − 2 y) = − 14y2 L − Last terms (7y)( − 2 y) \n12x2 − 8xy + 21 xy − 14y2 Combine like terms − 8xy + 21 xy\n12x2 + 13xy − 14y2 Our Solution\n197"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_33"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Multiply Polynomials well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [],

          "lesson_blocks": [],

        {
          "id": "SEC_34",
          "chapter_id": "CH_6",
          "number": "5.6",
          "title": "Multiply Special Products",
          "order": 33,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 201,
              "page_end": 201,
              "text": "5.6\nPolynomials - Multiply Special Products\nObjective: Recognize and use special product rules of a sum a nd diﬀer-\nence and perfect squares to multiply polynomials. \nThere are a few shortcuts that we can take when multiplying po lynomials. If we\ncan recognize them the shortcuts can help us arrive at the sol ution much quicker. \nThese shortcuts will also be useful to us as our study of algeb ra continues. \nThe ﬁrst shortcut is often called a sum and a diﬀerence . A sum and a diﬀer-\nence is easily recognized as the numbers and variables are ex actly the same, but\nthe sign in the middle is diﬀerent (one sum, one diﬀerence). T o illustrate the\nshortcut consider the following example, multiplied by the distributing method. \nExample 246. \n( a + b)( a − b) Distribute ( a + b) \na( a + b) − b( a + b) Distribute a and − b\na2 + ab − ab − b2 Combine like termsab − ab\na2 − b2 Our Solution\nThe important part of this example is the middle terms subtra cted to zero. \nRather than going through all this work, when we have a sum and a diﬀerence we\nwill jump right to our solution by squaring the ﬁrst term and s quaring the last\nterm, putting a subtraction between them. This is illustrat ed in the following\nexample\nExample 247. \n( x − 5)( x + 5) Recognize sum and diﬀerence\nx2 − 25 Square both , put subtraction between. Our Solution\nThis is much quicker than going through the work of multiplyi ng and combining\nlike terms. Often students ask if they can just multiply out u sing another method\nand not learn the shortcut. These shortcuts are going to be ve ry useful when we\nget to factoring polynomials, or reversing the multiplicat ion process. For this\nreason it is very important to be able to recognize these shor tcuts. More examples\nare shown here. \n201\nExample 248. \n(3x + 7)(3x − 7) Recognize sum and diﬀerence\n9x2 − 49 Square both , put subtraction between. Our Solution\nExample 249. \n(2 x − 6y)(2 x + 6y) Recognize sum and diﬀerence\n4x2 − 36y2 Square both, put subtraction between. Our Solution\nIt is interesting to note that while we can multiply and get an answer like a2 − b2 \n(with subtraction), it is impossible to multiply real numbe rs and end up with a\nproduct such as a2 + b2 (with addition). \nAnother shortcut used to multiply is known as a perfect square . These are easy\nto recognize as we will have a binomial with a 2 in the exponent. The following\nexample illustrates multiplying a perfect square\nExample 250. \n( a + b) 2 Squared is same as multiplying by itself\n( a + b)( a + b) Distribute ( a + b) \na( a + b) + b( a + b) Distribute again through ﬁnal parenthesis\na2 + ab + ab + b2 Combine like termsab + ab\na2 + 2 ab + b2 Our Solution\nThis problem also helps us ﬁnd our shortcut for multiplying. The ﬁrst term in the\nanswer is the square of the ﬁrst term in the problem. The middl e term is 2 times\nthe ﬁrst term times the second term. The last term is the squar e of the last term. \nThis can be shortened to square the ﬁrst, twice the product, s quare the last. If we\ncan remember this shortcut we can square any binomial. This i s illustrated in the\nfollowing example\nExample 251. \n( x − 5) 2 Recognize perfect square\nx2 Square the ﬁrst\n2( x)( − 5) = − 10x Twice the product\n( − 5) 2 = 25 Square the last\nx2 − 10x + 25 Our Solution\n202"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_34"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Multiply Special Products well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_35",
          "chapter_id": "CH_6",
          "number": "5.7",
          "title": "Divide Polynomials",
          "order": 34,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 205,
              "page_end": 205,
              "text": "5.7\nPolynomials - Divide Polynomials\nObjective: Divide polynomials using long division. \nDividing polynomials is a process very similar to long divis ion of whole numbers. \nBut before we look at that, we will ﬁrst want to be able to maste r dividing a\npolynomial by a monomial. The way we do this is very similar to distributing, \nbut the operation we distribute is the division, dividing ea ch term by the mono-\nmial and reducing the resulting expression. This is shown in the following exam-\nples\nExample 256. \n9x5 + 6x4 − 18x3 − 24x2 \n3x2 \nDivide each term in the numerator by3x2 \n9x5\n3x2 + 6x4\n3x2 − 18x3\n3x2 − 24x2 \n3x2 Reduce each fraction, subtracting exponents\n3x3 + 2 x2 − 6x − 8 Our Solution\nExample 257. \n8x3 + 4x2 − 2 x + 6\n4x2 Divide each term in the numerator by4x2 \n8x3\n4x2 + 4x2 \n4x2 − 2 x\n4x2 + 6\n4x2 Reduce each fraction, subtracting exponents\nRemember negative exponents are moved to denominator\n2 x + 1 − 1 \n2 x + 3\n2 x2 Our Solution\nThe previous example illustrates that sometimes we will hav e fractions in our\nsolution, as long as they are reduced this will be correct for our solution. Also\ninteresting in this problem is the second term 4x2 \n4x2 divided out completely. \nRemember that this means the reduced answer is 1 not 0. \nLong division is required when we divide by more than just a mo nomial. Long\ndivision with polynomials works very similar to long divisi on with whole numbers. \n205\nAn example is given to review the (general) steps that are use d with whole num-\nbers that we will also use with polynomials\nExample 258. \n4| 631 \nDivide front numbers: 6\n4\n= 1 / \n1 \n4| 631 Multiply this number by divisor: 1 · 4 = 4\n− 4 Change the sign of this number ( make it subtract) and combine\n23 Bring down next number\n1 5 Repeat, divide front numbers: 23\n4 = 5 / \n4| 631 \n− 4\n23 Multiply this number by divisor : 5 · 4 = 20\n− 20 Change the sign of this number( make it subtract) and combine\n31 Bring down next number\n157 Repeat, divide front numbers: 31 \n4 = 7/ \n4| 631 \n− 4\n23\n− 20\n31 Multiply this number by divisor : 7 · 4 = 28\n− 28 Change the sign of this number( make it subtract) and combine\n3 We will write our remainder asa fraction, over the divisor, added to the end\n157 3\n4\nOur Solution\nThis same process will be used to multiply polynomials. The o nly diﬀerence is we\nwill replace the word “number” with the word “term”\nDividing Polynomials\n1. Divide front terms\n2. Multiply this term by the divisor\n206"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_35"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Divide Polynomials well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

    {
      "id": "CH_7",
      "number": "6",
      "title": "Factoring",
      "order": 6,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_36",
          "chapter_id": "CH_7",
          "number": "6.1",
          "title": "Greatest Common Factor",
          "order": 35,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 212,
              "page_end": 212,
              "text": "6.1 \nFactoring - Greatest Common Factor\nObjective: Find the greatest common factor of a polynomial a nd factor\nit out of the expression. \nThe opposite of multiplying polynomials together is factor ing polynomials. There\nare many beniﬁts of a polynomial being factored. We use facto red polynomials to\nhelp us solve equations, learn behaviors of graphs, work wit h fractions and more. \nBecause so many concepts in algebra depend on us being able to factor polyno-\nmials it is very important to have very strong factoring skil ls. \nIn this lesson we will focus on factoring using the greatest c ommon factor or GCF\nof a polynomial. When we multiplied polynomials, we multipl ied monomials by\npolynomials by distributing, solving problems such as 4x2 (2 x2 − 3x + 8) = 8 x4 − \n12x3 + 32x. In this lesson we will work the same problem backwards. We wi ll\nstart with 8x2 − 12x3 + 32x and try and work backwards to the 4x2 (2 x − 3x + 8) . \nTo do this we have to be able to ﬁrst identify what is the GCF of a polynomial. \nWe will ﬁrst introduce this by looking at ﬁnding the GCF of sev eral numbers. To\nﬁnd a GCF of sevearal numbers we are looking for the largest nu mber that can be\ndivided by each of the numbers. This can often be done with qui ck mental math\nand it is shown in the following example\nExample 262. \nFind the GCF of 15, 24, and 27\n15\n3\n= 5 , 24\n3 = 6, 27\n3 = 9 Each of the numbers can be divided by3\nGCF = 3 Our Solution\nWhen there are variables in our problem we can ﬁrst ﬁnd the GCF of the num-\n212\nbers using mental math, then we take any variables that are in common with each\nterm, using the lowest exponent. This is shown in the next exa mple\nExample 263. \nGCF of 24x4y2 z , 18x2 y4, and 12x3yz 5\n24\n6 = 4, 18\n6 = 3, 12\n6 = 2 Each number can be divided by6\nx2 y x and y are in all3, using lowest exponets\nGCF = 6x2 y Our Solution\nTo factor out a GCF from a polynomial we ﬁrst need to identify t he GCF of all\nthe terms, this is the part that goes in front of the parenthes is, then we divide\neach term by the GCF, the answer is what is left inside the pare nthesis. This is\nshown in the following examples\nExample 264. \n4x2 − 20x + 16 GCF is 4, divide each term by4\n4x2 \n4\n= x2 , − 20x\n4 = − 5 x, 16\n4 = 4 This is what is left inside the parenthesis\n4( x2 − 5 x + 4) Our Solution\nWith factoring we can always check our solutions by multiply ing (distributing in\nthis case) out the answer and the solution should be the origi nal equation. \nExample 265. \n25x4 − 15x3 + 20x2 GCF is 5 x2 , divide each term by this\n25x4\n5 x2 = 5 x2 , − 15x3\n5 x2 = − 3x, 20x2 \n5 x2 = 4 This is what is left inside the parenthesis\n5 x2 (5 x2 − 3x + 4) Our Solution\nExample 266. \n3x3y2 z + 5 x4y3z5 − 4xy 4 GCF is xy 2 , divide each term by this\n213"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_36"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Greatest Common Factor well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        },
        {
          "id": "SEC_37",
          "chapter_id": "CH_7",
          "number": "6.2",
          "title": "Grouping",
          "order": 36,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 216,
              "page_end": 216,
              "text": "6.2\nFactoring - Grouping\nObjective: Factor polynomials with four terms using groupi ng. \nThe ﬁrst thing we will always do when factoring is try to facto r out a GCF. This\nGCF is often a monomial like in the problem 5 x y + 10x z the GCF is the mono-\nmial 5 x, so we would have 5 x( y + 2 z) . However, a GCF does not have to be a\nmonomial, it could be a binomial. To see this, consider the fo llowing two example. \nExample 270. \n3ax − 7bx Both havex in common, factor it out\nx(3a − 7b) Our Solution\nNow the same problem, but instead of x we have (2 a + 5 b) . \nExample 271. \n3a(2 a + 5 b) − 7b(2 a + 5 b) Both have(2 a + 5 b) in common, factor it out\n(2 a + 5 b)(3a − 7b) Our Solution\nIn the same way we factored out a GCF of x we can factor out a GCF which is a\nbinomial, (2 a + 5 b) . This process can be extended to factor problems where there\nis no GCF to factor out, or after the GCF is factored out, there is more factoring\nthat can be done. Here we will have to use another strategy to f actor. We will use\na process known as grouping. Grouping is how we will factor if there are four\nterms in the problem. Remember, factoring is like multiplyi ng in reverse, so ﬁrst\nwe will look at a multiplication problem and then try to rever se the process. \nExample 272. \n(2 a + 3)(5 b + 2) Distribute (2 a + 3) into second parenthesis\n5 b(2 a + 3) + 2(2 a + 3) Distribute each monomial\n10ab + 15b + 4a + 6 Our Solution\nThe solution has four terms in it. We arrived at the solution b y looking at the\ntwo parts, 5 b(2 a + 3) and 2(2 a + 3) . When we are factoring by grouping we will\nalways divide the problem into two parts, the ﬁrst two terms a nd the last two\nterms. Then we can factor the GCF out of both the left and right sides. When we\ndo this our hope is what is left in the parenthesis will match o n both the left and\nright. If they match we can pull this matching GCF out front, p utting the rest in\nparenthesis and we will be factored. The next example is the s ame problem\nworked backwards, factoring instead of multiplying. \n216\nExample 273. \n10ab + 15b + 4a + 6 Split problem into two groups\n10ab + 15b + 4a + 6 GCF on left is5 b, on the right is2 \n5 b(2 a + 3) + 2(2 a + 3) (2 a + 3) is the same! Factor out this GCF\n(2 a + 3)(5 b + 2) Our Solution\nThe key for grouping to work is after the GCF is factored out of the left and\nright, the two binomials must match exactly. If there is any d iﬀerence between\nthe two we either have to do some adjusting or it can’t be facto red using the\ngrouping method. Consider the following example. \nExample 274. \n6x2 + 9xy − 14x − 21 y Split problem into two groups\n6x2 + 9xy\n− 14x − 21 y GCF on left is3x, on right is7\n3x(2 x + 3y) + 7( − 2 x − 3y) The signs in the parenthesis don′t match! \nwhen the signs don’t match on both terms we can easily make the m match by fac-\ntoring the opposite of the GCF on the right side. Instead of 7 we will use − 7. \nThis will change the signs inside the second parenthesis. \n3x(2 x + 3y) − 7(2 x + 3y) (2 x + 3y) is the same! Factor out this GCF\n(2 x + 3y)(3x − 7) Our Solution\nOften we can recognize early that we need to use the opposite o f the GCF when\nfactoring. If the ﬁrst term of the ﬁrst binomial is positive i n the problem, we will\nalso want the ﬁrst term of the second binomial to be positive. If it is negative\nthen we will use the opposite of the GCF to be sure they match. \nExample 275. \n5 xy − 8x − 10y + 16 Split the problem into two groups\n5 xy − 8x\n− 10y + 16 GCF on left isx, on right we needa negative, \nso we use− 2 \nx(5 y − 8) − 2(5 y − 8) (5 y − 8) is the same! Factor out this GCF\n(5 y − 8)( x − 2) Our Solution\n217"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_37"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Grouping well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_38",
          "chapter_id": "CH_7",
          "number": "6.3",
          "title": "Trinomials where a = 1",
          "order": 37,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 221,
              "page_end": 221,
              "text": "6.3\nFactoring - Trinomials where a = 1 \nObjective: Factor trinomials where the coeﬃcient of x 2 is one. \nFactoring with three terms, or trinomials, is the most impor tant type of factoring\nto be able to master. As factoring is multiplication backwar ds we will start with a\nmultipication problem and look at how we can reverse the proc ess. \nExample 282. \n( x + 6)( x − 4) Distribute ( x + 6) through second parenthesis\nx( x + 6) − 4( x + 6) Distribute each monomial through parenthesis\nx2 + 6x − 4x − 24 Combine like terms\nx2 + 2 x − 24 Our Solution\nYou may notice that if you reverse the last three steps the pro cess looks like\ngrouping. This is because it is grouping! The GCF of the left t wo terms is x and\nthe GCF of the second two terms is − 4. The way we will factor trinomials is to\nmake them into a polynomial with four terms and then factor by grouping. This\nis shown in the following example, the same problem worked ba ckwards\nExample 283. \nx2 + 2 x − 24 Split middle term into + 6x − 4x\nx2 + 6x − 4x − 24 Grouping : GCF on left isx, on right is− 4\nx( x + 6) − 4( x + 6) ( x + 6) is the same, factor out this GCF\n( x + 6)( x − 4) Our Solution\nThe trick to make these problems work is how we split the middl e term. Why did\nwe pick + 6x − 4x and not + 5 x − 3x? The reason is because 6x − 4x is the only\ncombination that works! So how do we know what is the one combi nation that\nworks? To ﬁnd the correct way to split the middle term we will u se what is called\nthe ac method. In the next lesson we will discuss why it is call ed the ac method. \nThe way the ac method works is we ﬁnd a pair of numers that multi ply to a cer-\ntain number and add to another number. Here we will try to mult iply to get the\nlast term and add to get the coeﬃcient of the middle term. In th e previous\n221\nexample that would mean we wanted to multiply to − 24 and add to + 2 . The\nonly numbers that can do this are 6 and − 4 ( 6 · − 4 = − 24 and 6 + ( − 4) = 2) . \nThis process is shown in the next few examples\nExample 284. \nx2 + 9x + 18 Want to multiply to 18 , add to 9\nx2 + 6x + 3x + 18 6 and 3, split the middle term\nx( x + 6) + 3( x + 6) Factor by grouping\n( x + 6)( x + 3) Our Solution\nExample 285. \nx2 − 4x + 3 Want to multiply to3, add to − 4\nx2 − 3x − x + 3 − 3 and − 1 , split the middle term\nx( x − 3) − 1( x − 3) Factor by grouping\n( x − 3)( x − 1) Our Solution\nExample 286. \nx2 − 8x − 20 Want to multiply to − 20, add to − 8\nx2 − 10x + 2 x − 20 − 10 and 2 , split the middle term\nx( x − 10) + 2( x − 10) Factor by grouping\n( x − 10)( x + 2) Our Solution\nOften when factoring we have two variables. These problems s olve just like prob-\nlems with one variable, using the coeﬃcients to decide how to split the middle\nterm\nExample 287. \na2 − 9ab + 14b2 Want to multiply to 14, add to − 9\na2 − 7ab − 2 ab + 14b2 − 7 and − 2 , split the middle term\na( a − 7b) − 2 b( a − 7b) Factor by grouping\n( a − 7b)( a − 2 b) Our Solution\n222"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_38"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Trinomials where a = 1 well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [],
          "concepts": [],
          "lesson_blocks": [],

        {
          "id": "SEC_39",
          "chapter_id": "CH_7",
          "number": "6.5",
          "title": "Factoring Special Products",
          "order": 38,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 229,
              "page_end": 229,
              "text": "6.5\nFactoring - Factoring Special Products\nObjective: Identify and factor special products including a diﬀerence of\nsquares, perfect squares, and sum and diﬀerence of cubes. \nWhen factoring there are a few special products that, if we ca n recognize them, \ncan help us factor polynomials. The ﬁrst is one we have seen be fore. When multi-\nplying special products we found that a sum and a diﬀerence co uld multiply to a\ndiﬀerence of squares. Here we will use this special product t o help us factor\nDiﬀerence of Squares: a2 − b2 = ( a + b)( a − b) \nIf we are subtracting two perfect squares then it will always factor to the sum and\ndiﬀerence of the square roots. \nExample 300. \nx2 − 16 Subtracting two perfect squares , the square roots are x and 4\n( x + 4)( x − 4) Our Solution\nExample 301. \n9a2 − 25b2 Subtracting two perfect squares, the square roots are 3a and 5 b\n(3a + 5 b)(3a − 5 b) Our Solution\nIt is important to note, that a sum of squares will never facto r. It is always\nprime. This can be seen if we try to use the ac method to factor x2 + 36. \nExample 302. \nx2 + 36 No bx term, we use 0 x. \nx2 + 0 x + 36 Multiply to 36 , add to 0 \n1 · 36, 2 · 18, 3 · 12, 4 · 9, 6 · 6 No combinations that multiply to 36 add to0 \nPrime, cannot factor Our Solution\n229\nIt turns out that a sum of squares is always prime. \nSum of Squares: a2 + b2 = Prime\nA great example where we see a sum of squares comes from factor ing a diﬀerence\nof 4th powers. Because the square root of a fourth power is a sq uare ( a4\n√\n= a2 ), \nwe can factor a diﬀerence of fourth powers just like we factor a diﬀerence of\nsquares, to a sum and diﬀerence of the square roots. This will give us two factors, \none which will be a prime sum of squares, and a second which wil l be a diﬀerence\nof squares which we can factor again. This is shown in the foll owing examples. \nExample 303. \na4 − b4 Diﬀerence of squares with roots a2 and b2 \n( a2 + b2 )( a2 − b2 ) The ﬁrst factor is prime, the second is a diﬀerence of squares! \n( a2 + b2 )( a + b)( a − b) Our Solution\nExample 304. \nx4 − 16 Diﬀerence of squares with roots x2 and 4\n( x2 + 4)( x2 − 4) The ﬁrst factor is prime, the second is a diﬀerence of squares! \n( x2 + 4)( x + 2)( x − 2) Our Solution\nAnother factoring shortcut is the perfect square. We had a sh ortcut for multi-\nplying a perfect square which can be reversed to help us facto r a perfect square\nPerfect Square: a2 + 2 ab + b2 = ( a + b) 2 \nA perfect square can be diﬃcult to recognize at ﬁrst glance, b ut if we use the ac\nmethod and get two of the same numbers we know we have a perfect square. \nThen we can just factor using the square roots of the ﬁrst and l ast terms and the\nsign from the middle. This is shown in the following examples . \nExample 305. \nx2 − 6x + 9 Multiply to 9, add to − 6\nThe numbers are− 3 and − 3, the same! Perfect square\n( x − 3) 2 Use square roots from ﬁrst and last terms and sign from the middle\n230"
            }
          ],

          ],

          "learning_objectives": [],

        {
          "id": "SEC_40",
          "chapter_id": "CH_7",
          "number": "6.6",
          "title": "Factoring Strategy",
          "order": 39,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 234,
              "page_end": 234,
              "text": "6.6\nFactoring - Factoring Strategy\nObjective: Idenﬁty and use the correct method to factor vari ous poly-\nnomials. \nWith so many diﬀerent tools used to factor, it is easy to get lo st as to which tool\nto use when. Here we will attempt to organize all the diﬀerent factoring types we\nhave seen. A large part of deciding how to solve a problem is ba sed on how many\nterms are in the problem. For all problem types we will always try to factor out\nthe GCF ﬁrst. \nFactoring Strategy (GCF First!!!!!) \n• 2 terms: sum or diﬀerence of squares or cubes: \na2 − b2 = ( a + b)( a − b) \na2 + b2 = Prime\na3 + b3 = ( a + b)( a2 − ab + b2 ) \na3 − b3 = ( a − b)( a2 + ab + b2 ) \n• 3 terms: ac method, watch for perfect square! \na2 + 2 ab + b2 = ( a + b) 2 \nMultiply to ac and add to b\n• 4 terms: grouping\nWe will use the above strategy to factor each of the following examples. Here the\nemphasis will be on which strategy to use rather than the step s used in that\nmethod. \nExample 312. \n4x2 + 56xy + 196y2 GCF ﬁrst, 4\n4( x2 + 14xy + 49y2 ) Three terms, try ac method, multiply to 49, add to 14\n7 and 7, perfect square! \n234\n4( x + 7y) 2 Our Solution\nExample 313. \n5 x 2 y + 15xy − 35x2 − 105x GCF ﬁrst, 5 x\n5 x( xy + 3y − 7x − 21 ) Four terms, try grouping\n5 x[ y( x + 3) − 7( x + 3)] ( x + 3) match! \n5 x( x + 3)( y − 7) Our Solution\nExample 314. \n100x2 − 400 GCF ﬁrst , 100\n100( x2 − 4) Two terms, diﬀerence of squares\n100( x + 4)( x − 4) Our Solution\nExample 315. \n108x3y2 − 39x2 y2 + 3xy 2 GCF ﬁrst, 3xy 2 \n3xy 2 ( 36x2 − 13x + 1) Thee terms, ac method, multiply to 36, add to − 13\n3xy 2 ( 36x2 − 9x − 4x + 1) − 9 and − 4, split middle term\n3xy 2 [9x(4x − 1) − 1(4x − 1)] Factor by grouping\n3xy 2 (4x − 1)(9x − 1) Our Solution\nWorld View Note: Variables originated in ancient Greece where Aristotle wou ld\nuse a single capital letter to represent a number. \nExample 316. \n5 + 625y3 GCF ﬁrst, 5 \n5(1 + 125y3) Two terms, sum of cubes\n5(1 + 5 y)(1 − 5 y + 25y2 ) Our Solution\nIt is important to be comfortable and conﬁdent not just with u sing all the fac-\ntoring methods, but decided on which method to use. This is wh y practice is very\nimportant! \n235"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_40"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Factoring Strategy well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_41",
          "chapter_id": "CH_7",
          "number": "6.7",
          "title": "Solve by Factoring",
          "order": 40,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 237,
              "page_end": 237,
              "text": "6.7\nFactoring - Solve by Factoring\nObjective: Solve quadratic equation by factoring and using the zero\nproduct rule. \nWhen solving linear equations such as 2 x − 5 = 21 we can solve for the variable\ndirectly by adding 5 and dividing by 2 to get 13. However, when we have x2 (or a\nhigher power of x) we cannot just isolate the variable as we did with the linear\nequations. One method that we can use to solve for the varaibl e is known as the\nzero product rule\nZero Product Rule: If a b= 0 then either a = 0 or b = 0\nThe zero product rule tells us that if two factors are multipl ied together and the\nanswer is zero, then one of the factors must be zero. We can use this to help us\nsolve factored polynomials as in the following example. \nExample 317. \n(2 x − 3)(5 x + 1) = 0 One factor must be zero\n2 x − 3 = 0 or 5 x + 1 = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n+ 3 + 3\n− 1 − 1 Solve each equation\n2 x = 3 or 5 x = − 1 \n2 2 5 5 \nx = 3\n2 \nor − 1 \n5 Our Solution\nFor the zero product rule to work we must have factors to set eq ual to zero. This\nmeans if the problem is not already factored we will factor it ﬁrst. \nExample 318. \n4x2 + x − 3 = 0 Factor using the ac method, multiply to − 12, add to 1 \n4x2 − 3x + 4x − 3 = 0 The numbers are− 3 and 4, split the middle term\nx(4x − 3) + 1(4x − 3) = 0 Factor by grouping\n(4x − 3)( x + 1) = 0 One factor must be zero\n4x − 3 = 0 or x + 1 = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n237\n+ 3 + 3 − 1 − 1 Solve each equation\n4x = 3 or x = − 1 \n4 4\nx = 3\n4\nor − 1 Our Solution\nAnother important part of the zero product rule is that befor e we factor, the\nequation must equal zero. If it does not, we must move terms ar ound so it does\nequal zero. Generally we like the x2 term to be positive. \nExample 319. \nx2 = 8x − 15 Set equal to zero by moving terms to the left\n− 8x + 15 − 8x + 15\nx2 − 8x + 15 = 0 Factor using the ac method, multiply to 15, add to − 8\n( x − 5)( x − 3) = 0 The numbers are− 5 and − 3\nx − 5 = 0 or x − 3 = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n+ 5 + 5 + 3 + 3 Solve each equation\nx = 5 or x = 3 Our Solution\nExample 320. \n( x − 7)( x + 3) = − 9 Not equal to zero, multiply ﬁrst, use FOIL\nx2 − 7x + 3x − 21 = − 9 Combine like terms\nx2 − 4x − 21 = − 9 Move − 9 to other side so equation equals zero\n+ 9 + 9\nx2 − 4x − 12 = 0 Factor using the ac method, mutiply to − 12, add to − 4\n( x − 6)( x + 2) = 0 The numbers are6 and − 2 \nx − 6 = 0 or x + 2 = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n+ 6 + 6 − 2 − 2 Solve each equation\nx = 6 or − 2 Our Solution\nExample 321. \n3x2 + 4x − 5 = 7x2 + 4x − 14 Set equal to zero by moving terms to the right\n− 3x2 − 4x + 5 − 3x2 − 4x + 5 \n0 = 4x2 − 9 Factor using diﬀerence of squares\n238"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_41"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Solve by Factoring well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

          "is_assessment_section": false,
          "assessment_reason": "",

    {
      "id": "CH_8",
      "number": "7",
      "title": "Rational Expressions",
      "order": 7,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_42",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.1",
          "title": "Reduce Rational Expressions",
          "order": 41,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 243,
              "page_end": 243,
              "text": "7.1 \nRational Expressions - Reduce Rational Expressions\nObjective: Reduce rational expressions by dividing out com mon fac-\ntors. \nRational expressions are expressions written as a quotient of polynomials. \nExamples of rational expressions include: \nx2 − x − 12\nx2 − 9x + 20\nand 3\nx − 2 and a − b\nb − a and 3\n2 \nAs rational expressions are a special type of fraction, it is important to remember\nwith fractions we cannot have zero in the denominator of a fra ction. For this\nreason, rational expressions may have one more excluded val ues, or values that\nthe variable cannot be or the expression would be undeﬁned. \nExample 326. \nState the excluded value( s ): x2 − 1 \n3x2 + 5 x\nDenominator cannot be zero\n3x2 + 5 x /\u0002 0 Factor\nx(3x + 5) /\u0002 0 Set each factor not equal to zero\nx /\u0002 0 or 3x + 5 /\u0002 0 Subtract 5 from second equation\n− 5 − 5 \n3x /\u0002 − 5 Divide by 3\n3 3\nx /\u0002\n− 5 \n3 Second equation is solved\nx /\u0002 0 or − 5 \n3 Our Solution\nThis means we can use any value for x in the equation except for 0 and − 5\n3 . We\n243\ncan however, evaluate any other value in the expression. \nWorld View Note: The number zero was not widely accepted in mathematical\nthought around the world for many years. It was the Mayans of C entral America\nwho ﬁrst used zero to aid in the use of their base 20 system as a p lace holder! \nRational expressions are easily evaluated by simply substi tuting the value for the\nvariable and using order of operations. \nExample 327. \nx2 − 4\nx2 + 6x + 8\nwhen x = − 6 Substitute − 5 in for each variable\n( − 6) 2 − 4\n( − 6) 2 + 6( − 6) + 8 Exponents ﬁrst\n36 − 4\n36 + 6( − 6) + 8 Multiply\n36 − 4\n36 − 36 + 8 Add and subtract\n32\n8 Reduce\n4 Our Solution\nJust as we reduced the previous example, often a rational exp ression can be\nreduced, even without knowing the value of the variable. Whe n we reduce we\ndivide out common factors. We have already seen this with mon omials when we\ndiscussed properties of exponents. If the problem only has m onomials we can\nreduce the coeﬃcients, and subtract exponents on the variab les. \nExample 328. \n15x4y2 \n25x2 y6 Reduce , subtract exponents. Negative exponents move to denominator\n3x2 \n5 y4 Our Solution\n244"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_42"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Reduce Rational Expressions well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_43",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.2",
          "title": "Multiply and Divide",
          "order": 42,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 248,
              "page_end": 248,
              "text": "7.2\nRational Expressions - Multiply & Divide\nObjective: Multiply and divide rational expressions. \nMultiplying and dividing rational expressions is very simi lar to the process we use\nto multiply and divide fractions. \nExample 332. \n15\n49\n· 14\n45\nFirst reduce common factors from numerator and denominator( 15 and 7) \n1 \n7\n· 2 \n3\nMultiply numerators across and denominators across\n2 \n21 Our Solution\nThe process is identical for division with the extra ﬁrst ste p of multiplying by the\nreciprocal. When multiplying with rational expressions we follow the same pro-\ncess, ﬁrst divide out common factors, then multiply straigh t across. \n248\nExample 333. \n25x2 \n9y8 · 24y4\n55x7\nReduce coeﬃcients by dividing out common factors (3 and 5) \nReduce, subtracting exponents, negative exponents in denominator\n5 \n3y4 · 8\n11 x5 Multiply across\n40\n33x5y4 Our Solution\nDivision is identical in process with the extra ﬁrst step of m ultiplying by the\nreciprocal. \nExample 334. \na4b2 \na\n÷ b4\n4 Multiply by the reciprocal\na4b2 \na · 4\nb4 Subtract exponents on variables, negative exponents in denominator\na3\n1 · 4\nb2 Multiply across\n4a3\nb2 Our Solution\nJust as with reducing rational expressions, before we reduc e a multiplication\nproblem, it must be factored ﬁrst. \nExample 335. \nx2 − 9\nx2 + x − 20\n· x2 − 8x + 16\n3x + 9 Factor each numerator and denominator\n( x + 3)( x − 3) \n( x − 4)( x + 5) · ( x − 4)( x − 4) \n3( x + 3) Divide out common factors( x + 3) and ( x − 4) \nx − 3\nx + 5 · x − 4\n3 Multiply across\n249"
            }
          ],

            "COMP_43"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Multiply and Divide well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_44",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.3",
          "title": "Least Common Denominator",
          "order": 43,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 253,
              "page_end": 253,
              "text": "7.3\nRational Expressions - Least Common Denominators\nObjective: Idenﬁty the least common denominator and build u p\ndenominators to match this common denominator. \nAs with fractions, the least common denominator or LCD is ver y important to\nworking with rational expressions. The process we use to ﬁnd the LCD is based\non the process used to ﬁnd the LCD of intergers. \nExample 338. \nFind the LCD of8 and 6 Consider multiples of the larger number\n8, 16, 24\n/ . 24 is the ﬁrst multiple of8 that is also divisible by6\n24 Our Solution\nWhen ﬁnding the LCD of several monomials we ﬁrst ﬁnd the LCD of the coeﬃ-\ncients, then use all variables and attach the highest expone nt on each variable. \nExample 339. \nFind the LCD of4x2 y5 and 6x4y3z6\nFirst ﬁnd the LCD of coeﬃcients 4 and 6\n12 12 is the LCD of 4 and 6\nx4y5z6 Use all variables with highest exponents on each variable\n12x4y5z6 Our Solution\nThe same pattern can be used on polynomials that have more tha n one term. \nHowever, we must ﬁrst factor each polynomial so we can identi fy all the factors to\nbe used (attaching highest exponent if necessary). \nExample 340. \nFind the LCD of x2 + 2 x − 3 and x2 − x − 12 Factor each polynomial\n( x − 1)( x + 3) and ( x − 4)( x + 3) LCD uses all unique factors\n( x − 1)( x + 3)( x − 4) Our Solution\nNotice we only used ( x + 3) once in our LCD. This is because it only appears as a\nfactor once in either polynomial. The only time we need to rep eat a factor or use\nan exponent on a factor is if there are exponents when one of th e polynomials is\nfactored\n253\nExample 341. \nFind the LCD of x2 − 10x + 25 and x2 − 14x + 45\nFactor each polynomial\n( x − 5) 2 and ( x − 5)( x − 9) LCD uses all unique factors with highest exponent\n( x − 5) 2 ( x − 9) Our Solution\nThe previous example could have also been done with factorin g the ﬁrst polyno-\nmial to ( x − 5)( x − 5) . Then we would have used ( x − 5) twice in the LCD\nbecause it showed up twice in one of the polynomials. However , it is the author’s\nsuggestion to use the exponents in factored form so as to use t he same pattern\n(highest exponent) as used with monomials. \nOnce we know the LCD, our goal will be to build up fractions so t hey have\nmatching denominators. In this lesson we will not be adding a nd subtracting frac-\ntions, just building them up to a common denominator. We can b uild up a frac-\ntion’s denominator by multipliplying the numerator and den oinator by any factors\nthat are not already in the denominator. \nExample 342. \n5 a\n3a2 b\n= ? \n6a5b3 Idenﬁty what factors we need to match denominators\n2 a3b2 3 · 2 = 6and we need three morea′s and two moreb′s \n5 a\n3a2 b\n( 2 a3b2 \n2 a3b2 \n)\nMultiply numerator and denominator by this\n10a4b2 \n6a5b3 Our Solution\nExample 343. \nx − 2 \nx + 4 = ? \nx2 + 7x + 12 Factor to idenﬁty factors we need to match denominators\n( x + 4)( x + 3) \n( x + 3) The missing factor\nx − 2 \nx + 4\n( x + 3\nx + 3\n)\nMultiply numerator and denominator by missing factor, \nFOIL numerator\nx2 + x − 6\n( x + 4)( x + 3) Our Solution\n254"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_44"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Least Common Denominator well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [],
          "concepts": [],

        {
          "id": "SEC_45",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.4",
          "title": "Add and Subtract",
          "order": 44,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 257,
              "page_end": 257,
              "text": "7.4\nRational Expressions - Add & Subtract\nObjective: Add and subtract rational expressions with and w ithout\ncommon denominators. \nAdding and subtracting rational expressions is identical t o adding and subtracting\nwith integers. Recall that when adding with a common denomin ator we add the\nnumerators and keep the denominator. This is the same proces s used with\nrational expressions. Remember to reduce, if possible, you r ﬁnal answer. \nExample 346. \nx − 4\nx2 − 2 x − 8\n+ x + 8\nx2 − 2 x − 8 Same denominator, add numerators, combine like terms\n2 x + 4\nx2 − 2 x − 8 Factor numerator and denominator\n2( x + 2) \n( x + 2)( x − 4) Divide out ( x + 2) \n2 \nx − 4 Our Solution\n257\nSubtraction with common denominator follows the same patte rn, though the sub-\ntraction can cause problems if we are not careful with it. To a void sign errors we\nwill ﬁrst distribute the subtraction through the numerator . Then we can treat it\nlike an addition problem. This process is the same as “add the opposite” we saw\nwhen subtracting with negatives. \nExample 347. \n6x − 12\n3x − 6\n− 15x − 6\n3x − 6 Add the opposite of the second fraction( distribute negative) \n6x − 12\n3x − 6 + − 15x + 6\n3x − 6 Add numerators, combine like terms\n− 9x − 6\n3x − 6 Factor numerator and denominator\n− 3(3x + 2) \n3( x − 2) Divide out common factor of3\n− (3x + 2) \nx − 2 Our Solution\nWorld View Note: The Rhind papyrus of Egypt from 1650 BC gives some of\nthe earliest known symbols for addition and subtraction, a p air of legs walking in\nthe direction one reads for addition, and a pair of legs walki ng in the opposite\ndirection for subtraction.. \nWhen we don’t have a common denominator we will have to ﬁnd the least\ncommon denominator (LCD) and build up each fraction so the de nominators\nmatch. The following example shows this process with intege rs. \nExample 348. \n5 \n6\n+ 1 \n4\nThe LCD is 12. Build up, multiply 6 by 2 and 4 by 3\n( 2 \n2 \n) 5 \n6\n+ 1 \n4\n( 3\n3\n)\nMultiply\n10\n12\n+ 3\n12 Add numerators\n258"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_45"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Add and Subtract well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [],
