        {
          "id": "SEC_46",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.5",
          "title": "Complex Fractions",
          "order": 45,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 262,
              "page_end": 262,
              "text": "7.5\nRational Expressions - Complex Fractions\nObjective: Simplify complex fractions by multiplying each term by the\nleast common denominator. \nComplex fractions have fractions in either the numerator, o r denominator, or usu-\nally both. These fractions can be simpliﬁed in one of two ways . This will be illus-\ntrated ﬁrst with integers, then we will consider how the proc ess can be expanded\nto include expressions with variables. \nThe ﬁrst method uses order of operations to simplify the nume rator and denomi-\nnator ﬁrst, then divide the two resulting fractions by multi plying by the recip-\nrocal. \nExample 353. \n2 \n3\n− 1 \n4\n5\n6\n+ 1 \n2 \nGet common denominator in top and bottom fractions\n8\n12 − 3\n12 \n5\n6\n+ 3\n6\nAdd and subtract fractions, reducing solutions\n5\n12 \n4\n3\nTo divide fractions we multiply by the reciprocal\n( 5 \n12\n)( 3\n4\n)\nReduce\n( 5 \n4\n)( 1 \n4\n)\nMultiply\n5 \n16 Our Solution\nThe process above works just ﬁne to simplify, but between get ting common\ndenominators, taking reciprocals, and reducing, it can be a very involved process. \nGenerally we prefer a diﬀerent method, to multiply the numer ator and denomi-\nnator of the large fraction (in eﬀect each term in the complex fraction) by the\nleast common denominator (LCD). This will allow us to reduce and clear the\nsmall fractions. We will simplify the same problem using thi s second method. \nExample 354. \n2 \n3\n− 1 \n4\n5\n6\n+ 1 \n2 \nLCD is 12, multiply each term\n262\n2( 12 ) \n3 − 1( 12 ) \n4\n5( 12 ) \n6 + 1( 12 ) \n2 \nReduce each fraction\n2(4) − 1(3) \n5(2) + 1(6) Multiply\n8 − 3\n10 + 6 Add and subtract\n5 \n16 Our Solution\nClearly the second method is a much cleaner and faster method to arrive at our\nsolution. It is the method we will use when simplifying with v ariables as well. We\nwill ﬁrst ﬁnd the LCD of the small fractions, and multiply eac h term by this LCD\nso we can clear the small fractions and simplify. \nExample 355. \n1 − 1 \nx2 \n1 − 1 \nx\nIdentify LCD ( use highest exponent) \nLCD = x2 Multiply each term by LCD\n1( x2 ) − 1( x2 ) \nx2 \n1( x2 ) − 1( x2 ) \nx\nReduce fractions ( subtract exponents) \n1( x2 ) − 1 \n1( x2 ) − x Multiply\nx2 − 1 \nx2 − x Factor\n( x + 1)( x − 1) \nx( x − 1) Divide out ( x − 1) factor\nx + 1 \nx Our Solution\nThe process is the same if the LCD is a binomial, we will need to distribute\n3\nx + 4 − 2 \n5 + 2 \nx + 4\nMultiply each term by LCD, ( x + 4) \n263"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_46"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Complex Fractions well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [],
          "concepts": [],
          "lesson_blocks": [],

        {
          "id": "SEC_47",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.6",
          "title": "Proportions",
          "order": 46,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 268,
              "page_end": 268,
              "text": "7.6\nRational Expressions - Proportions\nObjective: Solve proportions using the cross product and us e propor-\ntions to solve application problems\nWhen two fractions are equal, they are called a proportion. T his deﬁnition can be\ngeneralized to two equal rational expressions. Proportion s have an important\nproperty called the cross-product. \nCross Product: If a\nb\n= c\nd then ad = bc\nThe cross product tells us we can multiply diagonally to get a n equation with no\nfractions that we can solve. \nExample 359. \n20\n6\n= x\n9 Calculate cross product\n268\n( 20)(9) = 6x Multiply\n180 = 6x Divide both sides by6\n6 6\n30 = x Our Solution\nWorld View Note: The ﬁrst clear deﬁnition of a proportion and the notation\nfor a proportion came from the German Leibniz who wrote, “I wr ite dy : x = dt: a; \nfor dy is to x as dt is to a, is indeed the same as, dy divided by x is equal to dt\ndivided by a. From this equation follow then all the rules of proportion. ”\nIf the proportion has more than one term in either numerator o r denominator, we\nwill have to distribute while calculating the cross product . \nExample 360. \nx + 3\n4 = 2 \n5 \nCalculate cross product\n5( x + 3) = (4)(2) Multiply and distribute\n5 x + 15 = 8 Solve\n− 15 − 15 Subtract 15 from both sides\n5 x = − 7 Divide both sides by5 \n5 5 \nx = − 7\n5 \nOur Solution\nThis same idea can be seen when the variable appears in severa l parts of the pro-\nportion. \nExample 361. \n4\nx\n= 6\n3x + 2 Calculate cross product\n4(3x + 2) = 6x Distribute\n12x + 8 = 6x Move variables to one side\n− 12x − 12x Subtract 12x from both sides\n8 = − 6x Divide both sides by− 6\n− 6 − 6\n− 4\n3\n= x Our Solution\n269"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_47"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Proportions well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [],
          "concepts": [],
          "lesson_blocks": [],
          "worked_examples": [],

          "mastery_quiz": [],

        {
          "id": "SEC_48",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.7",
          "title": "Solving Rational Equations",
          "order": 47,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 274,
              "page_end": 274,
              "text": "7.7\nRational Expressions - Solving Rational Equations\nObjective: Solve rational equations by identifying and mul tiplying by\nthe least common denominator. \nWhen solving equations that are made up of rational expressi ons we will solve\nthem using the same strategy we used to solve linear equation s with fractions. \nWhen we solved problems like the next example, we cleared the fraction by multi-\nplying by the least common denominator (LCD) \nExample 366. \n2 \n3\nx − 5 \n6\n= 3\n4\nMultiply each term by LCD, 12\n2( 12) \n3 x − 5( 12) \n6 = 3( 12) \n4 Reduce fractions\n2(4) x − 5(2) = 3(3) Multiply\n8x − 10 = 9 Solve\n+ 10 + 10 Add 10 to both sides\n8x = 19 Divide both sides by 8\n8 8\nx = 19\n8 Our Solution\nWe will use the same process to solve rational equations, the only diﬀerence is our\n274\nLCD will be more involved. We will also have to be aware of doma in issues. If our\nLCD equals zero, the solution is undeﬁned. We will always che ck our solutions in\nthe LCD as we may have to remove a solution from our solution se t. \nExample 367. \n5 x + 5 \nx + 2 + 3x = x2 \nx + 2 Multiply each term by LCD, ( x + 2) \n(5 x + 5)( x + 2) \nx + 2 + 3x( x + 2) = x2 ( x + 2) \nx + 2 Reduce fractions\n5 x + 5 + 3x( x + 2) = x2 Distribute\n5 x + 5 + 3x2 + 6x = x2 Combine like terms\n3x2 + 11 x + 5 = x2 Make equation equal zero\n− x2 − x2 Subtract x2 from both sides\n2 x2 + 11 x + 5 = 0 Factor\n(2 x + 1)( x + 5) = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n2 x + 1 = 0 or x + 5 = 0 Solve each equation\n− 1 − 1 − 5 − 5 \n2 x = − 1 or x = − 5 \n2 2 \nx = − 1 \n2 \nor − 5 Check solutions, LCD can′t be zero\n− 1 \n2 \n+ 2 = 3\n2 \n− 5 + 2 = − 3 Neither make LCD zero, both are solutions\nx = − 1 \n2 \nor − 5 Our Solution\nThe LCD can be several factors in these problems. As the LCD ge ts more com-\nplex, it is important to remember the process we are using to s olve is still the\nsame. \nExample 368. \nx\nx + 2 \n+ 1 \nx + 1 = 5 \n( x + 1)( x + 2) Multiply terms by LCD, ( x + 1)( x + 2) \nx( x + 1)( x + 2) \nx + 2 + 1( x + 1)( x + 2) \nx + 1 = 5( x + 1)( x + 2) \n( x + 1)( x + 2) Reduce fractions\n275"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_48"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Solving Rational Equations well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [],
          "concepts": [],
          "lesson_blocks": [],
          "worked_examples": [],

          "mastery_quiz": [],

        {
          "id": "SEC_49",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.8",
          "title": "Application: Dimensional Analysis",
          "order": 48,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 279,
              "page_end": 279,
              "text": "7.8\nRational Expressions - Dimensional Analysis\nObjective: Use dimensional analysis to preform single unit , dual unit, \nsquare unit, and cubed unit conversions. \nOne application of rational expressions deals with convert ing units. When we con-\nvert units of measure we can do so by multiplying several frac tions together in a\nprocess known as dimensional analysis. The trick will be to d ecide what fractions\nto multiply. When multiplying, if we multiply by 1, the value of the expression\ndoes not change. One written as a fraction can look like many d iﬀerent things as\nlong as the numerator and denominator are identical in value . Notice the numer-\nator and denominator are not identical in appearance, but ra ther identical in\nvalue. Below are several fractions, each equal to one where n umerator and denom-\ninator are identical in value. \n1 \n1 \n= 4\n4\n= \n1 \n2 \n2 \n4\n= 100cm\n1 m = 1 lb\n16oz = 1 hr\n60 min = 60 min\n1 hr\nThe last few fractions that include units are called convers ion factors. We can\nmake a conversion factor out of any two measurements that rep resent the same\ndistance. For example, 1 mile = 5280 feet. We could then make a conversion\nfactor 1 mi\n5280ft because both values are the same, the fraction is still equal to one. \nSimilarly we could make a conversion factor 5280ft\n1 mi . The trick for conversions will\n279\nbe to use the correct fractions. \nThe idea behind dimensional analysis is we will multiply by a fraction in such a\nway that the units we don’t want will divide out of the problem . We found out\nwhen multiplying rational expressions that if a variable ap pears in the numerator\nand denominator we can divide it out of the expression. It is t he same with units. \nConsider the following conversion. \nExample 371. \n17.37 miles to feet Write 17.37 miles as a fraction, put it over1 ( 17.37mi\n1 \n)\nTo divide out the miles we need miles in the denominator\n( 17.37mi\n1 \n)( ?? ft\n?? mi\n)\nWe are converting to feet, so this will go in the numerator\n( 17.37mi\n1 \n)( 5280ft\n1 mi\n)\nFill in the relationship described above, 1 mile = 5280 feet\n( 17.37\n1 \n)( 5280ft\n1 \n)\nDivide out the miles and multiply across\n91 , 713.6ft Our Solution\nIn the previous example, we had to use the conversion factor 5280ft\n1 mi so the miles\nwould divide out. If we had used 1 mi\n5280ft we would not have been able to divide out\nthe miles. This is why when doing dimensional analysis it is v ery important to\nuse units in the set-up of the problem, so we know how to correc tly set up the\nconversion factor. \nExample 372. \nIf 1 pound = 16 ounces, how many pounds 435 ounces? \n( 435oz\n1 \n)\nWrite 435 asa fraction, put it over1 \n( 435oz\n1 \n)( ?? lbs\n?? oz\n)\nTo divide out oz, \nput it in the denominator and lbs in numerator( 435oz\n1 \n)( 1 lbs\n16oz\n)\nFill in the given relationship, 1 pound = 16 ounces\n280"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_49"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Dimensional Analysis well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

    {
      "id": "CH_9",
      "number": "8",
      "title": "Radicals",
      "order": 8,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_50",
          "chapter_id": "CH_9",
          "number": "8.1",
          "title": "Square Roots",
          "order": 49,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 288,
              "page_end": 288,
              "text": "8.1 \nRadicals - Square Roots\nObjective: Simplify expressions with square roots. \nSquare roots are the most common type of radical used. A squar e root “un-\nsquares” a number. For example, because 5 2 = 25 we say the square root of 25 is 5. \nThe square root of 25 is written as 25\n√\n. \nWorld View Note: The radical sign, when ﬁrst used was an R with a line\nthrough the tail, similar to our perscription symbol today. The R came from the\nlatin, “radix”, which can be translated as “source” or “foun dation”. It wasn’t until\nthe 1500s that our current symbol was ﬁrst used in Germany (bu t even then it\nwas just a check mark with no bar over the numbers! \nThe following example gives several square roots: \nExample 377. \n1 \n√\n= 1 121 \n√\n= 11 \n4\n√\n= 2 625\n√\n= 25\n9\n√\n= 3 − 81 √ = Undeﬁned\nThe ﬁnal example, − 81 √ is currently undeﬁned as negatives have no square root. \nThis is because if we square a positive or a negative, the answ er will be positive. \nThus we can only take square roots of positive numbers. In ano ther lesson we will\ndeﬁne a method we can use to work with and evaluate negative sq uare roots, but\nfor now we will simply say they are undeﬁned. \nNot all numbers have a nice even square root. For example, if w e found 8\n√\non\nour calculator, the answer would be 2.8284271247461900976 03377448419... and\neven this number is a rounded approximation of the square roo t. To be as accu-\nrate as possible, we will never use the calculator to ﬁnd deci mal approximations of\nsquare roots. Instead we will express roots in simplest radi cal form. We will do\nthis using a property known as the product rule of radicals\nProduct Rule of Square Roots: a · b\n√\n= a√ · b\n√\nWe can use the product rule to simplify an expression such as 3 6 · 5 \n√\nby spliting\nit into two roots, 36\n√\n· 5 \n√\n, and simplifying the ﬁrst root, 6 5 \n√\n. The trick in this\n288\nprocess is being able to translate a problem like 180\n√\ninto 36 · 5 \n√\n. There are sev-\neral ways this can be done. The most common and, with a bit of pr actice, the\nfastest method, is to ﬁnd perfect squares that divide evenly into the radicand, or\nnumber under the radical. This is shown in the next example. \nExample 378. \n75\n√\n75 is divisible by 25, a perfect square\n25 · 3\n√\nSplit into factors\n25\n√\n· 3\n√\nProduct rule, take the square root of 25\n5 3\n√\nOur Solution\nIf there is a coeﬃcient in front of the radical to begin with, t he problem merely\nbecomes a big multiplication problem. \nExample 379. \n5 63\n√\n63 is divisible by9, a perfect square\n5 9 · 7\n√\nSplit into factors\n5 9\n√\n· 7\n√\nProduct rule, take the square root of9\n5 · 3 7\n√\nMultiply coeﬃcients\n15 7\n√\nOur Solution\nAs we simplify radicals using this method it is important to b e sure our ﬁnal\nanswer can be simpliﬁed no more. \nExample 380. \n72\n√\n72 is divisible by9, a perfect square\n9 · 8\n√\nSplit into factors\n9\n√\n· 8\n√\nProduct rule, take the square root of9\n3 8\n√\nBut 8 is also divisible bya perfect square, 4\n3 4 · 2 \n√\nSplit into factors\n3 4\n√\n· 2 \n√\nProduct rule, take the square root of4\n3 · 2 2 \n√\nMultiply\n289"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_50"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Square Roots well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_51",
          "chapter_id": "CH_9",
          "number": "8.2",
          "title": "Higher Roots",
          "order": 50,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 292,
              "page_end": 292,
              "text": "8.2\nRadicals - Higher Roots\nObjective: Simplify radicals with an index greater than two . \nWhile square roots are the most common type of radical we work with, we can\ntake higher roots of numbers as well: cube roots, fourth root s, ﬁfth roots, etc. Fol-\nlowing is a deﬁnition of radicals. \nam√ = b if bm = a\nThe small letter m inside the radical is called the index. It tells us which root we\nare taking, or which power we are “un-doing”. For square root s the index is 2. As\nthis is the most common root, the two is not usually written. \nWorld View Note: The word for root comes from the French mathematician\nFranciscus Vieta in the late 16th century. \nThe following example includes several higher roots. \nExample 383. \n1253√\n= 5 − 643√ = − 4\n81 4√\n= 3 − 1287√\n= − 2 \n325√\n= 2 − 164√ = undeﬁned\nWe must be careful of a few things as we work with higher roots. First its impor-\ntant not to forget to check the index on the root. 81 \n√\n= 9 but 81 4√\n= 3. This is\nbecause 92 = 81 and 34 = 81. Another thing to watch out for is negatives under\nroots. We can take an odd root of a negative number, because a n egative number\nraised to an odd power is still negative. However, we cannot t ake an even root of\na negative number, this we will say is undeﬁned. In a later sec tion we will discuss\nhow to work with roots of negative, but for now we will simply s ay they are unde-\nﬁned. \nWe can simplify higher roots in much the same way we simpliﬁed square roots, \nusing the product property of radicals. \nProduct Property of Radicals: a bm√\n= am√ · bm√\nOften we are not as familiar with higher powers as we are with s quares. It is\nimportant to remember what index we are working with as we try and work our\nway to the solution. \n292\nExample 384. \n543√\nWe are working witha cubed root, want third powers\n2 3 = 8 Test2 , 2 3 = 8, 54 is not divisible by8. \n33 = 27 Test 3, 33 = 27, 54 is divisible by 27! \n27 · 2 3√\nWrite as factors\n273√\n· 2 3√\nProduct rule, take cubed root of 27\n3 2 3√\nOur Solution\nJust as with square roots, if we have a coeﬃcient, we multiply the new coeﬃcients\ntogether. \nExample 385. \n3 484√\nWe are working witha fourth root, want fourth powers\n2 4 = 16 Test 2 , 2 4 = 16, 48 is divisible by 16! \n3 16 · 34√\nWrite as factors\n3 164√\n· 34√\nProduct rule, take fourth root of 16\n3 · 2 34√\nMultiply coeﬃcients\n6 34√\nOur Solution\nWe can also take higher roots of variables. As we do, we will di vide the exponent\non the variable by the index. Any whole answer is how many of th at varible will\ncome out of the square root. Any remainder is how many are left behind inside\nthe square root. This is shown in the following examples. \nExample 386. \nx25 y17z35√\nDivide each exponent by5 , whole number outside, remainder inside\nx5y3 y2 z35√\nOur Solution\nIn the previous example, for the x, we divided 25 \n5 = 5 R 0 , so x5 came out, no x’s\nremain inside. For the y, we divided 17\n5 = 3 R 2 , so y3 came out, and y2 remains\ninside. For the z, when we divided 3\n5\n= 0 R 3, all three or z3 remained inside. The\nfollowing example includes integers in our problem. \nExample 387. \n2 40a4b83√\nLooking for cubes that divide into 40. The number 8 works! \n2 8 · 5 a4b83√\nTake cube root of8, dividing exponents on variables by3\n2 · 2 ab 2 5 ab 2 3√\nRemainders are left in radical. Multiply coeﬃcients\n4ab 2 5 ab 2 3√\nOur Solution\n293"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_51"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Higher Roots well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_52",
          "chapter_id": "CH_9",
          "number": "8.3",
          "title": "Adding Radicals",
          "order": 51,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 295,
              "page_end": 295,
              "text": "8.3\nRadicals - Adding Radicals\nObjective: Add like radicals by ﬁrst simplifying each radic al. \nAdding and subtracting radicals is very similar to adding an d subtracting with\nvariables. Consider the following example. \nExample 388. \n5 x + 3x − 2 x Combine like terms\n6x Our Solution\n5 11 \n√\n+ 3 11 \n√\n− 2 11 \n√\nCombine like terms\n6 11 \n√\nOur Solution\nNotice that when we combined the terms with 11 \n√\nit was just like combining\nterms with x. When adding and subtracting with radicals we can combine li ke\nradicals just as like terms. We add and subtract the coeﬃcien ts in front of the\n295\nradical, and the radical stays the same. This is shown in the f ollowing example. \nExample 389. \n7 65√\n+ 4 35√\n− 9 35√\n+ 65√\nCombine like radicals7 65√\n+ 65√\nand 4 35√\n− 8 35√\n8 65√\n− 5 35√\nOur Solution\nWe cannot simplify this expression any more as the radicals d o not match. Often\nproblems we solve have no like radicals, however, if we simpl ify the radicals ﬁrst\nwe may ﬁnd we do in fact have like radicals. \nExample 390. \n5 45\n√\n+ 6 18\n√\n− 2 98\n√\n+ 20\n√\nSimplify radicals, ﬁnd perfect square factors\n5 9 · 5 \n√\n+ 6 9 · 2 \n√\n− 2 49 · 2 \n√\n+ 4 · 5 \n√\nTake roots where possible\n5 · 3 5 \n√\n+ 6 · 3 2 \n√\n− 2 · 7 2 \n√\n+ 2 5 \n√\nMultiply coeﬃcients\n15 5 \n√\n+ 18 2 \n√\n− 14 2 \n√\n+ 2 5 \n√\nCombine like terms\n17 5 \n√\n+ 4 2 \n√\nOur Solution\nWorld View Note: The Arab writers of the 16th century used the symbol sim-\nilar to the greater than symbol with a dot underneath for radi cals. \nThis exact process can be used to add and subtract radicals wi th higher indices\nExample 391. \n4 543√\n− 9 163√\n+ 5 93√\nSimplify each radical, ﬁnding perfect cube factors\n4 27 · 2 3√\n− 9 8 · 2 3√\n+ 5 93√\nTake roots where possible\n4 · 3 2 3√\n− 9 · 2 2 3√\n+ 5 93√\nMultiply coeﬃcients\n12 2 3√\n− 18 2 3√\n+ 5 93√\nCombine like terms 12 2 3√\n− 18 2 3√\n− 6 2 3√\n+ 5 93√\nOur Solution\n296"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_52"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Adding Radicals well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_53",
          "chapter_id": "CH_9",
          "number": "8.4",
          "title": "Multiply and Divide Radicals",
          "order": 52,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 298,
              "page_end": 298,
              "text": "8.4\nRadicals - Multiply and Divide Radicals\nObjective: Multiply and divide radicals using the product a nd quotient\nrules of radicals. \nMultiplying radicals is very simple if the index on all the ra dicals match. The\nprodcut rule of radicals which we have already been using can be generalized as\nfollows: \nProduct Rule of Radicals: a bm√\n· c dm√\n= ac bdm√\nAnother way of stating this rule is we are allowed to multiply the factors outside\nthe radical and we are allowed to multiply the factors inside the radicals, as long\nas the index matches. This is shown in the following example.\nExample 392. \n− 5 14\n√\n· 4 6\n√\nMultiply outside and inside the radical\n− 20 84\n√\nSimplify the radical, divisible by 4\n− 20 4 · 21 \n√\nTake the square root where possible\n− 20 · 2 21 \n√\nMultiply coeﬃcients\n− 40 21 \n√\nOur Solution\nThe same process works with higher roots\nExample 393. \n2 183√\n· 6 153√\nMultiply outside and inside the radical\n12 2703√\nSimplify the radical, divisible by 27\n12 27 · 103√\nTake cube root where possible\n12 · 3 103√\nMultiply coeﬃcients\n36 103√\nOur Solution\nWhen multiplying with radicals we can still use the distribu tive property or FOIL\njust as we could with variables. \nExample 394. \n7 6\n√\n(3 10\n√\n− 5 15\n√\n) Distribute, following rules for multiplying radicals\n21 60\n√\n− 35 90\n√\nSimplify each radical, ﬁnding perfect square factors\n21 4 · 15\n√\n− 35 9 · 10\n√\nTake square root where possible\n21 · 2 15\n√\n− 35 · 3 10\n√\nMultiply coeﬃcients\n42 15\n√\n− 105 10\n√\nOur Solution\nExample 395. \n( 5 \n√\n− 2 3\n√\n)(4 10\n√\n+ 6 6\n√\n) FOIL, following rules for multiplying radicals\n298\n4 50\n√\n+ 6 30\n√\n− 8 30\n√\n− 12 18\n√\nSimplify radicals, ﬁnd perfect square factors\n4 25 · 2 \n√\n+ 6 30\n√\n− 8 30\n√\n− 12 9 · 2 \n√\nTake square root where possible\n4 · 5 2 \n√\n+ 6 30\n√\n− 8 30\n√\n− 12 · 3 2 \n√\nMultiply coeﬃcients\n20 2 \n√\n+ 6 30\n√\n− 8 30\n√\n− 36 2 \n√\nCombine like terms\n− 16 2 \n√\n− 2 30\n√\nOur Solution\nWorld View Note: Clay tablets have been discovered revealing much about\nBabylonian mathematics dating back from 1800 to 1600 BC. In o ne of the tables\nthere is an approximation of 2 \n√\naccurate to ﬁve decimal places (1.41421) \nExample 396. \n(2 5 \n√\n− 3 6\n√\n)(7 2 \n√\n− 8 7\n√\n) FOIL, following rules for multiplying radicals\n14 10\n√\n− 16 35\n√\n− 21 12\n√\n− 24 42\n√\nSimplify radicals, ﬁnd perfect square factors\n14 10\n√\n− 16 35\n√\n− 21 4 · 3\n√\n− 24 42\n√\nTake square root where possible\n14 10\n√\n− 16 35\n√\n− 21 · 2 3\n√\n− 24 42\n√\nMultiply coeﬃcient\n14 10\n√\n− 16 35\n√\n− 42 3\n√\n− 24 42\n√\nOur Solution\nAs we are multiplying we always look at our ﬁnal solution to ch eck if all the radi-\ncals are simpliﬁed and all like radicals or like terms have be en combined. \nDivision with radicals is very similar to multiplication, i f we think about division\nas reducing fractions, we can reduce the coeﬃcients outside the radicals and\nreduce the values inside the radicals to get our ﬁnal solutio n. \nQuotient Rule of Radicals: a bm√\nc dm√ = a\nc\nb\nd\nm\n√\nExample 397. \n15 1083√\n20 2 3√ Reduce 15\n20\nand 1083√\n2 \n√ by dividing by5 and 2 respectively\n3 543√\n4 Simplify radical, 54 is divisible by 27\n3 27 · 2 3√\n4 Take the cube root of 27\n3 · 3 2 3√\n4 Multiply coeﬃcients\n9 2 3√\n4 Our Solution\nThere is one catch to dividing with radicals, it is considere d bad practice to have\na radical in the denominator of our ﬁnal answer. If there is a r adical in the\ndenominator we will rationalize it, or clear out any radical s in the denominator. \n299"
            }
          ],

            "COMP_53"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Multiply and Divide Radicals well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_54",
          "chapter_id": "CH_9",
          "number": "8.5",
          "title": "Rationalize Denominators",
          "order": 53,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 303,
              "page_end": 303,
              "text": "8.5\nRadicals - Rationalize Denominators\nObjective: Rationalize the denominators of radical expres sions. \nIt is considered bad practice to have a radical in the denomin ator of a fraction. \nWhen this happens we multiply the numerator and denominator by the same\nthing in order to clear the radical. In the lesson on dividing radicals we talked\nabout how this was done with monomials. Here we will look at ho w this is done\nwith binomials. \nIf the binomial is in the numerator the process to rationaliz e the denominator is\nessentially the same as with monomials. The only diﬀerence i s we will have to dis-\ntribute in the numerator. \nExample 403. \n3\n√\n− 9\n2 6\n√ Want to clear 6\n√\nin denominator, multiply by 6\n√\n( 3\n√\n− 9) \n2 6\n√\n(\n6\n√\n6\n√\n)\nWe will distribute the 6\n√\nthrough the numerator\n303\n18\n√\n− 9 6\n√\n2 · 6 Simplify radicals in numerator, multiply out denominator\n9 · 2 \n√\n− 9 6\n√\n12 Take square root where possible\n3 2 \n√\n− 9 6\n√\n12 Reduce by dividing each term by3\n2 \n√\n− 3 6\n√\n4 Our Solution\nIt is important to remember that when reducing the fraction w e cannot reduce\nwith just the 3 and 12 or just the 9 and 12. When we have addition or subtrac-\ntion in the numerator or denominator we must divide all terms by the same\nnumber. \nThe problem can often be made easier if we ﬁrst simplify any ra dicals in the\nproblem. \n2 20x5\n√\n− 12x2 \n√\n18x\n√ Simplify radicals by ﬁnding perfect squares\n2 4 · 5 x3\n√\n− 4 · 3x2 \n√\n9 · 2 x\n√ Simplify roots, divide exponents by2 . \n2 · 2 x2 5 x\n√\n− 2 x 3\n√\n3 2 x\n√ Multiply coeﬃcients\n4x2 5 x\n√\n− 2 x 3\n√\n3 2 x\n√ Multiplying numerator and denominator by 2 x\n√\n(4x2 5 x\n√\n− 2 x 3\n√\n) \n3 2 x\n√\n(\n2 x\n√\n2 x\n√\n)\nDistribute through numerator\n4x2 10x2 \n√\n− 2 x 6x\n√\n3 · 2 x\nSimplify roots in numerator, \nmultiply coeﬃcients in denominator\n4x3 10\n√\n− 2 x 6x\n√\n6x Reduce, dividing each term by2 x\n304"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_54"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Rationalize Denominators well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_55",
          "chapter_id": "CH_9",
          "number": "8.6",
          "title": "Rational Exponents",
          "order": 54,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 310,
              "page_end": 310,
              "text": "8.6\nRadicals - Rational Exponents\nObjective: Convert between radical notation and exponenti al notation\nand simplify expressions with rational exponents using the properties\nof exponents. \nWhen we simplify radicals with exponents, we divide the expo nent by the index. \nAnother way to write division is with a fraction bar. This ide a is how we will\ndeﬁne rational exponents. \nDeﬁnition of Rational Exponents: a\nn \nm \n= ( am√ ) n\nThe denominator of a rational exponent becomes the index on o ur radical, like-\nwise the index on the radical becomes the denominator of the e xponent. We can\nuse this property to change any radical expression into an ex ponential expression. \nExample 410. \n( x5√\n) 3 = x\n3 \n5 \n( 3 x6√\n) 5 = (3x) \n5 \n6 \n1 \n( a7√ ) 3 = a− 3 \n7\n1 \n( xy3√ ) 2 = ( xy ) − 2 \n3 \nIndex is denominator\nNegative exponents from reciprocals\nWe can also change any rational exponent into a radical expre ssion by using the\ndenominator as the index. \nExample 411. \na\n5 \n3 \n= ( a3√ ) 5 (2 mn) \n2 \n7\n= ( 2 mn7√\n) 2 \nx− 4\n5 \n= 1 \n( x5√ ) 4 ( xy ) − 2 \n9 \n= 1 \n( xy9√ ) 2 \nIndex is denominator\nNegative exponent means reciprocals\nWorld View Note: Nicole Oresme, a Mathematician born in Normandy was the\nﬁrst to use rational exponents. He used the notation 1 \n3\n• 9p to represent 9\n1 \n3 \n. How-\never his notation went largely unnoticed. \nThe ability to change between exponential expressions and r adical expressions\nallows us to evaluate problems we had no means of evaluating b efore by changing\nto a radical. \nExample 412. \n27− 4\n3 \nChange to radical, denominator is index, negative means reciprocal\n1 \n( 273√\n) 4\nEvaluate radical\n310\n1 \n(3) 4 Evaluate exponent\n1 \n81 Our solution\nThe largest advantage of being able to change a radical expre ssion into an expo-\nnential expression is we are now allowed to use all our expone nt properties to sim-\nplify. The following table reviews all of our exponent prope rties. \nProperties of Exponents\naman = am+ n ( ab) m = ambm a− m = 1 \nam\nam\nan = am− n\n( a\nb\n) m\n= am\nbm\n1 \na− m = am\n( am) n = am n a0 = 1 \n( a\nb\n) − m\n= bm\nam\nWhen adding and subtracting with fractions we need to be sure to have a\ncommon denominator. When multiplying we only need to multip ly the numera-\ntors together and denominators together. The following exa mples show several\ndiﬀerent problems, using diﬀerent properties to simplify t he rational exponents. \nExample 413. \na\n2 \n3 \nb\n1 \n2 \na\n1 \n6 \nb\n1 \n5 \nNeed common denominator ona′s (6) and b′s ( 10) \na\n4\n6 \nb\n5 \n10 a\n1 \n6 \nb\n2 \n10 Add exponents ona′s and b′s \na\n5 \n6 \nb\n7\n10 Our Solution\nExample 414. \n(\nx\n1 \n3 \ny\n2 \n5 \n) 3 \n4\nMultiply 3\n4\nby each exponent\nx\n1 \n4\ny\n3 \n10 Our Solution\nExample 415. \nx2 y\n2 \n3 \n· 2 x\n1 \n2 \ny\n5 \n6 \nx\n7\n2 \ny0\nIn numerator, need common denominator to add exponents\n311"
            }
          ],

            "COMP_55"
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_56",
          "chapter_id": "CH_9",
          "number": "8.7",
          "title": "Radicals of Mixed Index",
          "order": 55,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 314,
              "page_end": 314,
              "text": "8.7\nRadicals - Radicals of Mixed Index\nObjective: Reduce the index on a radical and multiply or divi de radi-\ncals of diﬀerent index. \nKnowing that a radical has the same properties as exponents ( written as a ratio) \nallows us to manipulate radicals in new ways. One thing we are allowed to do is\nreduce, not just the radicand, but the index as well. This is s hown in the fol-\nlowing example. \nExample 417. \nx6y2 8√\nRewrite as rational exponent\n( x6y2 ) \n1 \n5 \nMultiply exponents\nx\n6 \n8 \ny\n2 \n8 \nReduce each fraction\nx\n3 \n4\ny\n1 \n4\nAll exponents have denominator of4, this is our new index\nx3y4√\nOur Solution\nWhat we have done is reduced our index by dividing the index an d all the expo-\nnents by the same number (2 in the previous example). If we not ice a common\nfactor in the index and all the exponnets on every factor we ca n reduce by\ndividing by that common factor. This is shown in the next exam ple\nExample 418. \na6b9c15 24√\nIndex and all exponents are divisible by3\na2 b3c58√\nOur Solution\nWe can use the same process when there are coeﬃcients in the pr oblem. We will\nﬁrst write the coeﬃcient as an exponential expression so we c an divide the\nexponet by the common factor as well. \nExample 419. \n8m6n39√\nWrite8 as 2 3\n2 3m6n39√\nIndex and all exponents are divisible by3\n2 m2 n\n3√\nOur Solution\nWe can use a very similar idea to also multiply radicals where the index does not\nmatch. First we will consider an example using rational expo nents, then identify\nthe pattern we can use. \n314\nExample 420. \nab 2 3√\na2 b\n4√\nRewrite as rational exponents\n( ab 2 ) \n1 \n3 \n( a2 b) \n1 \n4\nMultiply exponents\na\n1 \n3 \nb\n2 \n3 \na\n2 \n4\nb\n1 \n4\nTo have one radical needa common denominator, 12\na\n4\n12 b\n8 \n12 a\n6 \n12 b\n3 \n12 Write undera single radical with common index, 12\na4b8a6b312 √\nAdd exponents\na10b11 12 √\nOur Solution\nTo combine the radicals we need a common index (just like the c ommon denomi-\nnator). We will get a common index by multiplying each index a nd exponent by\nan integer that will allow us to build up to that desired index . This process is\nshown in the next example. \nExample 421. \na2 b34√\na2 b\n6√\nCommon index is 12. \nMultiply ﬁrst index and exponents by3, second by 2 \na6b9a4b2 12 √\nAdd exponents\na10b11 12 √\nOur Solution\nOften after combining radicals of mixed index we will need to simplify the\nresulting radical. \nExample 422. \nx3y45√\nx2 y3√\nCommon index: 15. \nMultiply ﬁrst index and exponents by3, second by 5 \nx9y12 x10y515 √\nAdd exponents\nx19y1715 √\nSimplify by dividing exponents by index, remainder is left inside\nxy x 4y2 15 √\nOur Solution\nJust as with reducing the index, we will rewrite coeﬃcients a s exponential expres-\nsions. This will also allow us to use exponent properties to s implify. \nExample 423. \n4x2 y3√\n8xy 34√\nRewrite 4 as 2 2 and 8 as 2 3\n2 2 x2 y3√\n2 3xy 34√\nCommon index: 12. \nMultiply ﬁrst index and exponents by4, second by 3\n2 8x8y42 9x3y912 √\nAdd exponents ( even on the2) \n2 17x11 y1312 √\nSimplify by dividing exponents by index, remainder is left inside\n2 y 2 5x11 y12 √\nSimplify 2 5\n2 y 32x11 y12 √\nOur Solution\n315"
            }
          ],

          "order": 56,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 318,
              "page_end": 318,
              "text": "8.8\nRadicals - Complex Numbers\nObjective: Add, subtract, multiply, rationalize, and simp lify expres-\nsions using complex numbers. \nWorld View Note: When mathematics was ﬁrst used, the primary purpose was\nfor counting. Thus they did not originally use negatives, ze ro, fractions or irra-\ntional numbers. However, the ancient Egyptians quickly dev eloped the need for “a\npart” and so they made up a new type of number, the ratio or frac tion. The\nAncient Greeks did not believe in irrational numbers (peopl e were killed for\nbelieving otherwise). The Mayans of Central America later m ade up the number\nzero when they found use for it as a placeholder. Ancient Chin ese Mathematicians\nmade up negative numbers when they found use for them. \nIn mathematics, when the current number system does not prov ide the tools to\nsolve the problems the culture is working with, we tend to mak e up new ways for\ndealing with the problem that can solve the problem. Through out history this has\nbeen the case with the need for a number that is nothing (0), sm aller than zero\n(negatives), between integers (fractions), and between fr actions (irrational num-\nbers). This is also the case for the square roots of negative n umbers. To work\nwith the square root of negative numbers mathematicians hav e deﬁned what are\ncalled imaginary and complex numbers. \nDeﬁnition of Imaginary Numbers: i2 = − 1 ( thus i = − 1 √\n) \nExamples of imaginary numbers include 3i, − 6i, 3\n5\ni and 3i 5 \n√\n. A complex\nnumber is one that contains both a real and imaginary part, such as 2 + 5 i. \nWith this deﬁnition, the square root of a negative number is n o longer undeﬁned. \nWe now are allowed to do basic operations with the square root of negatives. \nFirst we will consider exponents on imaginary numbers. We wi ll do this by\nmanipulating our deﬁnition of i2 = − 1 . If we multiply both sides of the deﬁnition\nby i, the equation becomes i3 = − i. Then if we multiply both sides of the equa-\ntion again by i, the equation becomes i4 = − i2 = − ( − 1) = 1 , or simply i4 = 1 . \nMultiplying again by i gives i5 = i. One more time gives i6 = i2 = − 1 . And if this\npattern continues we see a cycle forming, the exponents on i change we cycle\nthrough simpliﬁed answers of i, − 1 , − i, 1 . As there are 4 diﬀerent possible\nanswers in this cycle, if we divide the exponent by 4 and consi der the remainder, \nwe can simplify any exponent on i by learning just the following four values: \nCyclic Property of Powers of i\ni0 = 1 \ni = i\ni2 = − 1 \ni3 = − i\n318\nExample 426. \ni35 Divide exponent by4\n8R3 Use remainder as exponent oni\ni3 Simplify\n− i Our Solution\nExample 427. \ni124 Divide exponent by4\n31 R0 Use remainder as exponent oni\ni0 Simplify\n1 Our Solution\nWhen performing operations (add, subtract, multilpy, divi de) we can handle i just\nlike we handle any other variable. This means when adding and subtracting com-\nplex numbers we simply add or combine like terms. \nExample 428. \n(2 + 5 i) + (4 − 7i) Combine like terms2 + 4and 5 i − 7i\n6 − 2 i Our Solution\nIt is important to notice what operation we are doing. Studen ts often see the\nparenthesis and think that means FOIL. We only use FOIL to mul tiply. This\nproblem is an addition problem so we simply add the terms, or c ombine like\nterms. \nFor subtraction problems the idea is the same, we need to reme mber to ﬁrst dis-\ntribute the negative onto all the terms in the parentheses. \nExample 429. \n(4 − 8i) − (3 − 5 i) Distribute the negative\n4 − 8i − 3 + 5 i Combine like terms4 − 3 and − 8i + 5 i\n1 − 3i Our Solution\nAddition and subtraction can be combined into one problem. \nExample 430. \n(5 i) − (3 + 8i) + ( − 4 + 7i) Distribute the negative\n5 i − 3 − 8i − 4 + 7i Combine like terms5 i − 8i + 7i and − 3 − 4\n− 7 + 4i Our Solution\nMultiplying with complex numbers is the same as multiplying with variables with\none exception, we will want to simplify our ﬁnal answer so the re are no exponents\non i. \n319"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_57"
          ],

    {
      "id": "CH_10",
      "number": "9",
      "title": "Quadratics",
      "order": 9,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_58",
          "chapter_id": "CH_10",
          "number": "9.1",
          "title": "Solving with Radicals",
          "order": 57,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 326,
              "page_end": 326,
              "text": "9.1 \nQuadratics - Solving with Radicals\nObjective: Solve equations with radicals and check for extr aneous solu-\ntions. \nHere we look at equations that have roots in the problem. As yo u might expect, \nto clear a root we can raise both sides to an exponent. So to cle ar a square root\nwe can rise both sides to the second power. To clear a cubed roo t we can raise\nboth sides to a third power. There is one catch to solving a pro blem with roots in\nit, sometimes we end up with solutions that do not actually wo rk in the equation. \nThis will only happen if the index on the root is even, and it wi ll not happen all\nthe time. So for these problems it will be required that we che ck our answer in\nthe original problem. If a value does not work it is called an e xtraneous solution\nand not included in the ﬁnal solution. \nWhen solving a radical problem with an even index: check answ ers! \nExample 442. \n7x + 2 \n√\n= 4 Even index! We will have to check answers\n( 7 x + 2 √ ) 2 = 42 Square both sides, simplify exponents\n7x + 2 = 16 Solve\n− 2 − 2 Subtract 2 from both sides\n7x = 14 Divide both sides by 7\n7 7\nx = 2 Need to check answer in original problem\n7(2) + 2 \n√\n= 4 Multiply\n14 + 2 \n√\n= 4 Add\n16\n√\n= 4 Square root\n4 = 4 True! It works! \nx = 2 Our Solution\nExample 443. \nx − 1 3√ = − 4 Odd index, we don′t need to check answer\n( x − 1 3√ ) 3 = ( − 4) 3 Cube both sides, simplify exponents\nx − 1 = − 64 Solve\n326\n+ 1 + 1 Add 1 to both sides\nx = − 63 Our Solution\nExample 444. \n3x + 64√ = − 3 Even index! We will have to check answers\n( 3 x + 64√ ) = ( − 3) 4 Rise both sides to fourth power\n3x + 6 = 81 Solve\n− 6 − 6 Subtract 6 from both sides\n3x = 75 Divide both sides by 3\n3 3\nx = 25 Need to check answer in original problem\n3( 25) + 64√\n= − 3 Multiply\n75 + 64√ = − 3 Add\n81 4√\n= − 3 Take root\n3 = − 3 False, extraneous solution\nNo Solution Our Solution\nIf the radical is not alone on one side of the equation we will h ave to solve for the\nradical before we raise it to an exponent\nExample 445. \nx + 4 x + 1 √\n= 5 Even index! We will have to check solutions\n− x − x Isolate radical by subtractingx from both sides\n4x + 1 √ = 5 − x Square both sides\n( 4 x + 1 √ ) 2 = (5 − x) 2 Evaluate exponents, recal ( a − b) 2 = a2 − 2 ab + b2 \n4x + 1 = 25 − 10x + x2 Re − order terms\n4x + 1 = x2 − 10x + 25 Make equation equal zero\n− 4x − 1 − 4x − 1 Subtract 4x and 1 from both sides\n0 = x2 − 14x + 24 Factor\n0 = ( x − 12)( x − 2) Set each factor equal to zero\nx − 12 = 0 or x − 2 = 0 Solve each equation\n+ 12 + 12 + 2 + 2 \nx = 12 or x = 2 Need to check answers in original problem\n( 12) + 4( 12) + 1 \n√\n= 5 Check x = 5 ﬁrst\n327"
            }
          ],

          "lesson_goal": "Learn Solving with Radicals well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_59",
          "chapter_id": "CH_10",
          "number": "9.2",
          "title": "Solving with Exponents",
          "order": 58,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 332,
              "page_end": 332,
              "text": "9.2\nQuadratics - Solving with Exponents\nObjective: Solve equations with exponents using the odd roo t property\nand the even root property. \nAnother type of equation we can solve is one with exponents. A s you might\nexpect we can clear exponents by using roots. This is done wit h very few unex-\npected results when the exponent is odd. We solve these probl ems very straight\nforward using the odd root property\nOdd Root Property: if an = b, then a = bn√\nwhen n is odd\nExample 449. \nx5 = 32 Use odd root property\nx55√\n= 325√\nSimplify roots\nx = 2 Our Solution\nHowever, when the exponent is even we will have two results fr om taking an even\nroot of both sides. One will be positive and one will be negati ve. This is because\nboth 32 = 9 and ( − 3) 2 = 9. so when solving x2 = 9 we will have two solutions, one\npositive and one negative: x = 3 and − 3\nEven Root Property: if an = b, then a = ± bn√\nwhen n is even\nExample 450. \nx4 = 16 Use even root property ( ± ) \n332\nx44√\n= ± 164√\nSimplify roots\nx = ± 2 Our Solution\nWorld View Note: In 1545, French Mathematicain Gerolamo Cardano pub-\nlished his book The Great Art, or the Rules of Algebra which included the solu-\ntion of an equation with a fourth power, but it was considered absurd by many to\ntake a quantity to the fourth power because there are only thr ee dimensions! \nExample 451. \n(2 x + 4) 2 = 36 Use even root property ( ± ) \n(2 x + 4) 2 √\n= ± 36\n√\nSimplify roots\n2 x + 4 = ± 6 To avoid sign errors we need two equations\n2 x + 4 = 6 or 2 x + 4 = − 6 One equation for + , one equation for − \n− 4 − 4 − 4 − 4 Subtract 4 from both sides\n2 x = 2 or 2 x = − 10 Divide both sides by 2 \n2 2 2 2 \nx = 1 or x = − 5 Our Solutions\nIn the previous example we needed two equations to simplify b ecause when we\ntook the root, our solutions were two rational numbers, 6 and − 6. If the roots did\nnot simplify to rational numbers we can keep the ± in the equation. \nExample 452. \n(6x − 9) 2 = 45 Use even root property ( ± ) \n(6x − 9) 2 √\n= ± 45\n√\nSimplify roots\n6x − 9 = ± 3 5 \n√\nUse one equation because root did not simplify to rational\n+ 9 + 9 Add 9 to both sides\n6x = 9 ± 3 5 \n√\nDivide both sides by6\n6 6\nx = 9 ± 3 5 \n√\n6 Simplify, divide each term by3\nx = 3 ± 5 \n√\n2 Our Solution\n333"
            }
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_60",
          "chapter_id": "CH_10",
          "number": "9.3",
          "title": "Complete the Square",
          "order": 59,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 337,
              "page_end": 337,
              "text": "9.3\nQuadratics - Complete the Square\nObjective: Solve quadratic equations by completing the squ are. \nWhen solving quadratic equations in the past we have used fac toring to solve for\nour variable. This is exactly what is done in the next example . \nExample 457. \nx2 + 5 x + 6 = 0 Factor\n( x + 3)( x + 2) = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\nx + 3 = 0 or x + 2 = 0 Solve each equation\n− 3 − 3 − 2 − 2 \nx = − 3 or x = − 2 Our Solutions\nHowever, the problem with factoring is all equations cannot be factored. Consider\nthe following equation: x2 − 2 x − 7 = 0 . The equation cannot be factored, however\nthere are two solutions to this equation, 1 + 2 2 \n√\nand 1 − 2 2 \n√\n. To ﬁnd these two\nsolutions we will use a method known as completing the square . When completing\nthe square we will change the quadratic into a perfect square which can easily be\nsolved with the square root property. The next example revie ws the square root\nproperty. \nExample 458. \n( x + 5) 2 = 18 Square root of both sides\n( x + 5) 2 √\n= ± 18\n√\nSimplify each radical\nx + 5 = ± 3 2 \n√\nSubtract 5 from both sides\n− 5 − 5 \nx = − 5 ± 3 2 \n√\nOur Solution\n337\nTo complete the square, or make our problem into the form of th e previous\nexample, we will be searching for the third term in a trinomia l. If a quadratic is\nof the form x2 + b x + c, and a perfect square, the third term, c, can be easily\nfound by the formula\n(\n1 \n2 \n· b\n) 2 \n. This is shown in the following examples, where we\nﬁnd the number that completes the square and then factor the p erfect square. \nExample 459. \nx2 + 8x + c c = \n( 1 \n2 \n· b\n) 2 \nand our b = 8\n( 1 \n2 \n· 8\n) 2 \n= 42 = 16 The third term to complete the square is 16\nx2 + 8x + 16 Our equation as a perfect square, factor\n( x + 4) 2 Our Solution\nExample 460. \nx2 − 7x + c c = \n( 1 \n2 \n· b\n) 2 \nand our b = 7\n( 1 \n2 \n· 7\n) 2 \n= \n( 7\n2 \n) 2 \n= 49\n4 The third term to complete the square is49\n4\nx2 − 11 x + 49\n4 Our equation as a perfect square, factor\n(\nx − 7\n2 \n) 2 \nOur Solution\nExample 461. \nx2 + 5 \n3\nx + c c = \n( 1 \n2 \n· b\n) 2 \nand our b = 8\n( 1 \n2 \n· 5 \n3\n) 2 \n= \n( 5 \n6\n) 2 \n= 25\n36\nThe third term to complete the square is25\n36\n338"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_60"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Complete the Square well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_61",
          "chapter_id": "CH_10",
          "number": "9.4",
          "title": "Quadratic Formula",
          "order": 60,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 343,
              "page_end": 343,
              "text": "9.4\nQuadratics - Quadratic Formula\nObjective: Solve quadratic equations by using the quadrati c formula. \nThe general from of a quadratic is a x2 + b x + c = 0 . We will now solve this for-\nmula for x by completing the square\nExample 465. \nax 2 + bc + c = 0 Separate constant from variables\n− c − c Subtract c from both sides\nax 2 + bx = − c Divide each term bya\na a a\nx2 + b\nax = − c\na Find the number that completes the square\n( 1 \n2 \n· b\na\n) 2 \n= \n( b\n2 a\n) 2 \n= b2 \n4a2 Add to both sides, \nb2 \n4a2 − c\na\n( 4a\n4a\n)\n= b2 \n4a2 − 4ac\n4a2 = b2 − 4ac\n4a2 Get common denominator on right\nx2 + b\na x + b2 \n4a2 = b2 \n4a2 − 4ac\n4a2 = b2 − 4ac\n4a2 Factor\n(\nx + b\n2 a\n) 2 \n= b2 − 4ac\n4a2 Solve using the even root property\n(\nx + b\n2 a\n) 2 \n√\n= ± b2 − 4ac\n4a2 \n√\nSimplify roots\nx + b\n2 a = ± b2 − 4ac\n√\n2 a Subtract b\n2 a from both sides\nx = − b ± b2 − 4ac\n√\n2 a Our Solution\nThis solution is a very important one to us. As we solved a gene ral equation by\ncompleting the square, we can use this formula to solve any qu adratic equation. \nOnce we identify what a, b, and c are in the quadratic, we can substitute those\n343\nvalues into x = − b ± b2 − 4ac\n√\n2 a and we will get our two solutions. This formula is\nknown as the quadratic fromula\nQuadratic Formula: if a x 2 + b x + c = 0 then x = − b ± b2 − 4a c\n√\n2 a\nWorld View Note: Indian mathematician Brahmagupta gave the ﬁrst explicit\nformula for solving quadratics in 628. However, at that time mathematics was not\ndone with variables and symbols, so the formula he gave was, “ To the absolute\nnumber multiplied by four times the square, add the square of the middle term; \nthe square root of the same, less the middle term, being divid ed by twice the\nsquare is the value.” This would translate to\n4ac + b2 \n√\n− b\n2 a as the solution to the equationax 2 + bx = c. \nWe can use the quadratic formula to solve any quadratic, this is shown in the fol-\nlowing examples. \nExample 466. \nx2 + 3x + 2 = 0 a = 1 , b = 3, c = 2 , use quadratic formula\nx = − 3 ± 32 − 4(1)(2) \n√\n2(1) Evaluate exponent and multiplication\nx = − 3 ± 9 − 8√\n2 Evaluate subtraction under root\nx = − 3 ± 1 \n√\n2 Evaluate root\nx = − 3 ± 1 \n2 Evaluate ± to get two answers\nx = − 2 \n2 or − 4\n2 Simplify fractions\nx = − 1 or − 2 Our Solution\nAs we are solving using the quadratic formula, it is importan t to remember the\nequation must ﬁst be equal to zero. \nExample 467. \n25x2 = 30x + 11 First set equal to zero\n− 30x − 11 − 30x − 11 \nSubtract 30x and 11 from both sides\n25x2 − 30x − 11 = 0 a = 25, b = − 30, c = − 11 , use quadratic formula\nx = 30 ± ( − 30) 2 − 4( 25)( − 11 ) \n√\n2( 25) Evaluate exponent and multiplication\n344"
            }
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_62",
          "chapter_id": "CH_10",
          "number": "9.5",
          "title": "Build Quadratics From Roots",
          "order": 61,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 348,
              "page_end": 348,
              "text": "9.5\nQuadratics - Build Quadratics From Roots\nObjective: Find a quadratic equation that has given roots us ing reverse\nfactoring and reverse completing the square. \nUp to this point we have found the solutions to quadratics by a method such as\nfactoring or completing the square. Here we will take our sol utions and work\nbackwards to ﬁnd what quadratic goes with the solutions. \nWe will start with rational solutions. If we have rational so lutions we can use fac-\ntoring in reverse, we will set each solution equal to x and then make the equation\nequal to zero by adding or subtracting. Once we have done this our expressions\nwill become the factors of the quadratic. \nExample 471. \nThe solutions are 4 and − 2 Set each solution equal tox\nx = 4 or x = − 2 Make each equation equal zero\n− 4 − 4\n+ 2 + 2 Subtract 4 from ﬁrst, add 2 to second\nx − 4 = 0 or x + 2 = 0 These expressions are the factors\n( x − 4)( x + 2) = 0 FOIL\nx2 + 2 x − 4x − 8 Combine like terms\nx2 − 2 x − 8 = 0 Our Solution\nIf one or both of the solutions are fractions we will clear the fractions by multi-\nplying by the denominators. \nExample 472. \nThe solution are 2 \n3\nand 3\n4\nSet each solution equal tox\nx = 2 \n3\nor x = 3\n4\nClear fractions by multiplying by denominators\n3x = 2 or 4x = 3 Make each equation equal zero\n− 2 − 2 − 3 − 3 Subtract 2 from the ﬁrst, subtract 3 from the second\n3x − 2 = 0 or 4x − 3 = 0 These expressions are the factors\n(3x − 2)(4x − 3) = 0 FOIL\n12x2 − 9x − 8x + 6 = 0 Combine like terms\n348\n12x2 − 17x + 6 = 0 Our Solution\nIf the solutions have radicals (or complex numbers) then we c annot use reverse\nfactoring. In these cases we will use reverse completing the square. When there\nare radicals the solutions will always come in pairs, one wit h a plus, one with a\nminus, that can be combined into “one” solution using ± . We will then set this\nsolution equal to x and square both sides. This will clear the radical from our\nproblem. \nExample 473. \nThe solutions are 3\n√\nand − 3\n√\nWrite as ′′one′′ expression equal to x\nx = ± 3\n√\nSquare both sides\nx2 = 3 Make equal to zero\n− 3 − 3 Subtract 3 from both sides\nx2 − 3 = 0 Our Solution\nWe may have to isolate the term with the square root (with plus or minus) by\nadding or subtracting. With these problems, remember to squ are a binomial we\nuse the formula ( a + b) 2 = a2 + 2 ab + b2 \nExample 474. \nThe solutions are 2 − 5 2 \n√\nand 2 + 5 2 \n√\nWrite as ′′one′′ expression equal to x\nx = 2 ± 5 2 \n√\nIsolate the square root term\n− 2 − 2 Subtract 2 from both sides\nx − 2 = ± 5 2 \n√\nSquare both sides\nx2 − 4x + 4 = 25 · 2 \nx2 − 4x + 4 = 50 Make equal to zero\n− 50 − 50 Subtract 50\nx2 − 4x − 46 = 0 Our Solution\nWorld View Note: Before the quadratic formula, before completing the square , \nbefore factoring, quadratics were solved geometrically by the Greeks as early as\n300 BC! In 1079 Omar Khayyam, a Persian mathematician solved cubic equations\ngeometrically! \nIf the solution is a fraction we will clear it just as before by multiplying by the\ndenominator. \n349"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_62"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Build Quadratics From Roots well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_63",
          "chapter_id": "CH_10",
          "number": "9.6",
          "title": "Quadratic in Form",
          "order": 62,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 352,
              "page_end": 352,
              "text": "9.6\nQuadratics - Quadratic in Form\nObjective: Solve equations that are quadratic in form by sub stitution\nto create a quadratic equation. \nWe have seen three diﬀerent ways to solve quadratics: factor ing, completing the\nsquare, and the quadratic formula. A quadratic is any equati on of the form 0 = \na x2 + b x + c, however, we can use the skills learned to solve quadratics t o solve\nproblems with higher (or sometimes lower) powers if the equa tion is in what is\ncalled quadratic form. \nQuadratic Form: 0 = a x m + bx n + c where m = 2 n\nAn equation is in quadratic form if one of the exponents on a va riable is double\nthe exponent on the same variable somewhere else in the equat ion. If this is the\ncase we can create a new variable, set it equal to the variable with smallest expo-\nnent. When we substitute this into the equation we will have a quadratic equa-\ntion we can solve. \nWorld View Note: Arab mathematicians around the year 1000 were the ﬁrst to\nuse this method! \nExample 478. \nx4 − 13x2 + 36 = 0 Quadratic form, one exponent, 4, double the other, 2 \ny = x2 New variable equal to the variable with smaller exponent\ny2 = x4 Square both sides\ny2 − 13y + 36 = 0 Substitute y for x2 and y2 for x4\n( y − 9)( y − 4) = 0 Solve. We can solve this equation by factoring\ny − 9 = 0 or y − 4 = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n+ 9 + 9 + 4 + 4\nSolve each equation\ny = 9 or y = 4 Solutions for y, need x. We will usey = x2 equation\n9 = x2 or 4 = x2 Substitute values for y\n± 9\n√\n= x2 \n√\nor ± 4\n√\n= x2 \n√\nSolve using the even root property, simplify roots\nx = ± 3, ± 2 Our Solutions\nWhen we have higher powers of our variable, we could end up wit h many more\nsolutions. The previous equation had four unique solutions . \n352\nExample 479. \na− 2 − a− 1 − 6 = 0 Quadratic form, one exponent, − 2 , is double the other, − 1 \nb = a− 1 Makea new variable equal to the variable with lowest exponent\nb2 = a− 2 Square both sides\nb2 − b − 6 = 0 Substitute b2 for a− 2 and b for a− 1 \n( b − 3)( b + 2) = 0 Solve. We will solve by factoring\nb − 3 = 0 or b + 2 = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n+ 3 + 3 − 2 − 2 Solve each equation\nb = 3 or b = − 2 Solutions for b, still need a, substitute into b = a− 1 \n3 = a− 1 or − 2 = a− 1 Raise both sides to − 1 power\n3− 1 = a or ( − 2) − 1 = a Simplify negative exponents\na = 1 \n3\n, − 1 \n2 \nOur Solution\nJust as with regular quadratics, these problems will not alw ays have rational solu-\ntions. We also can have irrational or complex solutions to ou r equations. \nExample 480. \n2 x4 + x2 = 6 Make equation equal to zero\n− 6 − 6 Subtract 6 from both sides\n2 x4 + x2 − 6 = 0 Quadratic form, one exponent, 4, double the other, 2 \ny = x2 New variable equal variable with smallest exponent\ny2 = x4 Square both sides\n2 y2 + y − 6 = 0 Solve. We will factor this equation\n(2 y − 3)( y + 2) = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n2 y − 3 = 0 or y + 2 = 0 Solve each equation\n+ 3 + 3 − 2 − 2 \n2 y = 3 or y = − 2 \n2 2 \ny = 3\n2 \nor y = − 2 We havey, still need x. Substitute into y = x2 \n3\n2 \n= x2 or − 2 = x2 Square root of each side\n± 3\n2 \n√\n= x2 \n√\nor ± − 2 √ = x2 \n√\nSimplify each root, rationalize denominator\nx = ± 6\n√\n2 , ± i 2 \n√\nOur Solution\n353"
            }

        {
          "id": "SEC_64",
          "chapter_id": "CH_10",
          "number": "9.7",
          "title": "Application: Rectangles",
          "order": 63,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 357,
              "page_end": 357,
              "text": "9.7\nQuadratics - Rectangles\nObjective: Solve applications of quadratic equations usin g rectangles. \nAn application of solving quadratic equations comes from th e formula for the area\nof a rectangle. The area of a rectangle can be calculated by mu ltiplying the width\nby the length. To solve problems with rectangles we will ﬁrst draw a picture to\nrepresent the problem and use the picture to set up our equati on. \nExample 484. \nThe length of a rectangle is 3 more than the width. If the area i s 40 square\ninches, what are the dimensions? \n40\nx We do not know the width, x. \nx + 3 Length is 4 more, or x + 4, and area is 40. \n357\nx( x + 3) = 40 Multiply length by width to get area\nx2 + 3x = 40 Distribute\n− 40 − 40 Make equation equal zero\nx2 + 3x − 40 = 0 Factor\n( x − 5)( x + 8) = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\nx − 5 = 0 or x + 8 = 0 Solve each equation\n+ 5 + 5 − 8 − 8\nx = 5 or x = − 8 Our x is a width, cannot be negative. \n(5) + 3 = 8 Length is x + 3, substitute 5 for x to ﬁnd length\n5 in by 8in Our Solution\nThe above rectangle problem is very simple as there is only on e rectangle\ninvolved. When we compare two rectangles, we may have to get a bit more cre-\native. \nExample 485. \nIf each side of a square is increased by 6, the area is multipli ed by 16. Find the\nside of the original square. \nx2 \nx Square has all sides the same length\nx Area is found by multiplying length by width\n16x2 x + 6 Each side is increased by6, \nx + 6 Area is 16 times original area\n( x + 6)( x + 6) = 16x2 Multiply length by width to get area\nx2 + 12x + 36 = 16x2 FOIL\n− 16x2 − 16x2 Make equation equal zero\n− 15x2 + 12x + 36 = 0 Divide each term by− 1 , changes the signs\n15x2 − 12x − 36 = 0 Solve using the quadratic formula\nx = 12 ± ( − 12) 2 − 4( 15)( − 36) \n√\n2( 15) Evaluate\nx = 16 ± 2304\n√\n30\nx = 16 ± 48\n30 Can ′t have a negative solution, we will only add\nx = 60\n30\n= 2 Our x is the original square\n358"
            }
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_65",
          "chapter_id": "CH_10",
          "number": "9.8",
          "title": "Application: Teamwork",
          "order": 64,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 364,
              "page_end": 364,
              "text": "9.8\nQuadratics - Teamwork\nObjective: Solve teamwork problems by creating a rational e quation to\nmodel the problem. \nIf it takes one person 4 hours to paint a room and another perso n 12 hours to\npaint the same room, working together they could paint the ro om even quicker, it\nturns out they would paint the room in 3 hours together. This c an be reasoned by\nthe following logic, if the ﬁrst person paints the room in 4 ho urs, she paints 1 \n4\nof\nthe room each hour. If the second person takes 12 hours to pain t the room, he\npaints 1 \n12 of the room each hour. So together, each hour they paint 1 \n4\n+ 1 \n12 of the\nroom. Using a common denominator of 12 gives: 3\n12 + 1 \n12 = 4\n12 = 1 \n3\n. This means\neach hour, working together they complete 1 \n3\nof the room. If 1 \n3\nis completed each\nhour, it follows that it will take 3 hours to complete the enti re room. \nThis pattern is used to solve teamwork problems. If the ﬁrst p erson does a job in\nA, a second person does a job in B, and together they can do a job in T (total). \nWe can use the team work equation. \nTeamwork Equation: 1 \nA + 1 \nB = 1 \nT \nOften these problems will involve fractions. Rather than th inking of the ﬁrst frac-\ntion as 1 \nA , it may be better to think of it as the reciprocal of A’s time. \nWorld View Note: When the Egyptians, who were the ﬁrst to work with frac-\ntions, wrote fractions, they were all unit fractions (numer ator of one). They only\nused these type of fractions for about 2000 years! Some belie ve that this cumber-\nsome style of using fractions was used for so long out of tradi tion, others believe\nthe Egyptians had a way of thinking about and working with fra ctions that has\nbeen completely lost in history. \nExample 490. \nAdam can clean a room in 3 hours. If his sister Maria helps, the y can clean it in\n2 2 \n5\nhours. How long will it take Maria to do the job alone? \n2 2 \n5 \n= 12\n5 Together time, 2 2 \n5 \n, needs to be converted to fraction\nAdan: 3, Maria: x, Total: 5 \n12 Clearly state times for each and total, using x for Maria\n364\n1 \n3\n+ 1 \nx = 5 \n12 Using reciprocals, add the individual times gives total\n1( 12x) \n3 + 1( 12x) \nx = 5( 12x) \n12 Multiply each term by LCD of 12x\n4x + 12 = 5 x Reduce each fraction\n− 4x − 4x Move variables to one side, subtracting 4x\n12 = x Our solution for x\nIt takes Maria 12 hours Our Solution\nSomtimes we only know how two people’s times are related to ea chother as in the\nnext example. \nExample 491. \nMike takes twice as long as Rachel to complete a project. Toge ther they can com-\nplete the project in 10 hours. How long will it take each of the m to complete the\nproject alone? \nMike: 2 x, Rachel: x, Total: 10 Clearly deﬁne variables . If Rachel isx, Mike is 2 x\n1 \n2 x\n+ 1 \nx = 1 \n10 Using reciprocals, add individal times equaling total\n1( 10x) \n2 x + 1( 10x) \nx = 1( 10x) \n10 Multiply each term by LCD, 10x\n5 + 10 = x Combine like terms\n15 = x We have ourx, we said x was Rachel′s time\n2( 15) = 30 Mike is double Rachel , this gives Mike′s time. \nMike: 30 hr, Rachel: 15hr Our Solution\nWith problems such as these we will often end up with a quadrat ic to solve. \nExample 492. \nBrittney can build a large shed in 10 days less than Cosmo can. If they built it\ntogether it would take them 12 days. How long would it take eac h of them\nworking alone? \nBritney: x − 10, Cosmo: x, Total: 12 If Cosmo is x, Britney is x − 10\n1 \nx − 10\n+ 1 \nx = 1 \n12 Using reciprocals, make equation\n365"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_65"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Teamwork well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_66",
          "chapter_id": "CH_10",
          "number": "9.9",
          "title": "Simultaneous Products",
          "order": 65,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 370,
              "page_end": 370,
              "text": "9.9\nQuadratics - Simultaneous Products\nObjective: Solve simultaneous product equations using sub stitution to\ncreate a rational equation. \nWhen solving a system of equations where the variables are mu ltiplied together\nwe can use the same idea of substitution that we used with line ar equations. \nWhen we do so we may end up with a quadratic equation to solve. W hen we used\nsubstitution we solved for a variable and substitute this ex pression into the other\nequation. If we have two products we will choose a variable to solve for ﬁrst and\ndivide both sides of the equations by that variable or the fac tor containing the\nvariable. This will create a situation where substitution c an easily be done. \nExample 495. \nxy = 48\n( x + 3)( y − 2) = 54\nTo solve forx, divide ﬁrst\nequation by x, second by x + 3\ny = 48\nx\nand y − 2 = 54\nx + 3 Substitute 48\nx for y in the second equation\n48\nx − 2 = 54\nx + 3 Multiply each term by LCD: x( x + 3) \n48x( x + 3) \nx − 2 x( x + 3) = 54x( x + 3) \nx + 3 Reduce each fraction\n48( x + 3) − 2 x( x + 3) = 54x Distribute\n48x + 144 − 2 x2 − 6x = 54x Combine like terms\n− 2 x2 + 42x + 144 = 54x Make equation equal zero\n− 54x − 54x Subtract 54x from both sides\n− 2 x2 − 12x + 144 = 0 Divide each term by GCF of− 2 \nx2 + 6x − 72 = 0 Factor\n( x − 6)( x + 12) = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\nx − 6 = 0 or x + 12 = 0 Solve each equation\n+ 6 + 6 − 12 − 12\nx = 6 or x = − 12 Substitute each solution into xy = 48\n6y = 48 or − 12y = 48 Solve each equation\n6 6 − 12 − 12\ny = 8 or y = − 4 Our solutions for y, \n(6, 8) or ( − 12, − 4) Our Solutions as ordered pairs\n370\nThese simultaneous product equations will also solve by the exact same pattern. \nWe pick a variable to solve for, divide each side by that varia ble, or factor con-\ntaining the variable. This will allow us to use substitution to create a rational\nexpression we can use to solve. Quite often these problems wi ll have two solu-\ntions. \nExample 496. \nxy = − 35\n( x + 6)( y − 2) = 5 \nTo solve forx, divide the ﬁrst\nequation by x, second by x + 6\ny = − 35\nx\nand y − 2 = 5 \nx + 6 Substitute − 35\nx for y in the second equation\n− 35\nx − 2 = 5 \nx + 6 Multiply each term by LCD: x( x + 6) \n− 35x( x + 6) \nx − 2 x( x + 6) = 5 x( x + 6) \nx + 6 Reduce fractions\n− 35( x + 6) − 2 x( x + 6) = 5 x Distribute\n− 35x − 210 − 2 x2 − 12x = 5 x Combine like terms\n− 2 x2 − 47x − 210 = 5 x Make equation equal zero\n− 5 x − 5 x\n− 2 x2 − 52x − 210 = 0 Divide each term by− 2 \nx2 + 26x + 105 = 0 Factor\n( x + 5)( x + 21 ) = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\nx + 5 = 0 or x + 21 = 0 Solve each equation\n− 5 − 5 − 21 − 21 \nx = − 5 or x = − 21 Substitute each solution into xy = − 35\n− 5 y = − 35 or − 21 y = − 35 Solve each equation\n− 5 − 5 − 21 − 21 \ny = 7 or y = 5 \n3\nOur solutions for y\n( − 5 , 7) or\n(\n− 21 , 5 \n3\n)\nOur Solutions as ordered pairs\nThe processes used here will be used as we solve applications of quadratics\nincluding distance problems and revenue problems. These wi ll be covered in\nanother section. \nWorld View Note: William Horner, a British mathematician from the late 18th\ncentury/early 19th century is credited with a method for sol ving simultaneous\nequations, however, Chinese mathematician Chu Shih-chieh in 1303 solved these\nequations with exponents as high as 14! \n371"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_66"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Simultaneous Products well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_67",
          "chapter_id": "CH_10",
          "number": "9.10",
          "title": "Application: Revenue and Distance",
          "order": 66,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 373,
              "page_end": 373,
              "text": "9.10\nQuadratics - Revenue and Distance\nObjective: Solve revenue and distance applications of quad ratic equa-\ntions. \nA common application of quadratics comes from revenue and di stance problems. \nBoth are set up almost identical to each other so they are both included together. \nOnce they are set up, we will solve them in exactly the same way we solved the\nsimultaneous product equations. \nRevenue problems are problems where a person buys a certain n umber of items\nfor a certain price per item. If we multiply the number of item s by the price per\nitem we will get the total paid. To help us organize our inform ation we will use\nthe following table for revenue problems\nNumber Price Total\nFirst\nSecond\nThe price column will be used for the individual prices, the t otal column is used\nfor the total paid, which is calculated by multiplying the nu mber by the price. \nOnce we have the table ﬁlled out we will have our equations whi ch we can solve. \nThis is shown in the following examples. \nExample 497. \nA man buys several ﬁsh for S 56. After three ﬁsh die, he decides to sell the rest at\na proﬁt of S 5 per ﬁsh. His total proﬁt was S 4. How many ﬁsh did he buy to\nbegin with? \nNumber Price Total\nBuy n p 56\nSell\nUsing our table, we don′t know the number\nhe bought, or at what price, so we use varibles\nn and p. Total price wasS 56. \nNumber Price Total\nBuy n p 56\nSell n − 3 p + 5 60\nWhen he sold, he sold 3 less ( n − 3) , for S 5 \nmore ( p + 5) . Total proﬁt wasS 4, combined\nwith S 56 spent is S 60\nnp = 56 Find equatinos by multiplying number by price\n( n − 3)( p + 5) = 60 These are a simultaneous product\np = 56\nn and p + 5 = 60\nn − 3 Solving for number, divide by n or ( n − 3) \n373\n56\nn + 5 = 60\nn − 3 Substitute 56\nn for p in second equation\n56n( n − 3) \nn + 5 n( n − 3) = 60n( n − 3) \nn − 3 Multiply each term by LCD: n( n − 3) \n56( n − 3) + 5 n( n − 3) = 60n Reduce fractions\n56n − 168 + 5 n2 − 15n = 60n Combine like terms\n5 n2 + 41 n − 168 = 60n Move all terms to one side\n− 60n − 60n\n5 n2 − 19n − 168 = 0 Solve with quadratic formula\nn = 19 ± ( − 19) 2 − 4(5)( − 168) \n√\n2(5) Simplify\nn = 19 ± 3721 \n√\n10 = 19 ± 61 \n10 We don′t want negative solutions, only do + \nn = 80\n10\n= 8 This is our n\n8 ﬁsh Our Solution\nExample 498. \nA group of students together bought a couch for their dorm tha t cost S 96. How-\never, 2 students failed to pay their share, so each student ha d to pay S 4 more. \nHow many students were in the original group? \nNumber Price Total\nDeal n p 96\nPaid\nS 96 was paid, but we don′t know the number\nor the price agreed upon by each student. \nNumber Price Total\nDeal n p 96\nPaid n − 2 p + 4 96\nThere were 2 less that actually paid( n − 2) \nand they had to payS 4 more ( p + 4) . The\ntotal here is stillS 96. \nnp = 96 Equations are product of number and price\n( n − 2)( p + 4) = 96 This is a simultaneous product\np = 96\nn and p + 4 = 96\nn − 2 Solving for number, divide by n and n − 2 \n96\nn + 4 = 96\nn − 2 Substitute 96\nn for p in the second equation\n374"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_67"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Revenue and Distance well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_68",
          "chapter_id": "CH_10",
          "number": "9.11",
          "title": "Graphs of Quadratics",
          "order": 67,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 380,
              "page_end": 380,
              "text": "9.11 \nQuadratics - Graphs of Quadratics\nObjective: Graph quadratic equations using the vertex, x-i ntercepts, \nand y-intercept. \nJust as we drew pictures of the solutions for lines or linear e quations, we can draw\na picture of solution to quadratics as well. One way we can do t hat is to make a\ntable of values. \nExample 501. \ny = x2 − 4x + 3 Make a table of values\nx\ny\n0 \n1 \n2 \n3\n4\nWe will test5 values to get an idea of shape\ny = (0) 2 + 4(0) + 3 = 0 − 0 + 3 = 3 Plug 0 in for x and evaluate\ny = (1) 2 − 4(1) + 3 = 1 − 4 + 3 = 0 Plug 1 in for x and evaluate\ny = (2) 2 − 4(2) + 3 = 4− 8 + 3 = − 1 Plug 2 in for x and evaluate\ny = (3) 2 − 4(3) + 3 = 9− 12 + 3 = 0 Plug 3 in for x and evaluate\ny = (4) 2 − 4(4) + 3 = 16 − 16 + 3 = 3 Plug 4 in for x and evaluate\nx y\n0 3\n1 0 \n2 − 1 \n3 0 \n4 3\nOur completed table. Plot points on graph\nPlot the points (0 , 3) , (1 , 0) , (2 , − 1) , \n(3, 0) , and (4, 3) . \nConnect the dots with a smooth\ncurve. \nOur Solution\nWhen we have x2 in our equations, the graph will no longer be a straight line.\nQuadratics have a graph that looks like a U shape that is calle d a parabola. \nWorld View Note: The ﬁrst major female mathematician was Hypatia of Egypt\nwho was born around 370 AD. She studied conic sections. The pa rabola is one\ntype of conic section. \n380\nThe above method to graph a parabola works for any equation, h owever, it can be\nvery tedious to ﬁnd all the correct points to get the correct b end and shape. For\nthis reason we identify several key points on a graph and in th e equation to help\nus graph parabolas more eﬃciently. These key points are desc ribed below. \nA\nB C\nD\nPoint A: y-intercept: Where the graph\ncrosses the vertical y-axis. \nPoints B and C: x-intercepts: Where\nthe graph crosses the horizontal x-axis\nPoint D: Vertex: The point where the\ngraph curves and changes directions. \nWe will use the following method to ﬁnd each of the points on ou r parabola. \nTo graph the equation y = ax 2 + b x + c, ﬁnd the following points\n1. y-intercept: Found by making x = 0 , this simpliﬁes down to y = c\n2. x-intercepts: Found by making y = 0 , this means solving 0 = ax 2 + bx + c\n3. Vertex: Let x = − b\n2 a\nto ﬁnd x. Then plug this value into the equation to ﬁnd\ny. \nAfter ﬁnding these points we can connect the dots with a smoot h curve to ﬁnd\nour graph! \nExample 502. \ny = x2 + 4x + 3 Find the key points\ny = 3 y = c is the y − intercept\n0 = x2 + 4x + 3 To ﬁndx − intercept we solve the equation\n0 = ( x + 3)( x + 1) Factor\nx + 3 = 0 and x + 1 = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n− 3 − 3\n− 1 − 1 Solve each equation\nx = − 3 and x = − 1 Our x − intercepts\nx = − 4\n2(1) = − 4\n2 = − 2 To ﬁnd the vertex, ﬁrst use x = − b\n2 a\ny = ( − 2) 2 + 4( − 2) + 3 Plug this answer into equation to ﬁndy − coordinate\ny = 4 − 8 + 3 Evaluate\ny = − 1 The y − coordinate\n( − 2 , − 1) Vertex asa point\n381"
            }
          ],
