    {
      "id": "CH_11",
      "number": "10",
      "title": "Functions",
      "order": 10,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_69",
          "chapter_id": "CH_11",
          "number": "10.1",
          "title": "Function Notation",
          "order": 68,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 386,
              "page_end": 386,
              "text": "10.1 \nFunctions - Function Notation\nObjective: Idenﬁty functions and use correct notation to ev aluate func-\ntions at numerical and variable values. \nThere are many diﬀerent types of equations that we can work wi th in algebra. An\nequation gives the relationship between variables and numb ers. Examples of sev-\neral relationships are below: \n( x − 3) 2 \n9\n− ( y + 2) 2 \n4 = 1 and y = x2 − 2 x + 7 and y + x√ − 7 = xy\nThere is a speical classiﬁcation of relationships known as f unctions. Functions\nhave at most one output for any input. Generally x is the variable that we plug\ninto an equation and evaluate to ﬁnd y. For this reason x is considered an input\nvariable and y is considered an output variable. This means the deﬁnition o f a\nfunction, in terms of equations in x and y could be said, for any x value there is at\nmost one y value that corresponds with it. \nA great way to visualize this deﬁnition is to look at the graph s of a few relation-\nships. Because x values are vertical lines we will draw a vertical line throug h the\ngraph. If the vertical line crosses the graph more than once, that means we have\ntoo many possible y values. If the graph crosses the graph only once, then we say\nthe relationship is a function. \n386\nExample 504. \nWhich of the following graphs are graphs of functions? \nDrawing a vertical line\nthrough this graph will\nonly cross the graph\nonce, it is a function. \nDrawing a vertical line\nthrough this graph will\ncross the graph twice, \nonce at top and once at\nbottom. This is not a\nfunction. \nDrawing a vertical line\nthrough this graph will\ncross the graph only\nonce, it is a function. \nWe can look at the above idea in an algebraic method by taking a relationship\nand solving it for y. If we have only one solution then it is a function. \nExample 505. \nIs 3x2 − y = 5 a function? Solve the relation for y\n− 3x2 − 3x2 \nSubtract 3x2 from both sides\n− y = − 3x2 + 5 Divide each term by− 1 \n− 1 − 1 − 1 \ny = 3x2 − 5 Only one solution for y. \nYes! It is a function\nExample 506. \nIs y2 − x = 5 a function? Solve the relation for y\n+ x + x Add x to both sides\ny2 = x + 5 Square root of both sdies\ny2 \n√\n= ± x + 5 \n√\nSimplify\ny = ± x + 5 √ Two solutions fory ( one + , one − ) \nNo! Not a function\nOnce we know we have a function, often we will change the notat ion used to\nemphasis the fact that it is a function. Instead of writing y = , we will use func-\ntion notation which can be written f ( x) = . We read this notation “ f of x”. So for\n387"
            }
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_70",
          "chapter_id": "CH_11",
          "number": "10.2",
          "title": "Operations on Functions",
          "order": 69,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 393,
              "page_end": 393,
              "text": "10.2\nFunctions - Operations on Functions\nObjective: Combine functions using sum, diﬀerence, produc t, quotient\nand composition of functions. \nSeveral functions can work together in one larger function. There are 5 common\noperations that can be performed on functions. The four basi c operations on func-\ntions are adding, subtracting, multiplying, and dividing. The notation for these\nfunctions is as follows. \nAddition ( f + g)( x) = f ( x) + g( x) \nSubtraction ( f − g)( x) = f ( x) − g( x) \nMultiplication ( f · g)( x) = f ( x) g( x) \nDivision\n( f\ng\n)\n( x) = f ( x) \ng( x) \nWhen we do one of these four basic operations we can simply eva luate the two\nfunctions at the value and then do the operation with both sol utions\nExample 515. \nf ( x) = x2 − x − 2 \ng( x) = x + 1 \nﬁnd ( f + g)( − 3) \nEvaluate f and g at − 3\nf ( − 3) = ( − 3) 2 − ( − 3) − 2 Evaluate f at − 3\nf ( − 3) = 9 + 3− 2 \nf ( − 3) = 10\ng( − 3) = ( − 3) + 1 Evaluate g at − 3\ng( − 3) = − 2 \nf ( − 3) + g( − 3) Add the two functions together\n( 10) + ( − 2) Add\n8 Our Solution\nThe process is the same regardless of the operation being per formed. \nExample 516. \nh( x) = 2 x − 4\nk( x) = − 3x + 1 \nFind ( h · k)(5) \nEvaluate h and k at 5 \nh(5) = 2(5) − 4 Evaluate h at 5 \n393\nh(5) = 10 − 4\nh(5) = 6\nk(5) = − 3(5) + 1 Evaluate k at 5 \nk(5) = − 15 + 1 \nk(5) = − 14\nh(5) k(5) Multiply the two results together\n(6)( − 14) Multiply\n− 84 Our Solution\nOften as we add, subtract, multiply, or divide functions, we do so in a way that\nkeeps the variable. If there is no number to plug into the equa tions we will simply\nuse each equation, in parenthesis, and simplify the express ion. \nExample 517. \nf ( x) = 2 x − 4\ng( x) = x2 − x + 5 Write subtraction problem of functions\nFind ( f − g)( x) \nf ( x) − g( x) Replace f ( x) with (2 x − 3) and g( x) with ( x2 − x + 5) \n(2 x − 4) − ( x2 − x + 5) Distribute the negative\n2 x − 4 − x2 + x − 5 Combine like terms\n− x2 + 3x − 9 Our Solution\nThe parenthesis are very important when we are replacing f ( x) and g( x) with a\nvariable. In the previous example we needed the parenthesis to know to distribute\nthe negative. \nExample 518. \nf ( x) = x2 − 4x − 5 \ng( x) = x − 5 \nFind\n( f\ng\n)\n( x) \nWrite division problem of functions\nf ( x) \ng( x) Replace f ( x) with ( x2 − 4x − 5) and g( x) with ( x − 5) \n( x2 − 4x − 5) \n( x − 5) To simplify the fraction we must ﬁrst factor\n394"
            }
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_71",
          "chapter_id": "CH_11",
          "number": "10.3",
          "title": "Inverse Functions",
          "order": 70,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 401,
              "page_end": 401,
              "text": "10.3\nFunctions - Inverse Functions\nObjective: Identify and ﬁnd inverse functions. \nWhen a value goes into a function it is called the input. The re sult that we get\nwhen we evaluate the function is called the output. When work ing with functions\nsometimes we will know the output and be interested in what in put gave us the\noutput. To ﬁnd this we use an inverse function. As the name sug gests an inverse\nfunction undoes whatever the function did. If a function is n amed f ( x) , the\ninverse function will be named f − 1 ( x) (read “f inverse of x”). The negative one is\nnot an exponent, but mearly a symbol to let us know that this fu nction is the\ninverse of f . \nWorld View Note: The notation used for functions was ﬁrst introduced by the\ngreat Swiss mathematician, Leonhard Euler in the 18th centu ry. \nFor example, if f ( x) = x + 5 , we could deduce that the inverse function would be\nf − 1 ( x) = x − 5 . If we had an input of 3, we could calculate f (3) = (3) + 5 = 8. Our\noutput is 8. If we plug this output into the inverse function w e get f − 1 (8) = (8) − \n5 = 3, which is the original input. \nOften the functions are much more involved than those descri bed above. It may\nbe diﬃcult to determine just by looking at the functions if th ey are inverses. In\norder to test if two functions, f ( x) and g( x) are inverses we will calculate the\ncomposition of the two functions at x. If f changes the variable x in some way, \nthen g undoes whatever f did, then we will be back at x again for our ﬁnal solu-\ntion. In otherwords, if we simplify ( f ◦ g)( x) the solution will be x. If it is any-\nthing but x the functions are not inverses. \n401\nExample 524. \nAre f ( x) = 3 x + 43√ and g( x) = x3 − 4\n3 inverses? Caculate composition\nf ( g( x)) Replace g( x) with x3 − 4\n3\nf\n( x3 − 4\n3\n)\nSubstitute\n( x3 − 4\n3\n)\nfor variable in f\n3\n( x3 − 4\n3\n)\n+ 43\n√\nDivide out the 3′s \nx3 − 4 + 43√\nCombine like terms\nx33√\nTake cubed root\nx Simpliﬁed to x! \nYes, they are inverses! Our Solution\nExample 525. \nAre h( x) = 2 x + 5 and g( x) = x\n2 − 5 inverses? Calculate composition\nh( g( x)) Replace g( x) with\n( x\n2 − 5 \n)\nh\n( x\n2 − 5 \n)\nSubstitute\n( x\n2 − 5 \n)\nfor variable inh\n2 \n( x\n2 − 5 \n)\n+ 5 Distrubte 2 \nx − 10 + 5 Combine like terms\nx − 5 Did not simplify tox\nNo, they are not inverses Our Solution\nExample 526. \nAre f ( x) = 3x − 2 \n4x + 1 and g( x) = x + 2 \n3 − 4x inverses? Calculate composition\nf ( g( x)) Replace g( x) with\n( x + 2 \n3 − 4x\n)\nf\n( x + 2 \n3 − 4x\n)\nSubstitute\n( x + 2 \n3 − 4x\n)\nfor variable in f\n3\n(\nx + 2 \n3 − 4x\n)\n− 2 \n4\n(\nx + 2 \n3 − 4x\n)\n+ 1 \nDistribute 3 and 4 into numerators\n402"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_71"
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_72",
          "chapter_id": "CH_11",
          "number": "10.4",
          "title": "Exponential Functions",
          "order": 71,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 406,
              "page_end": 406,
              "text": "10.4\nFunctions - Exponential Functions\nObjective: Solve exponential equations by ﬁnding a common b ase. \nAs our study of algebra gets more advanced we begin to study mo re involved\nfunctions. One pair of inverse functions we will look at are e xponential functions\nand logarithmic functions. Here we will look at exponential functions and then we\nwill consider logarithmic functions in another lesson. Exp onential functions are\nfunctions where the variable is in the exponent such as f ( x) = ax. (It is important\nnot to confuse exponential functions with polynomial funct ions where the variable\nis in the base such as f ( x) = x2 ). \nWorld View Note One common application of exponential functions is popula-\ntion growth. According to the 2009 CIA World Factbook, the co untry with the\nhighest population growth rate is a tie between the United Ar ab Emirates (north\nof Saudi Arabia) and Burundi (central Africa) at 3.69%. Ther e are 32 countries\nwith negative growth rates, the lowest being the Northern Ma riana Islands (north\nof Australia) at − 7.08% . \nSolving exponetial equations cannot be done using the skill set we have seen in\nthe past. For example, if 3x = 9, we cannot take the x − root of 9 because we do\nnot know what the index is and this doesn’t get us any closer to ﬁnding x. How-\never, we may notice that 9 is 32 . We can then conclude that if 3x = 32 then x = 2 . \nThis is the process we will use to solve exponential function s. If we can re-write a\nproblem so the bases match, then the exponents must also matc h. \nExample 529. \n5 2 x+1 = 125 Rewrite 125 as 5 3\n5 2 x+1 = 5 3 Same base, set exponents equal\n2 x + 1 = 3 Solve\n− 1 − 1 Subtract 1 from both sides\n2 x = 2 Divide both sides by2 \n2 2 \nx = 1 Our Solution\nSometimes we may have to do work on both sides of the equation t o get a\ncommon base. As we do so, we will use various exponent propert ies to help. First\nwe will use the exponent property that states ( ax) y = axy. \n406\nExample 530. \n83x = 32 Rewrite 8 as 2 3 and 32 as 2 5\n(2 3) 3x = 2 5 Multiply exponents 3 and 3x\n2 9x = 2 5 Same base, set exponents equal\n9x = 5 Solve\n9 9 Divide both sides by9\nx = 5 \n9\nOur Solution\nAs we multiply exponents we may need to distribute if there ar e several terms\ninvolved. \nExample 531. \n273x+5 = 81 4x+1 Rewrite 27 as 33 and 81 as 34 (92 would not be same base) \n(33) 3x+5 = (34) 4x+1 Multiply exponents 3(3x + 5) and 4(4x + 1) \n39x+ 15 = 316x+4 Same base, set exponents equal\n9x + 15 = 16x + 4 Move variables to one side\n− 9x − 9x\nSubtract 9x from both sides\n15 = 7x + 4 Subtract 4 from both sides\n− 4 − 4\n11 = 7x Divide both sides by7\n7 7\n11 \n7 = x Our Solution\nAnother useful exponent property is that negative exponent s will give us a recip-\nrocal, 1 \nan = a− n\nExample 532. \n( 1 \n9\n) 2 x\n= 37x− 1 Rewrite 1 \n9\nas 3− 2 ( negative exponet to ﬂip) \n(3− 2 ) 2 x = 37x− 1 Multiply exponents − 2 and 2 x\n3− 4x = 37x− 1 Same base, set exponets equal\n− 4x = 7x − 1 Subtract 7x from both sides\n− 7x − 7x\n− 11 x = − 1 Divide by − 11 \n− 11 − 11 \nx = 1 \n11 Our Solution\nIf we have several factors with the same base on one side of the equation we can\nadd the exponents using the property that states axay = ax+ y. \n407"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_72"
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_73",
          "chapter_id": "CH_11",
          "number": "10.5",
          "title": "Logarithmic Functions",
          "order": 72,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 410,
              "page_end": 410,
              "text": "10.5\nFunctions - Logarithmic Functions\nObjective: Convert between logarithms and exponents and us e that\nrelationship to solve basic logarithmic equations. \nThe inverse of an exponential function is a new function know n as a logarithm. \nLograithms are studied in detail in advanced algebra, here w e will take an intro-\nductory look at how logarithms works. When working with radi cals we found that\nthere were two ways to write radicals. The expression anm√\ncould be written as\na\nn \nm . Each form has its advantages, thus we need to be comfortable using both the\nradical form and the rational exponent form. Similarly an ex ponent can be\nwritten in two forms, each with its own advantages. The ﬁrst f orm we are very\nfamiliar with, bx = a, where b is the base, a can be thought of as our answer, and\nx is the exponent. The second way to write this is with a logarit hm, log ba = x. \nThe word “log” tells us that we are in this new form. The variab les all still mean\nthe same thing. b is still the base, a can still be thought of as our answer. \nUsing this idea the problem 5 2 = 25 could also be written as log 525 = 2 . Both\nmean the same thing, both are still the same exponent problem , but just as roots\ncan be written in radical form or rational exponent form, bot h our forms have\ntheir own advantages. The most important thing to be comfort able doing with\nlogarithms and exponents is to be able to switch back and fort h between the two\nforms. This is what is shown in the next few examples. \nExample 535. \nWrite each exponential equation in logarithmic form\nm3 = 5 Identify base, m, answer, 5 , and exponent 3\nlogm5 = 3 Our Solution\n72 = b Identify base, 7, answer, b, and exponent, 2 \nlog7b = 2 Our Solution\n( 2 \n3\n) 4\n= 16\n81 \nIdentify base, 2 \n3\n, answer, 16\n81 \n, and exponent 4\nlog 2 \n3 \n16\n81 \n= 4 Our Solution\nExample 536. \nWrite each logarithmic equation in exponential form\nlog416 = 2 Identify base, 4, answer, 16, and exponent, 2 \n42 = 16 Our Solution\n410\nlog3x = 7 Identify base, 3, answer, x, and exponent, 7\n37 = x Our Solution\nlog93 = 1 \n2 \nIdentify base, 9, answer, 3, and exponent, 1 \n2 \n9\n1 \n2 \n= 3 Our Solution\nOnce we are comfortable switching between logarithmic and e xponential form we\nare able to evaluate and solve logarithmic expressions and e quations. We will ﬁrst\nevaluate logarithmic expressions. An easy way to evaluate a logarithm is to set\nthe logarithm equal to x and change it into an exponential equation. \nExample 537. \nEvaluate log2 64 Set logarithm equal to x\nlog2 64 = x Change to exponent form\n2 x = 64 Write as common base , 64 = 2 6\n2 x = 2 6 Same base, set exponents equal\nx = 6 Our Solution\nExample 538. \nEvaluate log125 5 Set logarithm equal tox\nlog125 5 = x Change to exponent form\n125x = 5 Write as common base, 125 = 5 3\n(5 3) x = 5 Multiply exponents\n5 3x = 5 Same base, set exponents equal (5 = 5 1 ) \n3x = 1 Solve\n3 3 Divide both sides by3\nx = 1 \n3\nOur Solution\nExample 539. \nEvaluate log3\n1 \n27 Set logarithm equal tox\nlog3\n1 \n27 = x Change to exponent form\n3x = 1 \n27 Write as common base, 1 \n27 = 3− 3\n3x = 3− 3 Same base, set exponents equal\nx = − 3 Our Solution\nWorld View Note: Dutch mathematician Adriaan Vlacq published a text in\n1628 which listed logarithms calculated out from 1 to 100,00 0! \n411"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_73"

        {
          "id": "SEC_74",
          "chapter_id": "CH_11",
          "number": "10.6",
          "title": "Application: Compound Interest",
          "order": 73,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 414,
              "page_end": 414,
              "text": "10.6\nFunctions - Compound Interest\nObjective: Calculate ﬁnal account balances using the formu las for com-\npound and continuous interest. \nAn application of exponential functions is compound intere st. When money is\ninvested in an account (or given out on loan) a certain amount is added to the\nbalance. This money added to the balance is called interest. Once that interest is\nadded to the balance, it will earn more interest during the ne xt compounding\nperiod. This idea of earning interest on interest is called c ompound interest. For\nexample, if you invest S 100 at 10% interest compounded annually, after one year\nyou will earn S 10 in interest, giving you a new balance of S 110. The next year\nyou will earn another 10% or S 11, giving you a new balance of S 121. The third\nyear you will earn another 10% or S 12.10, giving you a new balance of S 133.10. \nThis pattern will continue each year until you close the acco unt. \nThere are several ways interest can be paid. The ﬁrst way, as d escribed above, is\ncompounded annually. In this model the interest is paid once per year. But\ninterest can be compounded more often. Some common compound s include com-\npounded semi-annually (twice per year), quarterly (four ti mes per year, such as\nquarterly taxes), monthly (12 times per year, such as a savin gs account), weekly\n(52 times per year), or even daily (365 times per year, such as some student\nloans). When interest is compounded in any of these ways we ca n calculate the\nbalance after any amount of time using the following formula : \nCompound Interest Formula: A = P\n(\n1 + r\nn\n) nt\nA = Final Amount\nP = Principle ( starting balance) \nr = Interest rate ( as a decimal) \nn = number of compounds per year\nt = time ( in years) \nExample 544. \nIf you take a car loan for S 25000 with an interest rate of 6.5% compounded quar-\nterly, no payments required for the ﬁrst ﬁve years, what will your balance be at\nthe end of those ﬁve years? \nP = 25000, r = 0.065, n = 4, t = 5 Identify each variable\nA = 25000\n(\n1 + 0.065\n4\n) 4· 5\nPlug each value into formula, evaluate parenthesis\nA = 25000( 1.01625) 4· 5 Multiply exponents\n414\nA = 25000( 1.01625) 20 Evaluate exponent\nA = 25000( 1.38041977 / ) Multiply\nA = 34510.49\nS 34, 510.49 Our Solution\nWe can also ﬁnd a missing part of the equation by using our tech niques for\nsolving equations. \nExample 545. \nWhat principle will amount to S 3000 if invested at 6.5% compounded weekly for\n4 years? \nA = 3000, r = 0.065, n = 52, t = 4 Identify each variable\n3000 = P\n(\n1 + 0.065\n52\n) 52 · 4\nEvaluate parentheses\n3000 = P ( 1.00125) 52 · 4 Multiply exponent\n3000 = P ( 1.00125) 208 Evaluate exponent\n3000 = P ( 1.296719528 / ) Divide each side by 1.296719528/ \n1.296719528 / 1.296719528 / \n2313.53 = P Solution for P\nS 2313.53 Our Solution\nIt is interesting to compare equal investments that are made at several diﬀerent\ntypes of compounds. The next few examples do just that. \nExample 546. \nIf S 4000 is invested in an account paying 3% interest compounded monthly, what\nis the balance after 7 years? \nP = 4000, r = 0.03, n = 12, t = 7 Identify each variable\nA = 4000\n(\n1 + 0.03\n12\n) 12 · 7\nPlug each value into formula, evaluate parentheses\nA = 4000( 1.0025) 12 · 7 Multiply exponents\nA = 4000( 1.0025) 84 Evaluate exponent\nA = 4000( 1.2333548) Multiply\nA = 4933.42\nS 4933.42 Our Solution\n415"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_74"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Compound Interest well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_75",
          "chapter_id": "CH_11",
          "number": "10.7",
          "title": "Trigonometric Functions",
          "order": 74,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 420,
              "page_end": 420,
              "text": "10.7\nFunctions - Trigonometric Functions\nObjective: Solve for a missing side of a right triangle using trigono-\nmetric ratios. \nThere are six special functions that describe the relations hip between the sides of\na right triangle and the angles of the triangle. We will discu ss three of the func-\ntions here. The three functions are called the sine, cosine, and tangent (the three\nothers are cosecant, secant, and cotangent, but we will not n eed to use them\nhere). \nTo the right is a picture of a right tri-\nangle. Based on which angle we are\ninterested in on a given problem we\nwill name the three sides in relation-\nship to that angle. In the picture, \nangle A is the angle we will use to\nname the other sides. The longest side, \nthe side opposite the right angle is\nalways called the hypotenouse. The\nside across from the angle A is called\nthe opposite side. \nA\nHypotenuse\nAdjacent\nOpposite\nThe third side, the side between our angle and the right angle is called the adja-\ncent side. It is important to remember that the opposite and a djacent sides are\nnamed in relationship to the angle A or the angle we are using i n a problem. If\nthe angle had been the top angle, the opposite and adjacent si des would have\nbeen switched. \nThe three trigonometric funtions are functions taken of ang les. When an angle\ngoes into the function, the output is a ratio of two of the tria ngle sides. The\nratios are as describe below: \nsinθ = opposite\nhypotenuse\ncosθ = adjacent\nhypotenuse tanθ = opposite\nadjacent\nThe “weird” variable θ is a greek letter, pronounced “theta” and is close in idea to\nour letter “t”. Often working with triangles, the angles are repesented with Greek\nletters, in honor of the Ancient Greeks who developed much of Geometry. Some\nstudents remember the three ratios by remembering the word “ SOH CAH TOA”\nwhere each letter is the ﬁrst word of: “Sine: Opposite over Hy potenuse; Cosine: \nAdjacent over Hypotenuse; and Tangent: Opposite over Adjac ent.” Knowing how\nto use each of these relationships is fundamental to solving problems using\ntrigonometry. \nWorld View Note: The word “sine” comes from a mistranslation of the Arab\nword jayb\nExample 550. \n420\nUsing the diagram at right, ﬁnd each\nof the following: sin θ, cosθ, tanθ, sinα, \ncosα, and tanα . \nFirst we will ﬁnd the three ratios of θ . \nThe hypotenuse is 10, from θ , the\nopposite side is 6 and the adjacent\nside is 8. So we ﬁll in the following: \nsinθ = opposite\nhypotenuse\n= 6\n10 = 3\n5\ncosθ = adjacent\nhypotenuse= 8\n10 = 4\n5\ntanθ = opposite\nadjacent\n= 6\n8\n= 3\n4\nNow we will ﬁnd the three ratios of α . \nThe hypotenuse is 10, from α , the\nopposite side is 8 and the adjacent\nside is 6. So we ﬁll in the following: \n6\n8\n10\nα θ\n10\n8\nα θOpposite of θ\nAdjacent of α\n6\nHypotenuse\nAdjacent of θ\nOpposite of α\nsinα = opposite\nhypotenuse= 8\n10 = 4\n5\ncosα = adjacent\nhypotenuse= 6\n10 = 3\n5\ntanα = opposite\nadjacent= 8\n6\n= 4\n3\nWe can either use a trigonometry table or a calculator to ﬁnd d ecimal values for\nsine, cosine, or tangent of any angle. We only put angle value s into the trigono-\nmetric functions, never values for sides. Using either a tab le or a calculator, we\ncan solve the next example. \nExample 551. \nsin 42◦ Use calculator or table\n0.669 Our Solution\ntan 12◦ Use calculator or table\n0.213 Our Solution\ncos 18◦ Use calculator or table\n0.951 Our Solution\nBy combining the ratios together with the decimal approxima tions the calculator\nor table gives us, we can solve for missing sides of a triangle . The trick will be to\ndetermine which angle we are working with, naming the sides w e are working\nwith, and deciding which trig function can be used with the si des we have. \n421"

              "text": "10.8\nFunctions - Inverse Trigonometric Functions\nObjective: Solve for missing angles of a right triangle usin g inverse\ntrigonometry. \nWe used a special function, one of the trig functions, to take an angle of a triangle\nand ﬁnd the side length. Here we will do the opposite, take the side lengths and\nﬁnd the angle. Because this is the opposite operation, we wil l use the inverse\nfunction of each of the trig ratios we saw before. The notatio n we will use for the\ninverse trig functions will be similar to the inverse notati on we used with func-\ntions. \nsin− 1 \n( opposite\nhypotenuse\n)\n= θ cos− 1 \n( adjacent\nhypotenuse\n)\n= θ tan− 1 \n( opposite\nadjacent\n)\n= θ\nJust as with inverse functions, the − 1 is not an exponent, it is a notation to tell\nus that these are inverse functions. While the regular trig f unctions take angles as\ninputs, these inverse functions will always take a ratio of s ides as inputs. We can\ncalculate inverse trig values using a table or a calculator ( usually pressing shift or\n2nd ﬁrst). \nExample 554. \nsinA = 0.5 We don ′t know the angle so we use an inverse trig function\nsin− 1 ( 0.5) = A Evaluate using table or calculator\n30◦ = A Our Solution\ncosB = 0.667 We don ′t know the angle so we use an inverse trig function\ncos− 1 ( 0.667) = B Evaluate using table or calculator\n48◦ = B Our Solution\ntanC = 1.54 We don ′t know the angle so we use an inverse trig function\ntan− 1 ( 1.54) = C Evaluate using table or calculator\n57◦ = C Our Solution\nIf we have two sides of a triangle, we can easily calculate the ir ratio as a decimal\nand then use one of the inverse trig functions to ﬁnd a missing angle. \nExample 555. \nFind the indicated angle. \n428\n12\n17θ\nFrom angle θ the given sides are the\nopposite (12) and the hypotenuse (17). \nThe trig function that uses opposite\nand hypotenuse is the sine\nBecause we are looking for an angle\nwe use the inverse sine\nsin− 1 \n( 12\n17\n)\nSine is opposite over hyptenuse, use inverse to ﬁnd angle\nsin− 1 ( 0.706) Evaluate fraction, take sine inverse using table or calculator\n45◦ Our Solution\nExample 556. \nFind the indicated angle\n35\nα\nFrom the angle α , the given sides are\nthe opposite (5) and the adjacent (3) \nThe trig function that uses opposite\nand adjacent is the tangent\nAs we are looking for an angle we will\nuse the inverse tangent. \ntan− 1 \n( 5 \n3\n)\nTangent is opposite over adjacent. Use inverse to ﬁnd angle\ntan− 1 ( 1.667) Evaluate fraction, take tangent inverse on table or calculator\n59◦ Our Solution\nUsing a combination of trig functions and inverse trig funct ions, if we are given\ntwo parts of a right triangle (two sides or a side and an angle) , we can ﬁnd all the\nother sides and angles of the triangle. This is called solvin g a triangle. \nWhen we are solving a triangle, we can use trig ratios to solve for all the missing\nparts of it, but there are some properties from geometry that may be helpful\nalong the way. \nThe angles of a triangle always add up to 180 ◦ , because we have a right triangle, \n90◦ are used up in the right angle, that means there are another 90 ◦ left in the\ntwo acute angles. In other words, the smaller two angles will always add to 90, if\nwe know one angle, we can quickly ﬁnd the other by subtracting from 90. \nAnother trick is on the sides of the angles.
